About the Author

Lawrence Arthur Ley has a Computer Science degree from the University of Toronto and over 20 years of IT industry experience. He is passionate about learning new technologies and how they can make a positive impact. His Cardano journey started in 2021 with the Plutus Pioneers program, and he has worked on several open-source Cardano impact projects. Lawrence is a professor in the Blockchain Development Program at George Brown College in Toronto and runs the Cardano Blockchain Toronto monthly meetup with over 500 members.

His professional experience includes IT consulting in the telecommunications, banking, and retail industries. He has a proven track record of building and managing complex, integrated, highly scalable consumer and retail applications. His first experiences with cryptocurrencies involved mining Bitcoin and participating in local meetups where he met Ethereum co-founders Vitalik Buterin and Anthony Di Iorio.

In July 2023, Lawrence was invited to attend the Cardano CIP-1694 Governance Workshop in Edinburgh, Scotland, where he met Cardano co-founder Charles Hoskinson. He was deeply honored to participate in the governance workshops with many highly esteemed individuals from the Cardano ecosystem. He continues to be active in the Cardano community, exploring new technologies and helping others along the way.

He has extensive experience with both public and private blockchains, including Cardano, Ripple, Ethereum, Hyperledger, Corda, and Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAGs). His expertise spans fintech, supply chain, agri-tech, and healthcare.

Venu’s proficiency in tokenization and tokenomics principles enhances business operations and drives innovation.

His technical skills include programming in Solidity, Go-lang, Python, Kotlin, and Haskell, and using development tools like Hardhat and Brownie. Venu is also skilled in emerging technologies such as AI, machine learning, IoT integration, and Distributed Ledger Technology (DLT).

In his current role, Venu focuses on the architecture, design, and development of blockchain systems, emphasizing Explainable AI (XAI), auditability, and transparency solutions. He manages GenAI solutions, leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) and building Minimum Viable Products (MVPs) using the RAG approach.

As a Board of Trustees member for the Blockchain For Productivity Forum (BCPF), Venu promotes blockchain adoption and implements innovative solutions for social welfare projects in India and internationally. Previously, as the Managing Director of Blocmatrix IT Solutions Pvt Ltd, he delivered cutting-edge solutions across various industries.

His competencies include solution design and development, product management, cloud deployment, consulting, project management, quality assurance, team leadership, training, and technical documentation. Venu excels at translating complex business requirements into innovative technological solutions, consistently driving success and efficiency.

Preface

Deciding what blockchain to use when building a decentralized application is a big decision. There are many considerations, and switching to a different blockchain may take time and effort. Ultimate Cardano Smart Contracts aims to help demonstrate Cardano's features and provide a hands-on approach to writing Smart Contracts. Using this knowledge and insight, you will understand the benefits of building on Cardano and why there is such a large and passionate Cardano community.

This book comprises 14 chapters, each building upon the previous chapters. We will start with the fundamentals of blockchain and then move into writing smart contracts. The book will close with an overview of the Cardano ecosystem and ethos. The book includes technical and non-technical material, so it will appeal to experienced developers and non-technical entrepreneurs looking to improve their knowledge and understanding.

Chapter 1. Blockchain Evolution: This chapter will introduce blockchain and distributed ledger technology, focusing on prior implementations and challenges to overcome. We will look at the Bitcoin and Ethereum whitepapers and how they paved the way for the third-generation blockchain, Cardano. The blockchain trilemma will be examined, along with a review of tokenomics and Cardano’s native currency, Ada. Finally, we will finish the chapter by discussing the Cardano roadmap and blockchain use cases.

Chapter 2. Cryptography and Consensus Algorithms Overview: This chapter will briefly introduce cryptography and how it is used in blockchain technology. We will first look at securing a blockchain using the Proof of Work consensus protocol. Then, we will look at Proof of Stake and its benefits over Proof of Work. Finally, we will review Ouroboros, the Proof-of-Stake consensus protocol used by Cardano.

Chapter 3. Transactions: This chapter will examine Cardano addresses and the UTXO architecture. It will also introduce Smart Contracts and explain how they are executed and evaluated. We will see how Redeemers and Datums pass data into Plutus scripts and where Datums are also used for data storage and persistence. Finally, we will examine the transaction Witness Set and how it authorizes the spending of transaction inputs.

Chapter 4. Plutus: In this chapter, we will examine the Plutus core specification, Untyped Plutus Core (UPLC), used for executing Cardano Smart Contracts. We will also explore the different data structures used in Plutus and how to serialize them using the Concise Binary Object Representation (CBOR) format. Next, we will calculate the CPU and memory units using the Plutus cost model and explain why we need to include collateral in a transaction.

Chapter 5. Transaction Builder: This chapter will combine our knowledge of on-chain Plutus scripts and how to lock Ada into a smart contract using off-chain code. We will look at Cardano wallets, the gateway to interacting with the blockchain and smart contracts. Next, we will look at tooling to help build and submit transactions like Demeter. run. Then, we will make a simple, smart contract for vesting using two popular approaches: Helios and Lucid + Aiken.

Chapter 6. Validators: In this chapter, we will look in detail at what a validator is and why they are so important. We will show how to spend UTXOs locked at a validator script address and how to access data provided in a Redeemer or Datum. Next, we will dive deep into understanding what ScriptContext is and how it is used to access all inputs and outputs associated with a transaction. The chapter will review on-chain and off-chain code examples using two different approaches: Helios and Lucid + Aiken.

Chapter 7. Minting: This chapter will examine Cardano native assets and how they differ from Ethereum tokens created using the ERC-20 standard. We will discuss the concept of token bundles and how to optimize coin selection. We will then dive deep into minting Cardano native tokens, the differences between Non-Fungible Tokens (NFTs) and Fungible Tokens (FTs), and the metadata standards CIP-25 and CIP-68. We will combine this with a working minting policy use case and demo.

Chapter 8. Tooling: In this chapter, we will review the tools and service providers you can use for off-chain development. We will look at Cardano Blockchain API providers such as Blockfrost, Maestro, Koios, and Dandelion. Then, we will look at hosted environments with Demeter.run to set up services like Cardano Node, Ogmios, and Kupo. Finally, we will look at how to set up a local Devnet for rapid development and prototyping with the Yaci DevKit.

Chapter 9. Ticket Application Design: This chapter reviews Smart Contract design patterns that leverage and utilize the UTXO architecture. Anti-patterns are also examined, which should be avoided and lead to poor application performance and UTXO contention. We look at Cardano Improvement Proposals (CIPs) and how they apply to the application design. Next, we will create state machine diagrams to model the event ticket application, looking at the inputs and outputs required for a successful transaction. Finally, we will conclude the chapter by reviewing sequence diagrams showing each use case's interaction between system components.

Chapter 10. Ticket Application Implementation: In this chapter, we will walk through the implementation of a ticket application, including blockchain integration. We will look at the application stack, which includes CharkraUI, Next.js, Redis DB, and Blockfrost. Next, we will inspect the front-end off-chain code used to build and submit transactions. Then, we will review the corresponding Smart Contract on-chain code, business logic, and validation rules. Finally, we will run through an end-to-end demo of the application.

Chapter 11. Testing, Security, and Scaling: This chapter will show how to test Smart Contracts and include them in a popular testing framework such as Vitest. We will look at both the traditional testing and property-based testing approaches. Next, we will focus on security and review vulnerabilities identified in real-world Cardano application audits. We will look at code examples of how to make your Smart Contracts more robust and more resilient to any such attacks. Finally, we will close the chapter by discussing scaling and some tools and techniques to improve dApp performance.

Chapter 12. Troubleshooting: This chapter will walk through the debugging tools and techniques for Helios and Aiken. We will look at how to generate CBOR for a transaction and compare data types against the formal specification. We will look at techniques to reduce the computational overhead of a Plutus script that exceeds the execution budget. We will also examine compiler errors and how transaction builders provide robust error-handling support. Finally, we will look at Cardano Node and Browser Wallet errors and how to diagnose them accordingly.

Chapter 13. Cardano Ecosystem: This chapter will review the state of Cardano and the GitHub repositories that contain all of the Cardano source code and specifications. We will look at the Cardano Improvement Process (CIP) and how to make changes and improvements to the Cardano blockchain. Next, we will explore the era of Voltaire and CIP-1694 regarding Cardano governance. We will also review Project Catalyst, one of the largest decentralized innovation platforms that provides grant funding to Cardano projects. Finally, we will look at Cardano partner chain frameworks such as Midnight, a data-protection blockchain.

Chapter 14. Closing Remarks: This chapter will review the ethos of Cardano. We will examine the software industry standards and how these standards fall short for high assurance software. We will see how IOG (IOHK) used semi-formal methods to build Cardano and how cryptographic associations like IACR facilitate peer review. Through real-world examples, we will explore Cardano's potential to drive positive social change. We will also discuss regulatory and compliance risks. Finally, we will review the five stages of technology adoption and the Cardano journey.

CHAPTER 1

Blockchain Evolution

Introduction

This chapter will introduce blockchain and distributed ledger technology, focusing on prior implementations and challenges to overcome. We will look at the landmark Bitcoin whitepaper and how it ushered in a new era of digital currencies. Next, we will introduce the Ethereum whitepaper, how the concept of smart contracts was born, and the problems that Ethereum was addressing. This chapter will also review the key takeaways from the famous Charles whiteboard video that led many to dig deeper into the Cardano ecosystem. We will then explore the classic Blockchain Trilemma of decentralization, security, and scalability. There will also be a review of tokenomics and Cardano’s native currency, Ada. Finally, we will finish the chapter discussing the Cardano roadmap and blockchain use cases.

Structure

In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:

  • Distributed Ledger Technology
  • Bitcoin Whitepaper
  • Ethereum Whitepaper
  • Charles Hoskinson Whiteboard Video
    • Scalability
    • Interoperability
    • Sustainability
  • Blockchain Trilemma
  • Tokenomics
    • Token Allocation
    • Token Distribution
    • Token Allocation Comparison
    • Cardano Inflation Rate
  • Cardano Roadmap
  • Blockchain Use Cases

Distributed Ledger Technology

The concept of using a ledger to record credit and debit transactions has existed for thousands of years.1 With the advent of distributed computing, the concept of the ledger was extended, where multiple parties can see and update a ledger using computers connected to a network. The difference between a centralized and a decentralized distributed ledger, as shown in Figure 1.1, is that you no longer have a single central authority. The issue, however, is that you still need a way to ensure the correct ordering of immutable transactions. In 1991, Stuart Haber and W. Scott Stornetta wrote “How to Time-Stamp a Digital Document,” which described an approach using timestamps and cryptographic hashes to prove when a digital document had been created or if it had been modified.2 This approach was used to check for immutability.

1.1: Types of Ledgers

Byzantine Generals Problem

Another core aspect of distributed ledger technology is ensuring the data stored on each node is correct in a network that may include malicious or unavailable nodes. This problem is known as the Byzantine Generals Problem (BGP).3 BGP describes a scenario where generals surround a castle for an attack and must communicate and coordinate with each other via messengers. The attack will only succeed if all generals attack simultaneously, using the messengers to signal the attack. The risk, however, is that the attack signal may or may not be received correctly by all generals because the messengers may have been killed or bribed. A network resistant to these types of failures is called Crash Fault-Tolerant (CFT) and Byzantine Fault-Tolerant (BFT), respectively.

The paper “Building Secure File Systems Out of Byzantine Storage” by Mazières and Shasha introduces a Byzantine fault-tolerant (BFT) algorithm that allows a distributed network to reach a consensus on the state of data in an untrusted network.4 The authors created a consistency protocol called SUNDR (Secure Untrusted Data Repository) by leveraging redundancy to handle Byzantine failures and using digital signatures. If a discrepancy is discovered, the protocol will detect malicious behavior and partition file system data blocks into forks.

Network Consensus

For a network to agree on a common state, it needs to use a consensus algorithm that has two key properties:

  • Liveness: Must continue to make progress in deciding the network state.
  • Safety: Once honest nodes have confirmed the network state, they must remain committed to this decision.

When dealing with decentralized ledger networks, it is vital for the network’s security that the majority of nodes participating in the consensus algorithm are honest. If not, then this is known as a 51% attack, where a single entity controls the majority of nodes. When this happens, the attacker can control the consensus outcome and undo past (or deny future) transactions that may benefit the attacker.

The concept of using computation cycles for solving puzzles in decentralized consensus was first introduced in 1998 by Wei Dai’s b-money.5 The benefit of this approach is that it does not require a centralized authority to create money. “In 2005, Hal Finney introduced the concept of ‘reusable proofs of work’6, a system that uses ideas from b-money together with Adam Back’s computationally difficult Hashcash puzzles to create a concept for a cryptocurrency.”7 This approach, unfortunately, still relied on a trusted back-end,8 so it did not meet the goal of trustless, decentralized money.

Bitcoin Whitepaper

In 2008, the pseudonym Satoshi Nakamoto published “Bitcoin: A Peer-to-Peer Electronic Cash System” to address the double spending problem with a decentralized, peer-to-peer electronic cash system, as shown in Figure 1.2. In this landmark paper, Satoshi proposed “a peer-to-peer network using proof-of-work to record a public history of transactions that quickly becomes computationally impractical for an attacker to change if honest nodes control a majority of CPU power.”9 This is accomplished by using network timestamp transactions and “by hashing them into an ongoing chain of hash-based proof-of-work, forming a record that cannot be changed without redoing the proof-of-work. The longest chain not only serves as proof of the sequence of events witnessed but also as proof that it came from the largest pool of CPU power. As long as a majority of CPU power is controlled by nodes that are not cooperating to attack the network, they’ll generate the longest chain and outpace attackers.”10

Figure 1.2: Bitcoin Ledger

Bitcoin’s Proof-of-Work implementation was innovative because it solved two problems at the same time. First, it provided a simple and moderately effective consensus algorithm, allowing nodes in the network to collectively agree on a set of canonical updates to the state of the Bitcoin ledger.11 Second, it provided a mechanism for allowing free entry into the consensus process, solving the political problem of deciding who gets to influence the consensus while simultaneously preventing Sybill attacks.12

On January 3rd, 2009, the first Bitcoin block was minted, and a new era of permissionless and trustless digital currency was born. In the subsequent chapters on Cryptography and Consensus Algorithms Overview and Transactions, we will explore the Bitcoin whitepaper in more detail.

Ethereum Whitepaper

The Ethereum whitepaper by Vitalik Buterin, published in 2014, introduced a new way of using blockchain technology to create a more general-purpose distributed computing platform. In particular, he described the concept of a Smart Contract, which can be considered as cryptographic “boxes” that contain value and only unlock it if certain conditions are met.13 Unlike Bitcoin scripts, Ethereum Smart Contracts would be Turing-complete and have access to a global state and persistence storage, as shown in Figure 1.3. The Smart Contract running in the Ethereum Virtual Machine (EVM) would be value-aware and could read the Ether (ETH) amount at the contract account.

Figure 1.3: Ethereum Ledger

The global state in Ethereum contains two types of accounts: externally owned accounts, controlled by private keys, and contract accounts, controlled by their Smart Contract code.14 Smart Contracts are self-executing since they contain all the information they need within a single node. By using a global state, Ethereum has the advantage that it does not need to recreate all previous transactions to determine the current state of the network. It only has to apply the current transactions in the most recent block to the last global state stored on the node.

Transactions in Ethereum refers to a signed data package that stores a message sent from an externally owned account. Contracts can send messages to other contracts, where messages are virtual objects that are never serialized and exist only in the Ethereum execution environment.15 Ethereum is also considered non-deterministic because of the use of a global state. In contrast, Bitcoin and Cardano are deterministic since they only use a local state in transactions.

Ethereum smart contracts have the risk of infinite loops because they are Turing-complete scripts. To address this, Ethereum uses the concept of gas fees that are consumed when a smart contract (or chain of smart contracts) is executed. When a transaction is submitted, it must include the maximum amount of gas it will consume to safeguard against an infinite loop code execution.

Charles Hoskinson’s Whiteboard Video

The popular whiteboard video by Charles Hoskinson, with over 1M views,16 introduces the concept that Cardano is a third-generation blockchain, with Bitcoin being the first generation and Ethereum being the second generation. Cardano builds upon the first- and second-generation blockchains and addresses some of the key pain points from each, namely:

  • Scalability
  • Interoperability
  • Sustainability

Scalability

Charles indicates that neither Bitcoin nor Ethereum can scale to billions of users, and to improve scalability, one must focus on three key areas:

  • TPS: Transaction per second.
  • Network: Network bandwidth required to use and share the network.
  • Storage: The size of the blockchain that needs to be saved persistently.

Cardano has created, published, and released Ouroboros, a Provable secure Proof-of-Stake Protocol to increase TPS. This protocol requires significantly fewer resources than Proof-of-Work, so parallelism and sidechains can be introduced to increase TPS. Cryptographic compression algorithms, in addition to sidechains, will reduce the size of the blockchain that needs to be transmitted and stored, improving both network and storage scalability. The concept of sidechains allows the base layer of Cardano to act as a settlement layer, while sidechains act as computation layers. When scaling to billions of users, it is not feasible or practicable to broadcast, relay, and store every transaction to all nodes on a settlement layer.

Interoperability

Another area that Cardano aims to address is how a blockchain interacts with other blockchains and legacy systems. In particular, Charles sees a future where multiple blockchains exist, and Cardano can interoperate with each other so value can flow between them. Additionally, there needs to be awareness and the ability to transact with traditional financial systems. To do so, a blockchain needs to have the following capabilities:

  • Metadata: The story behind a transaction.
  • Attribution: Identity of senders and receivers.
  • Compliance: KYC and anti-money laundering.

Figure 1.4: Cardano Interoperability

Each of these topics is highly sensitive and contentious in the blockchain space. Great care must be exercised when dealing with these items since they may contain personal information stored on an immutable blockchain.

Sustainability

The last area to address is how a blockchain will grow, be funded, and be governed. Will it be a patronage model susceptible to being co-opted by large entities motivated by self-interest? Or will it be a one-time initial coin offering (ICO), which is finite and will run out of funds at some point? The approach taken with Cardano is to build into the protocol a self-sustaining treasury that is funded by initial token supply distribution and ongoing transaction fees. Equally, if not more important, is the question of who decides and how the funds are spent. This falls into the Governance phase in Cardano, which Charles indicates will be the hardest of all the phases in Cardano. The concept is that Ada holders can vote on treasury spending along with governance actions on features or improvements to the protocol over time.

The Blockchain Trilemma

The Blockchain Trilemma refers to a widely held belief that decentralized networks can only provide two of the three benefits at any given time concerning decentralization, security, and scalability.17

Figure 1.5: Blockchain Trilemma

  • Decentralization: Creating a blockchain that avoids a central point of control.
  • Scalability: The blockchain must be able to handle an increasing number of users and transactions.
  • Security: A blockchain must operate as expected, and defend itself from attacks, bugs, and other unforeseen issues.18

Cardano aims to address each of these dimensions using the following approaches:

  • Promote decentralization by ensuring that a stake pool securing the network can be run with ordinary hardware and a typical internet connection.
  • Address scalability with side chains, cryptographic roll-ups, and parallelism (pipelining), increasing the speed at which transactions are processed.
  • For high-assurance software and security, use a scientific approach, including a peer review process and formal verification of the Ouroboros Proof-of-Stake protocol.

Tokenomics

The last topic in the chapter is a brief review of tokenomics. A cryptocurrency token is critical in establishing value for a cryptocurrency ecosystem. This is important because running and supporting a network will take real-world resources and costs. Therefore, it is essential to understand the initial token distribution and monetary policy, such as the maximum supply and inflation rate, and how they influence price and market capitalization. Finally, as with the stock market, price and value are frequently misaligned. So, be cautious when evaluating an ecosystem based on the price of the cryptocurrency token. The price may have less to do with the utility or value of that ecosystem and more to do with price manipulation and market sentiment.

Token Allocation

Cardano’s native token is Ada, which currently has a circulating supply of 35,125,959,544 ADA and a maximum supply of 45,000,000,000 ADA.19 The Cardano ICO was completed between October 2015 and January 2017,20 with the distribution shown in Figure 1.6.

Figure 1.6: Initial Token Distribution

There are three founding entities of Cardano:21

  • IOHK (IOG) is a research and engineering company and venture studio that builds blockchain and Web3 products to empower everyone, everywhere.22
  • Emurgo is a for-profit arm that develops, supports, and incubates commercial opportunities and helps integrate businesses into the Cardano blockchain system.
  • Cardano Foundation is an independent, Swiss-based not-for-profit organization that promotes and advances the Cardano platform for financial and social systems.

Token Distribution

Since there is a maximum supply of Ada, we can see the token distribution at any given time. This is helpful for comparison with other cryptocurrencies because a high allocation of a token not in the circulating supply means there may be downward pressure on the price when the token is released to the public.

Figure 1.7: Ada Token Distribution23

The reserve is the amount of Ada used for staking rewards and funding the treasury, as shown in Figure 1.7. The treasury pays for ecosystem improvements and maintenance. Proof-of-stake networks use staking to allow users to stake their Ada to help secure the network. In return, they receive rewards proportional to the amount of Ada a user stakes vs. all Ada staked in the network. Staking rewards and treasury funding also include transaction fees generated from network activity.

Token Allocation Comparison

Figure 1.8 compares the initial token allocation of the top cryptocurrencies by market capitalization, as of September 27th, 2023. Interestingly, the amount of initial insider + foundation allocation has an upward trend as cryptocurrencies launch over time.

Figure 1.8: Top 10 Crypto Market Cap Initial Token Allocations24

Inflation Rate

The last metric we will look at is the Cardano inflation rate of 0.3%.25 It is the amount of Ada being released from the reserve into the active supply through staking and funding the treasury. The inflation rate for Cardano is similar to that of Bitcoin, which starts out at the highest amount at launch and then exponentially reduces over time, as shown in Figure 1.9.

Figure 1.9: Cardano Inflation Rate

Cardano Roadmap

The Cardano roadmap has been organized into five themes or phases: Byron, Shelley, Goguen, Basho, and Voltaire.26 Each theme is focused on a set of capabilities that will be delivered across multiple code releases.27 Each development theme has one or more eras with a specific set of ledger rules, as shown in Tables 1.1 and 1.2.

Ledger Era

Shelley

Decentralization

Shelley

Allegra

Mary

Enabled native tokens and multi-assets

Alonzo

Babbage

Implemented features such as inline datums, reference scripts, and reference inputs

Basho

Scaling

N/A

Improve scalability and interoperability of the Cardano network30

Voltaire

Governance

Conway

Allow the Cardano blockchain to become a self-sustaining system with network participants using their staked Ada and voting rights to influence the future development of the network31

Blockchain Use Cases

To wrap up this introductory chapter, it is helpful to understand some of the blockchain use cases that demonstrate the potential of blockchain technology:

  • Capital Markets: more efficient flow of capital34
  • Tokenization: representing assets such as gold, stocks, bonds, property, or fiat currencies
  • Rewards: the ability to use rewards or points to incentivize behavior
  • Decentralized Exchange (DEX): non-custodial exchanges for asset and derivative swaps
  • Marketplaces: to buy and sell tokenized assets
  • Financial Products: such as lending and borrowing
  • Identity Systems: Self-Sovereign Identity (SSI) solutions
  • Insurance: using smart contracts and data feeds for claim processing
  • Media and Entertainment: track and monetize the life cycle of authentic digital content and distribution35
  • Decentralized Storage: leverage token incentivization models for peer-to-peer decentralized storage
  • Decentralized Autonomous Organizations (DAO): for governance and voting

Conclusion

We should now have a high-level understanding of the evolution of blockchain technology, from the origins of distributed ledgers to the famous Whiteboard Video by Charles Hoskinson, encapsulating the purpose and goals for Cardano. We introduced the Bitcoin and Ethereum whitepapers and will examine both Proof-of-Work and Proof-of-Stake consensus models in more detail in the next chapter. Additionally, we reviewed fundamental blockchain concepts such as the Blockchain Trilemma, tokenomics, Cardano roadmap, and blockchain use cases. In the next chapter, we will take a deeper technical dive into cryptography and consensus algorithms and explore how they make a blockchain secure.

Points to Remember

  • Networks can be either centralized or decentralized.
  • The difficulty of a decentralized network is that it must not have a central authority.
  • A Byzantine fault is where a network node is either unavailable or trying to harm the network.
  • A network uses a consensus algorithm to agree on the correct state of the network.
  • A 51% attack occurs when a single entity controls the majority of nodes in a decentralized ledger network.
  • The Bitcoin whitepaper solved two problems at the same time:
    • Provided a simple and efficient consensus algorithm.
    • Anyone can participate in the consensus algorithm, eliminating the need for a central authority.
  • The Ethereum whitepaper introduced the concept of Turing-complete scripts that can access the ledger’s global state.
  • Cardano is considered a third-generation blockchain with the goals of addressing scalability, interoperability, and sustainability.
  • The price and value of a token are frequently misaligned, so when evaluating a cryptocurrency, it is important to consider fundamental principles such as decentralization, security, and scalability.
  • Cardano has five development themes or phases: Byron, Shelley, Goguen, Basho, and Voltaire.

  • Compliance: KYC and anti-money laundering.

CHAPTER 2

Cryptography and Consensus Algorithms Overview

Introduction

In this chapter, we will introduce cryptography and cryptographic hashes. We will show how cryptographic hashes are made and used in blockchain technology. Next, we will explore the Proof-of-Work consensus protocol used by the Bitcoin blockchain. We will show how a prover and verifier work together to secure a blockchain. Next, we will examine Proof-of-Stake and its benefits over Proof-of-Work. Finally, we will review Ouroboros, Cardano’s Proof-of-Stake consensus protocol.

Structure

In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:

  • Cryptography
    • Public Key Encryption
    • Hashes
  • Digital Signatures
    • Chain of Ownership
  • Proof-of-Work
    • Mining
  • Proof-of-Stake
    • Probabilistic Finality (Chain-based)
    • Absolute Finality (PBFT)
  • Ouroboros
    • Ouroboros Classic
    • Ouroboros BFT
    • Ouroboros Praos
    • Ouroboros Genesis
    • Ouroboros Crypsinous
    • Ouroboros Chronos
    • Ouroboros Protocol Lifecycle

Cryptography

“The first recorded use of cryptography for correspondence was by the Spartans, who, as early as 400 BC, employed a cipher device”1 for secret communication. A cipher is any method of transforming a message to conceal its meaning.2 Before the mid-1970s, all cipher systems used symmetric key algorithms, in which the same cryptographic key is used with the underlying algorithm by both the sender and the recipient, who must both keep it secret.3 The challenge with symmetric encryption is that the secret key must be transmitted securely beforehand, which does not scale when there are many keys to manage or a prior secure channel does not exist.4 This limitation was addressed by asymmetric encryption, used in public key encryption and digital signatures.

Public Key Encryption

The first step in the process of using public key encryption is to generate a pair of public and private keys using a mathematically based one-way cryptographic algorithm.5 The classic cryptography example refers to Alice trying to send an encrypted message to Bob in Figure 2.1. In this case, Alice needs to use Bob’s public key to encrypt the message. The message is unreadable to everyone except for Bob, who holds the private key. When Bob receives the encrypted message, he decrypts it using his private key and reads the original message sent by Alice.

Figure 2.1: Public Key Encryption

Hashes

Cryptographic hashes are generated using a mathematical function that takes an input of arbitrary size and generates a fixed-length output.6 This is known as a one-way hash and cannot be decoded to reveal the original contents of the input. The generated output will always be the same for the same set of inputs. If there is even the smallest change in the inputs, the generated hash will be entirely different, known as the avalanche effect.7 The avalanche effect ensures collision resistance and tamper detection, which is used extensively in blockchains.

The BLAKE2b-256 cryptographic hash algorithm is used in Cardano to generate an address,8 and BLAKE2b-224 is used to generate public key hashes. Hash functions are also used in blockchains to generate a hash of a block containing the list of transactions. This helps check and ensure immutability since any change in the transactions contained in a block will change the block hash.

Digital Signatures

Digital signatures provide a way for Alice to sign a message so that the signature can be later verified by anyone using Alice’s public key.9 The message is not encrypted like public key encryption, instead, a digital signature proves that Alice and only Alice signed this message. In the context of blockchain, the message is a transaction and Alice is the owner of a blockchain address. When a transaction is signed by Alice’s private key, anyone else on the blockchain can verify by using Alice’s public key, as shown in Figure 2.2.

Figure 2.2: Digital Signatures

The first commonly used digital signature was Rivest-Shamir-Asleman (RSA), created in 1977, which uses the property that factoring two large primes is difficult. In 1991, the Digital Signature Algorithm (DSA) was created, leveraging the fact that solving the discrete log problem (x ^ y = z) is difficult. Given x and z, solving for y is difficult when y is a large enough number. An elliptic curve signature algorithm was added to DSA, creating the Elliptic Curve Digital Signature Algorithm (ECDSA), which has a smaller key size and faster operations.10 Cardano uses the Edwards-curve Digital Signature Algorithm (EdDSA), which has the following benefits11:

  • Fast signing and verification
  • High level of security
  • Small signatures
  • Small keys

Table 2.1 summarizes the different signature algorithms used in Bitcoin, Ethereum, and Cardano:

Blockchain

Signing Algorithm

Elliptic Curve

Hash Function

Bitcoin

ECDSA

Secp256k1

SHA-256

Ethereum

ECDSA

Secp256k1

Keccak-256

Cardano

EdDSA

Curve25519

BLAKE2b

Table 2.1: Digital signatures12

Chain of Ownership

An electronic coin, as defined in the Bitcoin whitepaper, is a chain of digital signatures.13 A Bitcoin is transferred digitally by signing the previous transaction’s hash and the next owner’s public key. A transaction will only be approved if the signature matches the public key included in the previous transaction. By linking these together, the chain of ownership can be verified, as shown in Figure 2.3.

Figure 2.3: Chain of Ownership14

Proof-of-Work (PoW)

In the Bitcoin whitepaper, the double-spend problem is where Bob tries to create two transactions to spend the same amount twice. To solve this issue, a timestamp or timeline is introduced. Each block hash is linked to the previous block hash, creating a chain of blocks and ordering transactions, as shown in Figure 2.4. The ledger can now confirm if a transaction is trying to double-spend outputs that have already been spent and reject it accordingly.

To help regulate the frequency of block production, a nonce is used and incremented to find a block hash with a required number of zero bits. The Bitcoin network monitors the pace of block production, and if blocks are produced too fast, the number of required zero bits increases. Conversely, if the number of blocks being produced takes too long, the number of required zero bits decreases. The adjustment of the required number of zero bits is also known as difficulty.15 The level of CPU effort increases exponentially as the difficulty increases. Because the blocks are chained together, undoing a previous transaction requires changing all the previous blocks, which becomes practically impossible as more blocks are confirmed by the network.

Figure 2.4: Proof-of-Work

Mining

The last issue to solve is to provide an incentive for creating new blocks called mining and encourage miners to be honest and not attack the network. This is accomplished by distributing new Bitcoins in the first transaction in each block to the miner who created the block. The number of new Bitcoins created decreases exponentially over time, which controls Bitcoin’s inflation rate. Additionally, if the output value of the transaction is less than the input value, this is considered a transaction fee and awarded to the miner.16 The new Bitcoins plus the transaction fee will make it more profitable for the miner to act honestly rather than try to subvert the network with an invalid block.

Proof-of-Stake (PoS)

With the growing popularity of Bitcoin, the difficulty has increased exponentially. As a result, the PoW consensus protocol requires a lot of energy for miners to produce a block. PoS, on the other hand, is significantly more energy efficient by selecting block producers randomly and proportionally to the amount of Ada they have staked on the network. Stakeholders can delegate their stake to a Stake Pool Operator (SPO) to aggregate Stake together and increase the chances for block production. In a PoS protocol, there is no need to generate computationally intensive puzzles; meanwhile, the security and permissionless properties are still comparable to PoW.17

PoS, however, introduces a different threat vector for determining the block (slot) leader. To achieve a fair randomized election among stakeholders, entropy must be introduced into the system and may be prone to manipulation by an adversary.18 Ouroboros PoS protocol mitigates this risk by using an entropy parameter that represents something unpredictable outside the Cardano ecosystem.19 “The value of this parameter is calculated based on events that could not have been known in advance, or that anyone could have had insider information about.”20

We will look into the Ouroboros protocol in more detail in the next section and will complete the PoS overview by examining the two major categories of PoS protocols:21

  • Probabilistic Finality (Chain-based)
  • Absolute Finality (PBFT)

Probabilistic Finality (Chain-based)

Probabilistic or chain-based finality attempts to simulate the PoW Nakamoto consensus protocol used in Bitcoin. As more blocks are linked together, they form the longest chain and become probabilistically final.22 Typically, waiting until 6 blocks have been linked together is sufficient for probabilistic finality, with a high likelihood that a transaction will not be reverted or rolled back. One of the key advantages of a probabilistic finality mechanism is that it prioritizes network availability for continuous and permissionless access.

Absolute Finality (PBFT)

In an Absolute Finality or Practical Byzantine Fault Tolerant (PBFT) protocol, a validator is randomly chosen to propose a new block. The validators then vote on the inclusion of this new block, and consensus is achieved when the required number of validators agree on accepting a new block.23 Additionally, PoS blockchains like Ethereum implement an economic penalty that makes it prohibitively expensive for validators who are unavailable or act maliciously against the network. By using Absolute and Economic Finality, the protocol prioritizes the consistency of the network and would halt the network rather than accept an invalid transaction for any length of time.24

Ouroboros Protocol

Ouroboros is the first provably secure PoS protocol and the first blockchain protocol based on peer-reviewed research.25 It consists of a family of PoS protocols, including:

  • Ouroboros Classic
  • Ouroboros BFT
  • Ouroboros Praos
  • Ouroboros Genesis
  • Ouroboros Crypsinous
  • Ouroboros Chronos

Ouroboros Classic

Ouroboros Classic was the first Ouroboros protocol published in July 2017.26 The paper defined the protocol as an energy-efficient rival to PoW, introduced the mathematical framework to analyze PoS, and introduced a novel incentive mechanism to reward participants in a PoS setting.27 What distinguishes Ouroboros from other blockchain protocols is its ability to generate unbiased randomness in its leader selection algorithm with security assurances.28 Let’s examine this in more detail.

“Ouroboros divides time on Cardano into epochs, where each epoch is divided into slots. A slot is a short period of time in which a block can be created, and grouping slots into epochs is central to adjusting the leader election process to the dynamically changing stake distribution. A slot leader is elected for each slot, responsible for adding a block to the chain and passing it to the next slot leader. To protect against adversarial attempts to subvert the protocol, each new slot leader must consider the last few blocks of the received chain as transient: only the chain that precedes the prespecified number of transient blocks is considered settled.”29

Ouroboros BFT

The Ouroboros Byzantine Fault Tolerance (BFT) was a simplified protocol30 based on Ouroboros Classic and was used primarily to help with one of the most significant milestones in Cardano known as the Shelly release. It transitioned the network to a federated model to avoid requiring all nodes to be online all the time.31

Ouroboros Praos

Praos introduces a verifiable random function (VRF) on the current timestamp and a nonce for a specific epoch.32 Given a private key and an input, a VRF outputs a pseudo-random number and a proof.33 The corresponding public key can verify the proof and random number. Additionally, this approach prevents anyone except the proposed slot leader from knowing the slot leader for a specific slot in advance. This is important to avoid DDoS or targeted attacks, where a slot leader is publicly known in advance.34

Ouroboros Genesis

The Bitcoin PoW consensus protocol has the advantage that any participant can join the network and participate using only the Genesis block. This is a very important feature of any blockchain, as it does not rely on any trusted notion or checkpoints of the correct state of the network. Ouroboros Genesis addresses this limitation with the Ouroboros PoS protocols by using a novel chain selection rule that allows new or offline participants to join the network using only the genesis block.35 This approach is called “bootstrapping from genesis,” and the Ouroboros Genesis paper proves that it can still retain the prior security assurances while using this approach. The paper also provides proof of the protocol’s Universal Composability, showing that it retains its security properties even when used in different protocol configurations.36

Ouroboros Crypsinous

Ouroboros Crypsinous is the first formally analyzed privacy-preserving PoS blockchain protocol.37 Using SNARKs and key-private forward secure encryption, the paper uses a novel coin evolution technique to prove the network is secure against adaptive attacks.38 This protocol is not intended to be implemented on the main Cardano settlement layer but may be used in a side chain such as Midnight, a privacy-preserving blockchain.

Ouroboros Chronos

Ouroboros Chronos demonstrates how blockchain protocols can synchronize clocks securely via a novel time synchronization mechanism, thereby becoming independent of external time services.39 The paper aims to make the ledger more resistant to attacks that target time information.40

Ouroboros Protocol Lifecycle

Let’s walk through the Ouroboros protocol lifecycle to provide a step-by-step example of how everything fits together.

Time

The first block in the Cardano blockchain is the genesis block, which is the beginning of time from a blockchain perspective. Cardano uses the concept of slots, which occur approximately every second, where at most one block can be created. The expected block production frequency is 0.05, meaning that, on average, a block will be produced every 20 seconds.41 Only a slot leader can create a block for a given slot. There are precisely 432,000 slots in every epoch, equivalent to every 5 days.

Register

The first step in participating in the Cardano network is registering the stake key associated with the Ada to be staked. This typically refers to the registration of Ada to a specific Stake Pool Operator (SPO). The amount staked is used to calculate rewards and distribute proportionally back to the staking addresses. The SPO will handle (among other things) the stake pool registration process, as shown in Figure 2.5:

  1. Synchronize with the network ledger.
  2. Obtain the current slot.
  3. Obtain the global random oracle.

Staking

  1. At the start of each epoch, each stake pool operator queries the blockchain to obtain the stake distribution of a previous block, which was two epochs ago.
  2. Using a VRF generated from the first ⅔ slots in the previous epoch, the stakeholder with their private key can produce an output in the current epoch to determine if they can be a slot leader.42
  3. If the VRF output value is below a certain threshold, they can become the slot leader for that block.
  4. Slot Leader Responsibilities:43
    1. Assemble the transactions that should be included in the block.
    2. Include in the block the random value (v) and proof (π) obtained from the VRF output.
    3. Generate a new private key and delete the old one so it is impossible to forge signatures with the old private key.
    4. Broadcast the new block to the network.

  5. The rewards are calculated at the end of the epoch and then distributed to the reward addresses in the next block.

    Figure 2.5: Ouroboros Protocol Lifecycle

Conclusion

This chapter introduced cryptography with a focus on public key hash algorithms. We saw how this extended to a concept called digital signatures, which underpins cryptocurrencies today. By linking digital signatures, a blockchain can create proof-of-ownership for the movement of a digital coin like Bitcoin. We looked deeper at Proof-of-Work and how mining a block incentivizes miners to be honest and prevent network attacks. We then looked at the energy-efficient Proof-of-Stake and how to mitigate the risk associated with predicting random number generation, along with the differences and tradeoffs between Probabilistic and Absolute Finality. Finally, we reviewed the Ouroboros family of protocols and the current protocol lifecycle on the Cardano Mainnet today. Using our knowledge of cryptography and how PoS blockchains work, we will explore how to transfer value using transactions in the next chapter.

Points to Remember

  • Using public key encryption requires generating a pair of public and private keys.
  • If a transaction is signed by a private key, anyone on the blockchain can verify it using the corresponding public key.
  • Bitcoin is transferred digitally by signing the previous transaction’s hash and including the next owner’s public key.
  • To solve the double-spend problem, a timestamp is included in the hash of each block, and more timestamps are included in the chain of blocks, securing the prior blocks.
  • Bitcoin mining incentivizes honest behavior by distributing new Bitcoins and transaction fees as the first transaction in each block to the miner who created the block.
  • Proof-of-Stake is significantly more energy efficient than Proof-of-Work.
  • Proof-of-Stake selects block producers (validators) randomly and proportionally to the native currency they have staked on the network.
  • Entropy must be introduced into the system to achieve a fair randomized election among Proof-of-Stake stakeholders.
  • With security assurances, Ouroboros can generate unbiased randomness in the protocol’s leader selection algorithm.
  • Probabilistic finality occurs as more blocks are linked, forming the longest chain.
  • Validators vote on including a new block, and the outcome is considered final, achieving Absolute Finality.

Key Terms

  • Block: Contains zero or more transactions.
  • Chain of Ownership: Links digital signatures together.
  • DDos: Distributed denial of service.
  • Difficulty: The increasing rate of computation required to create a block.
  • Digital Signature: Signed message using a private key.
  • Entropy: The amount of randomness in a system.
  • Epoch: A unit of time that contains a fixed number of slots.
  • Finality: A state that can be considered final and irreversible.
  • Mining: Incentives paid to miners for creating a block.
  • Public Key Encryption: Generates a pair of public and private keys.
  • PoS: Proof-of-Stake
  • PoW: Proof-of-Work
  • Slot: Occurs every second and may contain at most one block.
  • SPO: Stake Pool Operator
  • VRF: Verifiable Random Function

CHAPTER 3

Transactions

Introduction

In this chapter, we will first look at Cardano Addresses, how they are built, and the data inside them. Next, we will look at the UTXO architecture and show how transactions are created, along with optional metadata. We will also introduce how Smart Contracts, known as Plutus scripts, are executed and evaluated. We will see how Redeemers pass data into Plutus scripts and how Datums are used for data storage and persistence. Finally, we will look at the transaction Witness Set and how it authorizes the spending of transaction inputs.

Structure

In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:

  • Addresses
    • Network Tag
    • Base Address
    • Enterprise Address
    • Reward (Staking) Address
  • UTXO
    • Transaction Inputs
    • Transaction Outputs
  • Metadata
  • Plutus Scripts
  • Datum
  • Redeemer
  • Witness Set

Addresses

A Cardano (Shelly) address is a sequence of bytes encoded using Bech32.1 There are three types of addresses that you will typically interact with on Cardano:

  • Base Addresses
  • Enterprise Addresses
  • Reward (Stake) Addresses

The structure of a Cardano address is shown in Figure 3.1:

Figure 3.1: Cardano Address Structure

The header type indicates what type of address (or payload), and the network tag indicates what network this address is associated with.

Network Tag

Header Bits

Network

xxxx0000

Testnet(s)

xxxx0001

Mainnet

Table 3.1: Network Tag

Base Address

A base address is the most common type of address, as shown in Table 3.2. When the payment part is a PaymentKeyHash, the transaction must be signed with the corresponding private key for this address. When the payment part is a ScriptHash, a script with a matching hash must be executed and evaluated to true to spend from this address. The delegation part is similar to the payment part but is used to register (and delegate) a staking certificate for Ada that is locked at this address. As we recall from the previous chapter, staking is the mechanism that helps secure a Proof-of-Stake blockchain and receive rewards for doing so.

Header Bits

Payment Part

Delegation Part

0000xxxx

PaymentKeyHash

StakeKeyHash

0001xxxx

ScriptHash

StakeKeyHash

0010xxxx

PaymentKeyHash

ScriptHash

0011xxxx

ScriptHash

ScriptHash

Table 3.2: Base Address

Enterprise Address

An enterprise address is similar to a base address, except there is no delegation part. The header types for enterprise addresses are listed in Table 3.3.

Header Bits

Payment Part

Delegation Part

0110xxxx

PaymentKeyHash

N/A

0111xxxx

ScriptHash

N/A

Table 3.3: Enterprise Address

Reward (Stake) Addresses

Reward addresses are used to distribute rewards for staking in the Proof-of-Stake protocol and have the following properties:2

  • It uses account-style accounting and not UTXO.
  • Account balances are only increased when staking rewards are distributed.
  • To transfer out the Ada locked at a Reward Address, a withdrawal-type transaction is required to release the funds and zero out the balance of the rewards address.
  • A one-to-one relationship exists between a registered staking key and a reward account address.

Header Bits

Stake Reference

1110xxxx

StakeKeyHash

1111xxxx

ScriptHash

Table 3.4: Reward (Stake) Address

To put it all together, the following output is generated by the cardano-address address inspect command line tool. Note that in an address, a chain pointer may exist that refers to an on-chain location containing a stake key registration certificate.3 Pointers, however, are rarely used, and, in the following example, we can see that the stake_reference is “by_value”, indicating that the staking certificate is at this staking address and not a pointer.

UTXO

Cardano and Bitcoin use a similar unspent transaction output (UTXO) model, whereas a blockchain like Ethereum uses an account-based model. In a UTXO model, a transaction consumes one or more UTXOs as inputs and then produces one or more UTXOs as outputs, where each UTXO is locked at an address. A UTXO can only be spent (used) once and is no longer an unspent transaction output if it has already been used as a transaction input. The amount of assets at an address is the sum of all the UTXOs at a particular address. Additionally, only Alice can spend the UTXO at Alice’s address because she has the private key and can create a signature to prove it.

Figure 3.2: UTXO Model

In Figure 3.2, Alice has a UTXO with 10 Ada and wants to send 2 Ada to Bob. A transaction is created, and the UTXO with the 10 Ada is used as the input, and 2 new UTXOs are created as the outputs. The UTXO with 2 Ada will go to Bob’s address, 0.17 Ada will pay for transaction fees, and the remaining Ada (change) will be the UTXO that goes back to Alice. This works the same way as using a physical wallet when trying to pay with a 10-dollar bill at a store for a 2-dollar bottle of water.

Using a blockchain explorer, we can see what this transaction looks like on the Cardano blockchain, as shown in Figure 3.3: https://preprod.cexplorer.io/tx/c9e6dc1e7ecdf7110747c91d0d638ce896435a504e745025996230533ae9f548

Figure 3.3: Block Explorer Transaction View

Transaction Input

A transaction input simply points to a transaction hash on the ledger and index since there may be more than one transaction output for a specific transaction.

Figure 3.4: Transaction Input

The ledger specification4 for an input is defined as:

transaction_input = [ transaction_id : $hash32

, index : uint

]

Transaction Output

Before the Alonzo Era of smart contracts, a transaction output contained the following:

  • Address that the output is locked at
  • Value of Ada and any Cardano native tokens
  • Optional datum used for storing a persistent data hash

The ability to store a datum value with a UTXO is how Cardano uses a EUTXO model, whereas Bitcoin only uses a UTXO model.

Here is an example of 2 EUTXOs at an address on Cardano*:

$ cardano-cli query utxo --address addr_test1vzu…dxn7 --cardano-mode --testnet-magic 1

TxHash TxIx Amount

--------------------------------------------------------------------

158…925 0 3551912116 lovelace + TxOutDatumNone

b4e…0d2 0 5000000 lovelace + TxOutDatumNone

*Please note that the address and TxHash have been truncated for formatting.

Observations:

  • In this example, there are 2 UTXOs.
  • Transaction hash 158…925 with index 0 has 3,551,912,116 lovelace.
  • Transaction hash b4e…0d2 with index 0 has 5,000,000 lovelace.
  • There is 1,000,000 lovelace in 1 Ada.

  • The amount of Ada locked at the address is:

    3,551.912116

    + 5.000000

    3,556.912116

  • There is no datum associated with these UTXOs.

Post Alonzo, additional fields were added to the outputs. In the current Conway Era, we have the following output fields:

  • Address that the output is locked at
  • Amount of Ada and any Cardano native tokens
  • Optional datum used for storing a persistent data hash on-chain
  • Optional inline datum used for storing persistent data value on-chain
  • Optional reference script that allows a smart contract to be stored on-chain

Here is an example of a UTXO in the Conway era:

$ cardano-cli query utxo --tx-in 5f07cfe475c846a7a644850f7174d98d281b09d3a780a0e51d06d1adff0cdcf7#4 --cardano-mode --testnet-magic 1 --out-file utxo.out

$ cat utxo.out

{

"5f07cfe475c846a7a644850f7174d98d281b09d3a780a0e51d06d1adff0cdcf7#4"

: {

"address":

"addr_test1qrazs4ngp8vl0h2qaexugnm4lhjts0g7l94d780pyx99f87m76rh0cewyp03f8w9e2lemp96jtqk0hyjqtg5528pn9tsyqhfr5",

"datum": null,

"datumhash": null,

"inlineDatum": null,

"referenceScript": null,

"value": {

"lovelace": 981840654

}

}

The datum field contains the datum value attached to a transaction when it is submitted to the Cardano blockchain. If an inlineDatum is used, the datum value is stored on-chain, otherwise, only the datumhash is recorded on-chain.

Looking at the ledger specification,5 the output is defined as follows:

post_alonzo_transaction_output =

{ 0 : address

, 1 : value

, ? 2 : datum_option ; datum option

, ? 3 : script_ref ; script reference

}

datum_option = [ 0, $hash32 // 1, data ]

Metadata

A transaction can have metadata and is optionally included when submitting a transaction to the network. Only the hash of the metadata contents is stored on-chain and is not accessible to smart contracts. Blockchain indexers, however, capture, store, and retrieve the full contents of the transaction metadata, which are used for off-chain transaction building. CIP-25,6 using label 721, was the first transaction metadata standard for minting transactions of Non-Fungible Tokens or NFTs.

A minimal structure for an NFT using CIP-25, version 1, is as follows:

{

"721": {

"<policy_id>": {

"<asset_name>": {

"name": <string>,

"image": <uri | array>

}

}

}

}

An example of this standard being used and viewed with a blockchain explorer7 is shown in Figure 3.5:

Figure 3.5: Transaction Metadata

Where:

  • policy_id is 63733059deaddff620870b22f1ff6a9907bcd103e70d5dfcc53bbb14
  • asset_name is Mad Dog
  • NFT name is Mad Dog
  • NFT image is QmckPQPzthJjkS9GRSxdw2SzSinAwdDW34xXDZd26MrV5S
  • NFT description is Crazy dog that loves treats

Plutus Scripts

Up to this point, we have only looked at transactions that send (spend) assets from an address owned by a user with a corresponding public and private key. In Cardano, we have Smart Contracts that use a Turing-complete scripting language called Plutus. A Plutus script can spend or mint successfully only if the evaluated result returns true. The script is included when submitting a transaction so it can be evaluated by the Plutus Core Evaluator inside a Cardano node. As we recall, the network comprises connected nodes that form a consensus on valid blocks. So, when a transaction is submitted, each node must execute the included script and confirm the result. If true, the node will accept the transaction into the block and become part of the blockchain.

Typically, a Plutus script is either a spending (validator) or a minting script. A spending script involves spending UTXOs locked at a script address. The script address is simply a hash of the script itself and the appropriate address header byte value. A minting script involves minting (adding) or burning (removing) native tokens in the Cardano ledger. When a minting script is used to mint, the new tokens must be included in a UTXO(s) as output(s) and sent to an address. Conversely, when a minting script is used to burn, the tokens must be included in UTXO(s) as input(s).

Datums

When we looked at transaction outputs, we observed that you can include a datum value for data persistence. This datum value can be read by a Plutus spending script that examines the transaction UTXO input. Since a UTXO can only be spent once as an input, the datum value must be recreated and included in the UTXO output if used by another script. If using a reference input, however, the UTXO is read-only, so the datum value can be reused for any number of transactions serially or in parallel.

Redeemers

Redeemers can provide information to a Plutus script during script evaluation within a transaction. For example, the ability to specify what type of mint operation (minting or burning) a transaction will execute. Redeemers can also pass information to a Plutus script that is only known at run time and does not need to be persistent.

The following are the different types of Redeemers from the ledger specification8:

redeemer_tag =

0 ; Spending

/ 1 ; Minting

/ 2 ; Certifying

/ 3 ; Rewarding

/ 4 ; Voting

/ 5 ; Proposing

We reviewed the Spending and Minting Reedmer tags, so now let’s look at Certifying and Rewarding. A Certifying Redeemer is used for publishing certificates to the network. This is used, for example, when registering a staking key to the network for staking. A Rewarding Redeemer is used for withdrawing staking rewards locked at a stake key address. Recall that a staking address is not a UTXO-based address and is only used to receive staking rewards. At the time of writing, Voting and Proposing Redeemer tags will be used for on-chain governance and voting.

Putting this all together is a simplified diagram (without a transaction fee), as shown in Figure 3.6, of the inputs and outputs to create a transaction using a Plutus Script, which spends 2 Ada.

Figure 3.6: Simplified Script Transaction

Witness Set

All transactions include a witness set that includes the witnesses required to spend a UTXO. In this chapter, we have focused on the following types of witnesses:

  • Signature
  • Redeemer
  • Datum
  • Plutus Script

Therefore, when looking at the ledger specification,9 all transactions must have the following high-level structure:

transaction =

[ transaction_body

, transaction_witness_set

, bool

, auxiliary_data / null

]

Where:

  • Transaction_body contains, among other things, a fee, the UTXO inputs and outputs, metadata hash, script hash, and minting value if any.
  • Transaction_witness_set contains any witnesses required for spending UTXO inputs
  • Bool indicates if this is a valid transaction
  • Auxiliary_data optionally contains transaction metadata

Conclusion

We reviewed the different types of Cardano addresses and how they are constructed and deconstructed. We looked at the UTXO architecture, how inputs and outputs are used, and how UTXOs are locked at an address. We looked at an example of transaction metadata and how it is stored and linked to an on-chain transaction. Next, we looked at the Datum and Redeemer and how it provides information to a Plutus script as part of a transaction. Finally, we reviewed the concept of a Witness Set that allows a transaction to spend UTXO inputs. In the next chapter, we will look at Plutus scripts in more detail and how to use them in a transaction.

Points to Remember

  • There are three main types of addresses: Base, Enterprise, and Reward.
  • A transaction header type determines the type of address.
  • A transaction network tag determines the network a transaction is associated with (Mainnet or Testnet).
  • A transaction contains a Payment Part and an optional Delegation Part.
  • The Payment or Delegation Part can be either a PaymentKeyHash (public key hash) or a ScriptHash.
  • A reward address is a special type of account-based address only used for staking rewards.
  • A transaction consumes one or more UTXOs as inputs and must produce one or more UTXOs as outputs.
  • A signature is proof that the transaction was signed by the correct private key.
  • A transaction input includes an index because there may be more than one output UTXO for a given transaction.
  • A Datum is used to store persistent data on-chain and is included in a UTXO.
  • A Datum value can be stored as a hash or in full as an inline datum.
  • The Plutus Core Evaluator evaluates a Plutus script to see if it returns true.
  • A Plutus script is typically a spending (validator) or minting script.
  • A Plutus spending (validator) script spends UTXOs locked at a script address.
  • A Plutus minting script is used to mint or burn Cardano native tokens.
  • A UTXO can also be a reference input, a read-only UTXO that cannot be spent.
  • A redeemer provides information to a script during a transaction evaluation.
  • The witness set may contain a signature, redeemer, datum, or Plutus scripts.

Multiple Choice Questions

  1. What is not a valid Cardano Address?
    1. Base
    2. Enterprise
    3. Reward
    4. UTXO
  2. A script address is derived from?
    1. Public key hash
    2. Plutus script hash
    3. Private stake key hash
    4. Private key hash
  3. What does a signature prove?
    1. A transaction is signed with a valid public key
    2. A transaction is signed with a valid staking key
    3. A transaction is signed with a valid private key
    4. A transaction is signed with a valid reward key
  4. A Plutus script evaluates to?
    1. A boolean value
    2. A binary value
    3. A hexadecimal value
    4. Non-zero value
  5. What is a Redeemer?
    1. Used for identifying reference scripts
    2. Used for transaction balancing
    3. Provides information to a script at runtime
    4. Halts a transaction if false

Answers

  1. d
  2. b
  3. c
  4. a
  5. c

Questions

  1. Why can’t you lock a UTXO at a reward address?
  2. What is the process for claiming staking rewards?
  3. Why do you include a Plutus script as a witness in the witness set and what can it prove?
  4. Why not store all metadata data on-chain, not just the metadata hash?
  5. Why is a Plutus spending script also known as a validator?

Key Terms

  • Base Address: Address that includes both the payment and staking keys.
  • Datum: It is used to store the datum hash on-chain.
  • Enterprise Address: Address that only includes the payment key.
  • Inline datum: It is used to store the actual datum value on-chain.
  • Metadata: Data that describes a transaction.
  • Minting script: A Plutus script used to mint or burn Cardano native tokens.
  • NFT: Non-Fungible Token.
  • PaymentKeyHash: The hash digest of a public key.
  • Plutus Core Evaluator: The component in a node that evaluates a plutus script.
  • Plutus script: A Turing-complete language that is executed on the node.
  • Redeemer: Redeemer is used to pass information into a script at runtime.
  • Reference script: A read-only UTXO that cannot be spent.
  • Reward Address: It is used for distributing and receiving staking rewards.
  • ScriptHash: The hash digest of a Plutus script.
  • Signature: The cryptographic hash signature of a transaction hash.
  • Spending (validator) script: A Plutus script used to spend UTXOs locked at a script address.
  • UTXO: An unspent transaction output.
  • Witness: It provides proof that the transaction can spend UTXO inputs.

CHAPTER 4

Plutus

Introduction

Plutus, the Smart Contract language for Cardano, is compiled and executed as an Untyped Plutus Core (UPLC) program. The first Plutus smart contracts were written in Haskell using the PlutusTx Haskell module. Unfortunately, PlutusTx was not optimized and created a very challenging development experience when Cardano smart contracts first launched. Shortly thereafter, alternative Plutus scripting languages, such as Aiken (https://aiken-lang.org/) and Helios (https://github.com/Hyperion-BT/helios), emerged, which were significantly more performant and used syntax and coding styles similar to popular programming languages like Rust or C.

In this chapter, we will look at the Plutus core specification in detail and how the Cardano ledger executes a Lambda Calculus program. We will examine the different data structures used in Plutus and how the data is serialized and transmitted using the Concise Binary Object Representation (CBOR) format. Next, we will look at the Plutus cost model and how it is used to calculate the CPU and memory units, which must not exceed the maximum values. Finally, we will discuss collateral and its role in a transaction.

Structure

In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:

  • Plutus Specification
  • Concise Binary Representation (CBOR)
    • Transaction
    • Redeemer
    • Plutus Script
    • Datum
  • Plutus Execution Budget
  • Collateral

Plutus Specification

The first generation of scripts on Cardano were native scripts,1 followed by Plutus.2 Native (phase-1) scripts have very limited functionality and are executed internally within the ledger. Plutus, a Turing-complete scripting language, executes outside the ledger but requires a successful evaluation to spend a UTXO locked by a Plutus script.3 The ledger must support multiple Plutus script versions, so each script includes the version number in the script itself. This way, the ledger will know how to evaluate a script, each with its own version. Table 4.1 summarizes the Plutus versions known at the time of writing, and we will only focus on PlutusV2 and Plutus Core Version 1.0.0 for this book.

Era

Ledger Language (Id)

Plutus Core Version

Protocol Version

Date

Alonzo

PlutusV1 (0)

1.0.0

5.0

Sep, 2021

Vasil

PlutusV2 (1)

1.0.0

7.0

Jun, 2022

Valentine

PlutusV2 (1)

1.0.0

8.0

Feb, 2023

Conway

PlutusV3 (2)

1.1.0

9.0

Upcoming

Table 4.1: Plutus Versions

The objective of the Plutus specification was to create a simple scripting language that can be evaluated with minimal (and constrained) computational footprint on the Cardano Node. Unlike the Ethereum Virtual Machine, which has a long and complicated proof, the Plutus abstract state machine is short and succinct.4 This is a very desirable property when building a highly stable blockchain to minimize hard fork upgrades. Plutus scripts are written using a language called Untyped Plutus Core or UPLC, which was derived from System F, a form of Lambda Calculus.5 Fortunately, Smart Contract Plutus development uses higher-level programming languages that compile and generate the UPLC for us.

Note: Learning UPLC or Lambda Calculus is not required to write Cardano smart contracts. The remainder of this section is optional but may be helpful to readers looking for more details on how generated UPLC code maps to the Plutus Core specification. For an introduction to Lambda Calculus, please look at the video “Lambda Calculus - Fundamentals of Lambda Calculus & Functional Programming in JavaScript.”6

UPLC Primitive Types

UPLC uses a simple language syntax with a limited set of atomic types, listed in Table 4.2:

Type

Syntax

integer

-?[0-9]+

bytestring

#([0-9A-Fa-f][0-9A-Fa-f])*

string

UTF-8 characters, enclosed in double quotes " "

bool

True | False

unit

()

Type

Denotation

Data

data Data =

Constr Integer [Data]

| Map [(Data, Data)]

| List [Data]

| I Integer

| B ByteString

Table 4.2: UPLC Atomic Types

In addition to the atomic data types, there are two types of operators included in UPLC, as shown in Table 4.3:

Operator op

|op|

Denotation

list

1

[list(t)] = [t]

pair

2

[pair(t1,t2)] = [t1] x [t2]

Table 4.3: UPLC Type Operators

The con keyword defines constants, followed by the type declaration and the value. Table 4.4 provides some usage examples:7

Type

Example

unit

con unit ()

bool

con bool False

integer

con integer 123

bytestring

con bytestring #abc123

string

con string "GM Cardano"

pair

con pair<bool, integer> [True, 123]

list

list<bytestring> [#0d, #f3]

Table 4.4: UPLC Type Examples8

A lambda function uses the keyword lam and is passed a variable name and returns a term that can be either a constant, a function, or a well-formed value of any type. The UPLC program must not contain any free variables and, therefore, be considered a “closed term.”9 For example, (program 1.0.0 (lam x x)) is a valid program, but (program 1.0.0 (lam x y)) is not. Please note that all Plutus scripts must include a program and version.

Plutus Core has no arithmetic operators but uses built-in functions for arithmetic operations. Some builtin functions are domain-specific to the Cardano blockchain, while other builtin functions may be added over time. The last items we will review are the force and delay directives. The force directive is applied to a polymorphic function to force the instantiation of a typed parameter.10 The delay directive is used for lazy evaluation of a branch condition, which preserves type abstraction.11 Lazy evaluation allows for more efficient code execution because values are only computed if they are used. This also allows for the creation of infinite data structures that are impossible to create in languages like C.

UPLC Grammar

Putting this all together, we define the grammar of a UPLC program, as shown in Figure 4.1:

Figure 4.1: UPLC Grammar (Plutus Core Version 1.0.0)12

UPLC Example

Looking at a simplified example, we can see how a higher-level language, such as Aiken, generates a UPLC program. Table 4.5 has a simple validator script that always returns true. The program uses lambda (lam) functions to evaluate and return the constant True.

Higher Level Language

Table 4.5: UPLC Example

Concise Binary Representation (CBOR)

CBOR is a binary and encoding format defined in the Internet standard RFC 8949.13 In the previous chapter on Transactions, we looked at the Cardano ledger CDDL specification,14 which defines the format of a transaction. When a transaction is submitted to the network, the entire transaction and all relevant data are serialized and encoded in CBOR. We will examine a transaction in detail and deserialize it to see the UPLC Plutus code and related data (redeemer and datum).

For example, Figure 4.2 shows a transaction executing a Plutus validator script:

https://preprod.cexplorer.io/tx/99c529355ebd1f410517457b6ff04e66c2de28b8667b9428ec232e03efa22c29

Figure 4.2: Transaction Example

When we decode this to a diagnostic form using https://cbor.nemo157.com/, we can see the transaction details, as illustrated in Figures 4.3 and 4.4. Figure 4.3 shows the UTXO inputs and outputs and the required signatures for this transaction:

Figure 4.3: CBOR Transaction Part 1
