Figure 4.4: CBOR Transaction Part 2

Redeemer

To get a human-readable version of the Redeemer, we need to convert from the Hex ByteString to a UTF-8 string. Plutus, however, will use the ByteString directly during script execution. Here is an example of using xxd to convert a Hex-encoded ByteString to a normal string:

$ echo 48656c6c6f2c20576f726c6421 | xxd -p -r

$ Hello, World!

Plutus Script

The Plutus script is double encoded (CBOR + flat), meaning we need to deserialize it twice to get it into UPLC. So, the first step is to decode it using a CBOR decoder,15 and save it to a file called plutus.flat:

$ cat plutus.flat

010000323232323232323222232325333008323232533300b002100114a066446466002

00200644a66602200229404c8c94ccc040cdc78010028a511330040040013014002375c

60240026eb0c038c03cc03cc03cc03cc03cc03cc03cc03cc020c008c020014dd7180118

0400399b8f375c6002600e00a91010d48656c6c6f2c20576f726c6421002300d0011498

58c94ccc020cdc3a400000226464a66601a601e0042930b1bae300d0013006004163006

0033253330073370e900000089919299980618070010a4c2c6eb8c030004c01401058c0

1400c8c014dd5000918019baa0015734aae7555cf2ab9f5742ae8

Next, we need to convert it from hex to UPLC using the Aiken utility tool to decode it:

$ aiken uplc decode --hex plutus.flat

(program

1.0.0

[

(lam

i_0

[

(lam

i_1

…

)

(force (builtin headList))

]

)

(force (builtin tailList))

]

)

Datum

Next, we will look at a Datum in Figure 4.5 using cexplorer.io:16

Figure 4.5: Datum Example

The datum in Figure 4.5 is an inline datum, so the value is stored on-chain and locked at a UTXO that we want to spend. If we look at the UTXO, we will see that the value is encoded in CBOR hex format:

d8799f581c6d04be153546d63c8a33e0c11ec44cf790803f31c2f893fc24152953ff

When we decode the datum CBOR hex value, we get the following output of a byte array:

121_0([_

h'6d04be153546d63c8a33e0c11ec44cf790803f31c2f893fc24152953',

])

In this example, the datum value represents a public key hash.

Plutus Execution Budget

Looking at the Redeemer Witness of the transaction in Figure 4.4, we will notice an execution budget for the attached plutus script:

[30485_1, 10332844_2]

These values refer to the memory and CPU (steps) execution units, which are defined in the Plutus ledger specification:

ex_units = [mem: uint, steps: uint]

The mem and steps are calculated and submitted with the transaction, then verified by the Plutus Core Evaluator during script execution. If either of the values exceeds the maximum limits during script execution, then an error will be raised:

Error: The budget when the machine terminated was:\n({ cpu: -84834\n| mem: 17553\n}

In this example, the cpu/steps value was exceeded and resulted in a negative number. As a result, the execution was halted, and the validation failed. To calculate the mem and cpu/step costs for a given Plutus script, a cost model17 is used to map the execution cost for each Plutus operation. The cost model is located in the network protocol parameters and can be changed over time via network updates. To see the protocol parameters, you can use the cardano-cli tool:

$ cardano-cli query protocol-parameters --testnet-magic 1

{

"collateralPercentage": 150,

"costModels": {

…

"PlutusScriptV2": {

"addInteger-cpu-arguments-intercept": 205665,

"addInteger-cpu-arguments-slope": 812,

"addInteger-memory-arguments-intercept": 1,

"addInteger-memory-arguments-slope": 1,

…

"maxTxExecutionUnits": {

"memory": 14000000,

"steps": 10000000000

},

"maxTxSize": 16384,

…

The protocol parameters specify, among other things, the execution cost of each Plutus operation, the maximum memory and steps units per transaction, and the maximum overall transaction size (maxTxSize) in KB.

Collateral

The last topic we will look at is collateral included in a transaction. Collateral is a small amount of Lovelace (1.5 × Transaction Fee) that will be used if there is a phase-2 Plutus script failure.18 Any change left over from the collateral inputs is returned to the user and is called the collateral return. However, Plutus script evaluation is usually done locally before submitting it to the network. This will catch and prevent the submission of an invalid Plutus script. The role of collateral is to help protect the network from malicious or spam transactions.

Conclusion

The reader should now understand the Plutus core specification and the UPLC code used for Plutus script execution and evaluation. We examined a CBOR transaction, how to deserialize it, and looked into the transaction details. We also looked at the Plutus script execution budget and how it is calculated using the cost model. Lastly, we discussed collateral, which is used during a phase-2 Plutus script evaluation failure. The next chapter will explore the off-chain code used to create and submit a transaction. We will also look at the user wallet, which is the gateway to interacting with the Cardano blockchain and smart contracts.

Points to Remember

  • The first generation of scripts on Cardano was native scripts that were evaluated inside the ledger.
  • A Plutus script is a phase-2 script that evaluates outside of the ledger.
  • Untyped Plutus Core or UPLC is a form of Lambda Calculus.
  • UPLC is the low-level language that is executed on the node.
  • Writing smart contracts is done with a higher-level language that generates the low-level UPLC.
  • UPLC programs must be closed with all terms properly defined.
  • A UPLC program can take advantage of the lazy evaluation found in Lambda Calculus.
  • CBOR is a binary standard for serializing and deserializing data structures.
  • Cardano transactions are serialized to CBOR when submitted to the network and deserialized during Cardano node validation.
  • The Redeemer in a transaction contains the memory and step/cpu units that a script will consume.
  • During Plutus script evaluation, the script will halt and fail if the memory or step/cpu units exceed the maximum allowable units.
  • The cost model in the network parameters contains the cost for each Plutus operation and the maximum memory and step/cpu unit allowed in a transaction.
  • Collateral must be provided in a transaction and is designed to protect the network from malicious and spam transactions.

Multiple Choice Questions

  1. The first generation of scripts on Cardano are?
    1. PlutusV1
    2. PlutusV2
    3. Native Scripts
    4. Smart Scripts
  2. What statement is false about Native Scripts and Plutus Scripts?
    1. Both must be evaluated to be true to spend a UTXO locked by the script
    2. Both can create a multi-sig script address
    3. Both are Turing-complete
    4. Native scripts are a phase-1 script
  3. What is lam in UPLC?
    1. A lambda function abstraction
    2. A lambda function application
    3. A lambda function permutation
    4. A lambda function association
  4. Why is lazy evaluation desirable?
    1. Less efficient code execution
    2. More efficient code execution
    3. Forces evaluation of all declared variables
    4. Cannot create infinite data structures
  5. Where is the current Plutus cost model located?
    1. Node configuration
    2. Plutus script configuration
    3. Plutus evaluator configuration
    4. Protocol parameters

Answers

  1. c
  2. c
  3. a
  4. b
  5. d

Questions

  2. Are Turing-complete and Lambda Calculus programs formally equivalent?
  3. What are the differences between CBOR and flat encoding?
  4. Why are the memory and cpu/step units included in the Redeemer in a transaction?

  6. What inputs can be used for collateral?

Key Terms

  • CBOR: Concise Binary Object Representation
  • Collateral Return: The amount of Ada change returned if collateral is used during a transaction.
  • CDDL: Concise Data Definition Library
  • Cost Model: The execution cost parameters for each Plutus operation.
  • Deserialize: The process of decoding code and data structures from a binary format.
  • Execution Cost: The amount of memory and cpu/step units used for each Plutus operation.
  • Higher Level Programming: A language that allows the use of more complex functions and data structures to make the code more readable.
  • Lambda Calculus: A minimal language to define functions and applications.
  • Lazy Evaluation: Only instantiate variables that are used in code execution.
  • Native Scripts: Simple scripts that are used in phase-1 evaluation.
  • Protocol Parameters: A file with parameters and settings that is used by the network.
  • Serialize: The process of encoding code and data structures to a binary format.
  • UPLC: Untyped Plutus Core

CHAPTER 5

Transaction Builder

Introduction

This chapter will combine our knowledge of on-chain Plutus scripts and how to lock Ada into a smart contract using off-chain code. We will look at Cardano wallets, the gateway to interacting with the blockchain and Smart Contracts. Next, we will look at some of the tools to help build and submit transactions, such as Demeter.run. Then, we will build a simple vesting smart contract using two popular approaches: Helios and Lucid + Aiken.

In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:

Transaction Lifecycle

In the previous chapter, we looked at how the Cardano node and ledger evaluate a Plutus script. This chapter will focus on how to build and submit a transaction that includes a Plutus script. The building of a transaction is assembling the UTXO inputs and outputs, attaching scripts we want to run, and adding redeemers, datums, and metadata, if any. Once the transaction is assembled, we must get it signed and include the signature as a witness. Figure 5.1 shows the off-chain steps of building, signing, serializing, and submitting a transaction to the network.

Figure 5.1: Transaction Lifecycle

Wallets

In cryptocurrencies, a wallet is the user interface that interacts with the blockchain. A wallet typically allows users to create an account, which is a collection of UTXOs locked at a public key address. This type of account abstraction helps users visualize familiar concepts such as account balances and transaction history. Many wallets also allow users to select stake pools and register their stake key to collect staking rewards. Table 5.1 lists some of the wallet providers on the Cardano network in alphabetical order.

Hardware Wallet Support: A wallet that supports hardware devices in signing transactions.

Mobile App: A wallet with a native mobile app on iOS or Android devices.

Open Source: The wallet’s open-source code is available on GitHub.

Seed Phrase

To create an account with a wallet, you must either create a new seed phrase or import an existing one. If we recall from Chapter 3, Transactions, a private key is needed to sign a transaction. To create this private key, wallets typically use a seed phrase to generate a random private key. This is a two-step process, whereby, a mnemonic sentence or a group of words is used to create a random seed.2 This random seed is then used to derive the public and private key pairs.3

Testnet Faucet

Cardano typically has two Testnets used for end-to-end testing before Mainnet.4 Please be mindful of what network you are connecting to when sending and receiving funds.

  • Preview Testnet: The epoch has been shortened to 1 day and is ahead of a Mainnet fork by at least 4 weeks.
  • Preprod Testnet: This network is used for release candidate testing for mainnet launches. It mimics production as closely as possible and is the more stable Testnet network.

We will use the Testnet faucet to acquire test Ada and send it to our wallet address. In this book, we will use the Eternl wallet, and Figure 5.2 shows how to get an address to receive funds.

Figure 5.2: Eternl Wallet Receives Funds

Copy the address and paste it into the Testnet faucet form on https://docs.cardano.org/cardano-testnet/tools/faucet/, as shown in Figure 5.3.

Figure 5.3: Testnet Faucet Request

Select “Request Funds,” and you should see the “Success” message, which includes a transaction hash that you can use on https://preprod.cexplorer.io/ to verify the status.

Figure 5.4: Testnet Faucet Submitted

After 10–60 seconds, you should see 10,000 test Ada (tAda) in the Eternl wallet.

Figure 5.5: Eternl Wallet Account Summary

Address Derivation

The Hierarchy for Deterministic (HD) wallets, such as Eternl, follows the specification in CIP-18525 for the derivation path:

m / purpose' / coin_type' / account' / role / index

In Figure 5.5, we can see the derivation path for the receiving address is:

m/1852'/1815'/0'/0/0

Table 5.2 explains each of the derivation path elements:

Name

Value

Notes

Purpose

1852

Specification name (for example, CIP-1852)

Coin Type

1815

Different value for each cryptocurrency (for example, Ada Lovelace birth)

Account

0

Allows for creating separate wallet accounts

Role

0

0 = External Chain (receiving payments)

1 = Internal Chain (change address)

2 = Staking Key

Index

0

Address index starting at 0

Table 5.2: Derivation Path

You can create different addresses for the same private key to receive payments (and change) using HD wallets. In practice, however, using a “single address mode” is generally preferable, where the same address is re-used. Creating a new address for each transaction helps anonymize it so it cannot be linked to the wallet account. With Cardano, however, as soon as a stake key is registered for a wallet address, all transactions from that address can be linked via the stake key. Having multiple addresses for the receiving and change addresses adds additional dApp complexity, which is why some wallets only support single address mode. To switch (or keep) Eternl to use single address mode is shown in Figure 5.6.

Figure 5.6: Eternl Single Address Mode

Staking

One of the most important features of the Cardano network is the ability to stake from your wallet easily. This is known as liquid, non-custodial staking, and it is why Cardano has a high level of staking on the network, which contributes to its security and decentralization. To stake, a user goes to the staking tab, chooses a stake pool, and selects “Delegate,” as shown in Figure 5.7.

Figure 5.7: Stake Pool Delegation

Next, the user must sign a transaction that registers the stake key and stake pool delegation to the network, as shown in Figure 5.8.

Figure 5.8: Stake Pool Registration

When a transaction with the stake key registration certificate and stake pool delegation certificate has been confirmed, the user will receive staking rewards as per the Ouroboros lifecycle discussed in Chapter 2, Cryptography and Consensus Algorithms Overview.

CIP-30

For a dApp to connect to a browser extension wallet such as Eternl, the dApp should follow the CIP-30 standard.6 This standard describes a webpage-based communication bridge to allow webpages to interact with the browser extension wallet. This is implemented by injecting JavaScript code into the webpage that is being rendered. The webpage or dApp can then call the JavaScript code with predefined public methods to retrieve and submit data to the wallet. In the off-chain code examples, we will see how to interact with the wallet using CIP-30 later in this chapter.

It is a fully hosted solution that can spin up a variety of Cardano environments. Cardano is fully open source, so you can install and run Cardano components locally or in a cloud environment. For this book, we will use Demeter.run to focus more on the concepts and application logic and spend less time on environment setup and configuration.

Off-Chain Examples

This section will show two approaches to building the off-chain code for a simple vesting smart contract. Each approach will use Next.js for the application framework. For more information on Next.js, the reader is encouraged to check out the Next.js documentation at https://nextjs.org/docs.

Helios

Helios is both an off-chain JavaScript/TypeScript SDK and an on-chain functional, strongly typed programming language. It prioritizes readability and ease of use for an optimal developer experience. One of the main advantages of Helios is that it is a single JavaScript file with no third-party libraries, which improves the auditability and transparency of the smart contract being executed.

Initialize

We will refer to the GitHub code examples with a fully functional end-to-end example to execute your first off-chain code. In the Visual Studio web setup in the prior section with a Demeter.run workspace, select a new Terminal window and perform the following steps:

$ cd chapter_05/helios/vesting

$ nvm install 18.17.0

$ npm install

$ npm run dev

This will start up Next.js and run on the default port of 3000. Ignore any prompts from Visual Studio about port forwarding, since we will access the port using the exposed port URL. You can go into the workspace on Demeter.run and select the URL for the exposed port to access the Next.js web application. In your browser, you should see something like Figure 5.13.

Figure 5.13: Helios Transaction Builder

Please ensure you have either Eternl (or Nami) browser wallet extensions installed on your Chrome browser before selecting a wallet. You may need to refresh the page if you just installed or enabled the browser wallet extension. On the Helios Tx Builder page, when a wallet is selected, the wallet will prompt the user to confirm if it is okay to connect to this site if has not already connected to it previously. Confirm ok, and then you will see the wallet balance of the selected wallet, as shown in Figure 5.14.

Figure 5.14: Helios Wallet Selector

Lock

In Figure 5.14, you will notice a “Lock UTXO” button at the bottom of the screen. This button will create an off-chain transaction to lock 2 Ada and a Datum at a script address. Let’s look at the code required to submit an on-chain transaction in the following steps.

Step 1

To connect to the browser wallet API, we will create a WalletConnector.tsx component within the Next.js application. In Figure 5.15, on lines 52 and 53, the code first confirms if the browser plugin exists. If yes, then it updates the walletAPI state variable on line 56 so that the rest of the application can access it.

Figure 5.15: Wallet API7

Step 2

Once we have access to the walletAPI, we can query the wallet for available UTXOs and change the address, as seen on lines 89 and 92 in Figure 5.16. Helios uses a walletHelper class that wraps the walletAPI object to provide convenience functions and automatic conversions to Helios data types.

Figure 5.16: Helios WalletHelper8

Next, we must compile the Plutus script to confirm the script address. This can be done via client-side JavaScript, as seen on line 104 in Figure 5.17. On line 107, we can see how easy it is to import the Datum structure from the on-chain code and create the appropriate Datum off-chain. Next, we construct the UTXO output on lines 113 to 117 using the script address and the amount of Lovelace (2 Ada), and attach the Datum as an inline datum.

Figure 5.17: Helios Program9

Step 3

The last step before we sign the transaction is to get the current network parameters, including the current epoch, cost model, max tx size, and more. This is needed for internal validation of the script while it is being constructed. In this example, we are simply sending (locking) Ada at the script address, so we do not need to evaluate the Plutus script. We will, however, evaluate the Plutus script in the next chapter on Validators. On line 124 in Figure 5.18, the transaction is finalized, which balances the transaction inputs and outputs and returns any change to the wallet if needed. Once the transaction has been finalized, we ask the wallet to prompt the user to sign the transaction. The fully signed transaction is submitted to the network on line 131.

Figure 5.18: Helios Submit Transaction10

The user is prompted to sign the transaction using the Eternl wallet, as shown in Figure 5.19.

Figure 5.19: Eternl Wallet Sign Transaction

After successfully signing the transaction with the browser wallet, a transaction hash (TxId) will be shown with a link to a blockchain explorer, as shown in Figure 5.20.

Figure 5.20: Transaction Successful

If we select the link to the blockchain explorer, we can see the transaction including the attached inline datum, as shown in Figure 5.21.

Figure 5.21: Blockchain Explorer11

Lucid + Aiken

Lucid + Aiken combo has the advantage of broader adoption and usage within the Cardano community. The Aiken team has also been focused on excellent developer experience and has created several utilities to help the Smart Contract developer lifecycle. This section will focus on building an off-chain transaction using Lucid to lock a UTXO at an Aiken script address.

Unlike Helios, which has the option to compile a Plutus script at run-time, Aiken follows the more typical two-phase deployment process:

  1. Compile an Aiken script using the Rust-based Aiken compiler to generate artifacts such as the compiled Plutus script and script hash into a file known as a blueprint.
  2. The Lucid off-chain code reads the compiled script in the blueprint file and any contract parameters that may be required.

Blockfrost API

In our examples, we will use Blockfrost API to interact with the Cardano Preprod network. Therefore, you must obtain an API key and set it as an environment variable.

  1. Go to https://blockfrost.io/
  2. Create a free account and create an API key for the Cardano Preprod network.

Initialize

For the Lucid off-chain code example, we will refer to the GitHub code that can be run with the following steps:

$ cd chapter_05/aiken/vesting

$ nvm install 18.17.0

$ npm install

$ echo export NEXT_PUBLIC_BLOCKFROST_API=https://cardano-preprod.blockfrost.io/api/v0 >> ~/.bashrc

$ echo export NEXT_PUBLIC_BLOCKFROST_KEY=your-blockfrost-API-key >> ~/.bashrc

$ source ~/.bashrc

$ npm run dev

This will launch Next.js and provide the same experience we saw using the Helios Transaction Builder. For this chapter, the Aiken contract has already been pre-compiled, and we will discuss the Aiken installation and build steps in Chapter 6, Validators.

Figures 5.22, 5.23, and 5.24 show the blueprint file’s contents. It describes the Aiken Validator and the datum and redeemer data types.

Figure 5.22: Aiken Blueprint Validator

Figure 5.23: Aiken Blueprint Datum Definition

Figure 5.24: Aiken Blueprint Redeemer Definition

Lucid Off-Chain Code

The WalletConnect.tsx component will get the walletAPI the same way as the Helios off-chain code example. The difference with Lucid is that it also needs to use a blockchain API provider such as Blockfrost. Once the provider has been created on lines 32-35 in Figure 5.25, the walletAPI is passed to it on line 118 in Figure 5.26.

Figure 5.25: Lucid Blockfrost Provider12

The Datum is manually constructed, and the Plutus validator script is read from the blueprint file on lines 126 and 129, respectively, in Figure 5.26. Finally, the transaction is built, signed, and submitted on lines 133, 139, and 142.

Figure 5.26: Lucid Submit Transaction13

Tip: If you only have 1 UTXO in your wallet, the balance may show 0 lovelace because your wallet has excluded it and reserved it for collateral. For Lucid, you must explicitly set collateral in your wallet. For Eternl, you can set collateral by going to the Wallet Settings tab, selecting the Collateral dropdown, and then pressing the Set Collateral button.

Conclusion

We started the chapter with a walkthrough of the transaction lifecycle and the wallets typically used in Cardano. We then proceeded into off-chain transaction building using two different approaches: Helios and Lucid + Aiken. The same user experience was created for each approach to highlight the code differences between them. We also demonstrated how useful tools like Demeter.run are used for standing up a development environment and how you register your stake to a stake pool using a wallet. Looking ahead, we are ready to review the on-chain code and execute it as part of a transaction.

Points to Remember

  • The transaction lifecycle involves building, signing, and submitting.
  • Transactions are serialized when submitted to the network using the CBOR format.
  • Wallets are the gateway to the blockchain and Smart Contracts.
  • A seed phrase is used to generate a private key.
  • A Testnet faucet is used to get test Ada (tAda) for the preview and pre-prod networks.
  • Address derivation is used to derive multiple addresses using the same private key.
  • Staking from a wallet will submit a stake registration and stake pool delegation certificate.
  • CIP-30 is a Cardano standard for connecting to browser extension wallets.
  • Demeter Run is an on-demand hosting platform for Cardano environments.
  • Helios is both an off-chain and on-chain library.
  • Helios and Lucid can be used in a client browser.
  • Blueprints are created during the Aiken build process and contain the compiled script, metadata, and type definitions for the Datum and Redeemer.

Multiple Choice Questions

  1. What is required to delegate to a stake pool?
    1. Stake pool delegation certificate
    2. Withdraw certificate
    3. Use a wallet
    4. Stake registration certificate
  2. Which statement is true regarding a receiving and a change address?
    1. Both are internal addresses
    2. One is an internal and the other is a variable
    3. One is external and the other is internal

  3. What CIP among the following is a Cardano browser wallet connection standard?
    1. CIP-22
    2. CIP-42
    3. CIP-30
    4. CIP-32

    1. Network
    2. Seed phrase
    3. Password
    4. None of the above
  5. What is the Aiken blueprint used for in Lucid?
    1. UTXOs locked at a script address
    2. Current Epoch
    3. Compiled Plutus script and parameters
    4. ADA price

  2. c
  3. c
  4. b
  5. c

Questions

  1. What are the advantages and disadvantages of Single Address Mode wallets?
  2. Can Helios or Lucid + Aiken be used in a server-side model?
  3. What other languages and frameworks can you use to write Cardano off-chain code?
  4. Why are Aiken blueprints helpful?
  5. Are seed phrases required to make a private key?

Key Terms

  • Assembled Transaction: Create a transaction with inputs, outputs, Datums, and Redeemers.
  • Account Abstraction: Create a view or model of an account with a balance and transaction history.
  • Full Node: A Cardano node and does not use any third-party service to look up transactions on the blockchain.
  • Seed Phrase: Used to help generate a private key.
  • Hierarchy for Deterministic Wallet: Used to derive multiple addresses from the same private key.
  • Single Address Mode: Reuses the same network address.
  • Auditability: The ability to confirm that the smart contract has been audited.
  • Transparency: How to ensure the smart contract being executed is the correct one.
  • WalletAPI: JavaScript object to interact with CIP-30 compliant browser wallet.
  • Blockfrost: An API service provided to query the Cardano blockchain.

CHAPTER 6

Validators

Introduction

This chapter will examine what a validator is and why they are so important. We will show how to spend UTXOs locked at a validator script address and how to access data provided in a Redeemer and Datum. Next will be a deep dive into understanding the ScriptContext and how it is used to access inputs and outputs associated with a transaction. The chapter will also review code examples using two different approaches: Helios and Lucid + Aiken.

Structure

In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:

  • Validators
  • Helios
    • Helios On-Chain Validator
    • Helios Off-Chain Unlock
    • Helios Off-Chain Cancel
  • Lucid + Aiken
    • Aiken On-Chain Validator
    • Lucid Off-Chain Unlock
    • Lucid Off-Chain Cancel

Validators

Validators are fundamental to Cardano Smart Contracts because they specify the on-chain conditions required to spend a UTXO locked at a script address. Once a validator is on-chain, it is immutable where code becomes “law”. A typical use case for validators is a vesting contract where funds are locked into a contract until the specific condition(s) are met. We will further build on our vesting example in the upcoming chapters and look at both the on-chain and off-chain codes. Validators consist of two components: the on-chain code that defines the rules and logic, and the off-chain code that creates and submits the transaction.

Helios

We first saw how to build an off-chain transaction using Helios in Chapter 5, Transaction Builder. In this chapter, we will first look at the code to build the on-chain validator and then the off-chain code required to spend a UTXO locked at the script address. The Helios language for on-chain code is a strongly typed, functional programming language with similar code quality benefits from languages like Haskell or Aiken. The Helios compiler, however, is written in JavaScript and has the unique advantage of compiling at run-time using only JavaScript. This makes the setup and installation trivial since all you need is a recent version of Node.js, which is widely available on many operating systems.

Helios On-Chain Validator

The first line of a Helios on-chain validator specifies the purpose of the Plutus script. In this case, it is a spending script, and the name of the script is called vesting. In Figure 6.1, on line 7, a Datum is defined as a struct with an owner variable using a PubKeyHash data type. This will be stored on-chain within a UTXO locked at the script address. We previously saw an example of this type of Datum in Chapter 4, Plutus, Figure 4.5. On line 15 in Figure 6.1, there is a Redeemer with an enum data type that contains two elements: Cancel and Claim. The Claim element has a msg variable that is used to pass a message to the validator at runtime.

Figure 6.1: Helios On-Chain Validator Declarations1

Note: When running locally, a Helios Visual Studio Code extension (ext:hl) is available for syntax highlighting. For Visual Studio Web, you must associate the .hl extension with C #.

On line 23 in Figure 6.2, a contract parameter MESSAGE is declared that will be hard coded into the Plutus script. A contract parameter is a UPLC value that precedes the main validator function and can be updated dynamically at compile time or run-time if required.

Figure 6.2: Helios On-Chain Validator Body2

The main validator function is defined on line 26 in Figure 6.2. We can see that three parameters, Datum, Redeemer, and ScriptContext, are passed to the main function. A validator may use none or any of these arguments depending on the validator logic. We have already discussed Datum and Redeemer in Chapter 4, Plutus, so we will look into the ScriptContext in more detail. The ScriptContext includes the script purpose and all information related to the submitted transaction, except for auxiliary data (metadata).

As we recall from Chapter 3, Transactions, a script purpose is specified in the redeemer for each UTXO locked at a script address. Therefore, the four script purposes that may be used in the ScriptContext are as follows:

  • Spending: used for validating the spending of a UTXO locked at a script address.
  • Minting: used for minting or burning native tokens.
  • Certifying: used for stake key registration and delegating stake to a stake pool.
  • Rewarding: used for withdrawing staking rewards.

In Figure 6.2, line 27, we define a local variable tx, which gets all of the transaction information from the current transaction being executed. This will then be used to extract the required signers of this transaction. Next, we use a switch statement on the Redeemer to execute different codes based on the value of the Redeemer. If the Redeemer is a Cancel, then we look at the required signatures in the transaction to see if they match the public key hash in the datum. If this is true, the validator allows the owner to do whatever he wants with this UTXO. If the Redeemer is a Claim, then we return true if the message in the redeemer matches the contract parameter MESSAGE value. If the Redeemer does not match either a Cancel or Claim or returns a false value, the validator will return false and the transaction will fail.

Helios Off-Chain Unlock

First, we will create an Unlock.tsx component that will allow the user to enter the text for the Redeemer and select a button to build the unlock transaction, as shown in Figure 6.3.

Figure 6.3: Unlock UI Component3

Then, we create the unlock function in our main index.tsx page that takes the Redeemer parameter and builds the transaction. The unlock function in Figure 6.4 is similar to the lock function, but we need to get a receiving address on line 171 to send the unlocked funds. On line 186, we attach the validator script to the transaction to be included as a script witness.

Figure 6.4: Unlock Function Vesting Program4

Next, in Figure 6.5, we need to get a UTXO locked at the script address on line 189 and construct the Redeemer on line 193. In Helios, we can simply import the Redeemer type from the on-chain code and generate the UPLC code with the _toUplcData() function. We then add the UTXO input and the Redeemer on line 198, which will be linked together as a Redeemer witness. A UTXO output on line 206 is also created for 2 Ada to be sent to the receiving address once the UTXO locked at the script address is spent.

Figure 6.5: Unlock Function Inputs and Outputs5

The getVestingUtxo function on line 189 makes a network call to a Next.js API created to query the blockchain for UTXOs at the script address. The Next.js API uses Blockfrost, a third-party Blockchain API provider, and is executed on the server. There are other providers and approaches to querying the Cardano blockchain, such as Maestro, Ogmios, and Koios. We will review other blockchain API providers in Chapter 8, Tooling. The advantage of using the Blockfrost API server-side is that it avoids API key leakage when calling the API directly from the browser. Additionally, this avoids network calls to a different domain, which can result in browser CORS errors. Figure 6.6 shows the code of the Next.js vestingUtxo API that is called by the function getVestingUtxo in index.tsx.

Figure 6.6: getVestingUtxo API6

The remainder of the unlock function is the same as the lock function, which assembles, signs, and submits the transaction to the network. The new UI with the Unlock button and the Redeemer message input is shown in Figure 6.7.

Figure 6.7: Helios Unlock UTXO

Off-Chain Cancel

We create a Cancel.tsx component and a cancel function inside index.tsx, which will be almost identical to the Lock.tsx component and the unlock function. There are only a few changes between the unlock and the cancel function. Can you spot the differences in Figure 6.8?

Figure 6.8: Helios Cancel UTXO7

One difference is that we must explicitly add the required signer for the transaction. The .addSigner() function, on line 294, will add the wallet’s public key hash to the transaction and include it as a required signer. The Cardano Node will ensure that any required signers have a corresponding valid signature in the witness set. The on-chain code then looks up the public key hash in the list of required signers to see if there is a match.

Test Drive

To run the demo, you need to execute the following commands; unless you change the version number or the vesting script, the validator script address will be the same for everyone:

$ cd chapter_06/helios/vesting

$ npm install

$ echo export BLOCKFROST_KEY=your-blockfrost-API-key >> ~/.bashrc

$ echo export NEXT_PUBLIC_ENV="dev" >> ~/.bashrc

$ echo export NEXT_PUBLIC_HOST="demeter-run-host" >> ~/.bashrc

$ echo export NEXT_PUBLIC_PROTOCOL="https" >> ~/.bashrc

$ echo export NEXT_PUBLIC_PORT="" >> ~/.bashrc

$ source ~/.bashrc

$ npm run dev

You should now be able to access the application using the exposed port URL. You can lock the 2 Ada and either claim (unlock) using the correct Redeemer value or cancel it. Figure 6.9 shows a successful transaction claiming the 2 Ada using the correct Redeemer value.

Figure 6.9: Helios Vesting Claim8

Aiken

Aiken is a functional programming language with a compiler written in Rust, and we will compile an Aiken script using the aiken build command. This generates the compiled Plutus code and a blueprint file containing metadata and type definitions. To install Aiken in your Demeter.run workspace, you first need to download the installer:

$ curl -sSfL https://install.aiken-lang.org | bash

$ echo export PATH=:/config/.aiken/bin:$PATH >> ~/.bashrc

$ source ~/.bashrc

Then, run the following command:

aikup

With aiken installed, you can use the help switch to see the following available options:

$ aiken --help

Cardano smart contract language and toolchain

Usage: aiken <COMMAND>

Commands:

new         Create a new Aiken project

fmt         Format an Aiken project

build       Build an Aiken project

address     Compute a validator's address

check       Type-check an Aiken project

docs        Build the documentation for an Aiken project

add         Add a new project package as dependency

blueprint   Commands for working with Plutus blueprints

packages    Managing project dependencies

tx          Commands for working with transactions

uplc        Commands for working with untyped Plutus-core

completion  Generates shell completion scripts

help        Print this message or the help of the given subcommand(s)

You can create a new project with the following command:

aiken new aiken-lang/new-project

This will create the following project files and folders:

└── new-project

├── aiken.toml

├── lib

│   └── new-project

├── README.md

└── validators

aiken.toml contains information about your project, including libraries and the GitHub repo. The validators folder contains the on-chain Aiken source code, and the lib directory contains any project libraries needed or created for this project. You can run a quick test to confirm everything is installed correctly by executing aiken build and aiken check inside the new-project directory.

In the examples in this book, we place all of the Aiken on-chain validator code and artifacts in the contracts directory within each chapter project(s). For example:

chapter_06/aiken/vesting/contracts

If you want to learn more about setup and installation tasks including IDE language configurations, please refer to the Aiken online documentation.9

Aiken On-Chain Validator

Now, we are ready to look at our first Aiken validator script, as shown in Figure 6.10:

Figure 6.10: Aiken On-Chain Validator Declarations10

The first 5 lines, in Figure 6.10, are the explicit import of classes we will use, followed by the Datum and Redeemer types. The Datum includes a Hash object, which contains a Blake2b_224 and VerificationKey object. It should be noted that this is stored on-chain as a single ByteArray representing the public key hash. On line 15, the Redeemer contains two conditions (Cancel and Claim) to spend the UTXOs locked at this script address. The Claim redeemer also contains a msg payload using the ByteArray data type. On line 21, we hard-coded the “Hello World!” message as a script constant.

Next, in Figure 6.11, we define the validator function on line 23 and create the vesting function with the Datum, Redeemer, and ScriptContext as arguments:

Figure 6.11: Aiken On-Chain Validator Body11

On line 26, we determine the type of Redeemer provided for a particular transaction. In the case of Cancel, the code on line 29 looks through the list of required signers and confirms if there is a public key hash matching the public key hash in the datum. If true, then the validator evaluates to true and allows the spending of the UTXO locked at the script address. If the Redeemer is a Claim, we compare the msg payload to the hard-coded message constant on line 32. If there are no matches for the Redeemer, then the validator will fail and not allow the spending of the UTXO locked at the script address.

Lucid Off-Chain Unlock

We will create the same Unlock.tsx component that we did with Helios and an unlock function in index.tsx. We need to create a Redeemer for the unlock function but don’t need to create a Datum since it is already part of the UTXO we want to spend. The Redeemer on line 172, in Figure 6.12, creates a Plutus Data object corresponding to the UPLC Datatypes we saw in Chapter 4, Plutus. In this case, we are creating a Constr at index 1, containing a hexadecimal-encoded string. When the script is evaluated, this hexadecimal-encoded string will be converted into the Redeemer Claim ByteArray payload. We specify an index of 1 because the Redeemer Cancel was the first Redeemer defined in the Plutus script with an index of 0. The next Redeemer in order will have an index of 1, the Claim Redeemer.

Figure 6.12: Lucid Off-Chain Unlock12

On line 175, the UTXOs locked at the script address are obtained by calling the lucid.utxosAt function, which uses the Blockfrost API to query the blockchain. Next, we build the transaction and specify the input UTXO on line 182 and the Validator script on line 183. The lucid transaction can now be assembled using the .complete() method. There is a lot of activity behind the scenes to assemble and finalize a transaction. We will focus on the more commonly used lucid tx methods to build and submit a transaction. Once the transaction has been finalized, the wallet will ask the user to sign it and submit it to the network.

Lucid Off-Chain Cancel

Lastly, we will create a new Cancel.tsx component and a cancel function in index.tsx. The Cancel.tsx component is almost identical to the Lock.tsx component, the main difference being the required signatures in the transaction on line 226 in Figure 6.13. This is needed because the on-chain code looks to see if the required public key hash matches the public key hash stored in the datum.

Figure 6.13: Lucid Off-Chain Cancel13

Conclusion

We looked at our first on-chain Plutus validators built using the languages Helios and Aiken. We then looked at the corresponding off-chain code to execute these validators using Helios and Lucid, respectively. We looked at how to initialize a project using Aiken and how to compile an Aiken Smart Contract. Then, the ScriptContext was examined and how it contains key transaction information such as inputs, outputs, script purpose, and more. We also looked at the Datum and Redeemer definitions on-chain and how they are accessed during script execution. The creation of an Off-chain Redeemer and required signers was also demonstrated in the examples. The next chapter will dive into Minting Policies, parameterized smart contracts, and minting native tokens.

Points to Remember

  • Helios is used for both on-chain and off-chain code.
  • Helios on-chain code can be compiled on the fly because the Helios compiler is written in JavaScript.
  • Aiken is used for on-chain code and is compiled prior to run-time using the rust-based aiken compiler.
  • Aiken also generates a blueprint file that contains Plutus script metadata and type definitions.
  • The aiken program has several convenient utility functions that may be used during smart contract development.
  • Lucid is used for the off-chain code and is frequently used in combination with Aiken.
  • Lucid and Helios off-chain code rely on a blockchain API provider to obtain blockchain information such as UTXOs locked at a script address.
  • The Helios off-chain code can import the Helios on-chain code, allowing for sharing type definitions.
  • Helios off-chain code does not require any third-party libraries and is consolidated into one JavaScript file.
  • When spending a UTXO, a Redeemer must be provided for each UTXO locked at a script address.
  • A required signer must include a signature in the witness set for a transaction.

Multiple Choice Questions

  1. What is required to spend a UTXO at a Plutus script address?
    1. Datum Only
    2. Minting Script
    3. Datum and Redeemer
    4. Epoch
  2. What activity must occur during transaction finalization?
    1. Balance the inputs and the outputs
    2. Specify the Redeemer
    3. Specify the Datum
    4. Sign the transaction
  3. How does off-chain code access blockchain data?
    1. Makes a direct RPC call to the node
    2. Usually not needed
    3. Uses an API provider (for example, Blockfrost, Ogmios, and more)
    4. CIP-30 wallet connector
  4. What is the typical sequence for on-chain code execution?
    1. Local evaluator, Node for tx submission, All blockchain nodes
    2. All blockchain nodes only
    3. Node for tx submission only
    4. Local evaluator then All blockchain nodes
  5. What are the four different script purposes?
    1. Validate, Mint, Reward, Cert
    2. Spend, Burn, Cert, Reward
    3. Spend, Mint, Burn, Context
    4. Spend, Mint, Cert, Reward

Answers

  1. c
  2. a
  3. c
  4. a
  5. d

Questions

  1. How could you improve the auditability of a smart contract that is executed?
  2. Why are most smart contracts deployed before run-time?
  3. What is the role of Datum and ScriptData hashes?
  4. Why must you always specify a redeemer when executing a smart contract?
  5. How are the Plutus cost models retrieved by off-chain frameworks such as Lucid and Helios?
  6. What is the CDDL specification identifier for the required signers of a transaction?

Key Terms

  • Auxiliary Data: Metadata about the transaction where only the metadata hash is stored on-chain.
  • Blake2b: A hash algorithm that generates a hash based on an input of bytes.
  • Blueprint: An Aiken-generated file that contains Plutus script metadata and type definitions.
  • Blockfrost: An API Provider for blockchain data.
  • ByteArray: An array of bytes encoded using a hexadecimal format.
  • Contract Parameter: A variable on-chain that can be updated during compilation or at run-time.
  • Enum: A data type that can be enumerated and used in a switch/case statement.
  • ScriptContext: Contains all information about the current transaction being evaluated except for Auxiliary Data.
  • Spending script: Type of validator for spending UTXO locked at a script address.
  • Switch Statement: Used to evaluate different Enum combinations.
  • VerificationKey: The public key of the corresponding private key.

CHAPTER 7

Minting

Introduction

In this chapter, we will examine Cardano native assets and how they are different from tokens on Ethereum created using the ERC-20 and ERC-721 standards. We will discuss the concept of token bundles and how to optimize coin selection. Next, we will deep dive into how Cardano native tokens are minted and the differences between Non-Fungible Tokens (NFTs) and Fungible Tokens (FTs). We will also review the NFT and FT metadata standards CIP-25 and CIP-68 and how to implement them when minting. Finally, we will introduce the Cardano Token Registry and explore its benefits for the end-user wallet experience. We will put this all together with a working minting policy use case and demo.

Structure

In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:

  • Tokens
    • Native Assets
    • Token Bundles
  • Minting Policy
    • Fungible Tokens
    • Non-Fungible Tokens
    • Token Registry
    • NFT Metadata Standards
      • CIP-25
      • CIP-68
  • Coin Selection
    • Minting Policy Use Case

Tokens

On March 1st, 2021, as part of the Mary hard fork, Cardano was upgraded to a multi-asset ledger. This was a significant event because custom native tokens can now leverage the secure, peer-reviewed, ledger rules used for Ada, Cardano’s native currency. This is much different than in Ethereum, which requires a custom ledger code for each token following either the ERC-20 or ERC-721 standards. Additionally, there are no requirements for special fees or additional event-handling logic to track transactions associated with a Cardano native asset.1

Native Assets

A Native asset is a custom token on Cardano that operates in the same manner as the base layer currency, Ada. The only requirement is that a native asset must always have a minimum amount of Ada included. CIP-552 simplified the minimum Ada calculation and used the number of CBOR bytes of a UTXO, which includes all of the native assets locked in a particular UTXO.

Assets represent value such as 1 Ada (1,000,000 lovelace) or 100 gold coins. Each asset token is always made of three components*:

  • Policy Identifier (ID): A unique identifier that is the hash of the Minting Policy Plutus script.
  • Asset Name: An immutable token name on-chain for a specific minting policy.
  • Quantity: The accounting unit.

* Ada is the only asset token where the Policy ID is an empty string.

Token Bundles

A token bundle is a collection of tokens that may be the same or different from each other. Token bundles organize tokens into a particular structure that properly defines the Minting Policy ID, Token Name, and quantity of each of the tokens in a collection. Let’s look at the following example:

{

"lovelace": "1646420",

"assets": {

"Game Monetary Policy ID": {

"Gold": "100",

"Silver": "1,000",

"Bronze": "10,000",

},

"Game Character Policy ID": {

"Dracula": "1"

}

}

In the preceding example, we have a collection of tokens that each represents value locked at a UTXO. The Game Monetary Policy ID is a unique Minting Policy ID, and the player holds a collection of 100 Gold, 1,000 Silver, and 10,000 Bronze tokens. So, a user would be able to spend up to 100 Gold, up to 1,000 Silver, and up to 10,000 Bronze tokens in a single transaction. Since this player only has 1 Dracula token, they can only send (spend) 1 Dracula token to another player. There always needs to be a minimum amount of Ada in a token bundle, and in this case, it is 1,646,420 lovelace.

Minting Policy

There are two different types of minting policies on Cardano:

  • A native script minting policy that is created using the cardano-cli tool and uses a private and public key.
  • A minting policy that is a plutus script.

The cardano-cli approach3 was first used before smart contracts were introduced, but this approach is still used today because it is a proven and secure technique. Using cardano-cli with native script provides the ability to specify time-locks and multi-signature conditions.4

A minting policy is written in a similar way to how we would write a validator script. The difference between a validator script and a minting policy is that a minting policy uses a minting script purpose and does not use a datum. The minting script purpose allows the minting policy to mint or burn native tokens as part of a transaction. Only the conditions to mint or burn are defined in the custom logic of a minting policy.

Fungible Tokens

You may want to create a monetary policy with a fixed supply of 1,000,000 tokens. The minting policy would only allow a one-time minting of 1,000,000 tokens, with each token having the same name. This allows each to token be fungible or tradable with each other, similar to how fiat currency operates today.

Non-Fungible Tokens

A Non-Fungible Token (NFT) is where each token is unique and distinct. There are two approaches to making a unique token on Cardano.

  • Option 1: Time-lock, where a token can only be minted before a specific date and time.
  • Option 2: Minting Policy logic that checks for the existence of a UTXO in the input that has to be spent.

The second option is preferred because it leverages the unique properties of the Cardano UTXO architecture. Once a UTXO is spent, it can never be spent again, and there will not be another UTXO with the same hash. By hard coding this condition into the minting policy and also ensuring that only one token is minted, we guarantee that the token is unique. A time-lock approach was used before smart contract functionality was available, but this method is still used when creating large NFT collections.

Token Registry

The Token Registry5 is used to store off-chain metadata for a minting policy ID and token name. A minting policy ID is a hexadecimal hash value, so it is helpful to have a mapping of human-readable information about a specific Minting Policy and token. The registry provides an API that wallets and other applications can use to look up a token image URL, decimal precision, and other metadata. With the adoption of CIP-68 increasing, the need for a centralized off-chain token registry will be phased out over time. For a step-by-step guide to register a token, please visit the following URL:

https://github.com/cardano-foundation/cardano-token-registry/wiki

NFT Metadata Standards

When minting an NFT, you can take advantage of transaction metadata that can be included in any transaction on the Cardano network. The first Cardano metadata standard was documented in CIP-256, which specifies the standard fields for NFT metadata. A notable difference between the Ethereum ERC-721 token metadata is that CIP-25 metadata is linked to the minting transaction and not the minting policy. So, the process to look up the metadata using CIP-25 is to look at the original minting transaction for a particular minting policy.

CIP-25

The format for CIP-25 NFT metadata is illustrated in Figure 7.1:

Figure 7.1: CIP-25 NFT Metadata Format

The label 721 indicates that this is token metadata, and the format is flexible, allowing you to add additional elements as required. To test out and verify valid transaction metadata in production, you can use a tool like Pool.pm test metadata tool.7

CIP-68

The disadvantage of CIP-25 is that it is created with the original Mint transaction and is not updateable or accessible to a Plutus script. CIP-688 addresses this by using the concept of a reference token that is created at the same time when an NFT or FT is created. The reference token can be either locked at an unspendable script address to be immutable, or locked at a spending script address if there is a requirement for it to be updateable. The metadata is stored in a datum included with the reference token UTXO output. Therefore, it can be accessible to a Plutus script by reading the datum values. With the Vasil hard fork in September 2022, reference inputs were introduced that allowed a Plutus script to include a UTXO input as read-only and not have to spend it. Therefore, any number of transactions (even in parallel) can include a read-only CIP-68 reference token UTXO without any concurrency issues.

In Figure 7.2, we can see how a mint transaction generates a user token and a reference token, which are linked using the same token name and minting policy ID. The token name is prefixed with 222 to indicate an NFT token, and 100 to indicate a reference token. For fungible tokens, the prefix of 333 is used. It should be noted that the reference token and the NFT or FT tokens do not need to be minted at the same time.

Figure 7.2: CIP-68 Datum Metadata

In Chapter 9, Ticket Application Design, we will discuss the process of creating and using CIP-68 metadata datum in more detail, with a real-world example.

Coin Selection

Coin selection is the process of determining what UTXO inputs are required to cover the cost of the UTXO outputs, including network fees. There are a number of constraints that we need to be aware of during the coin selection process, which are as follows:9

  • The maximum size limit of a transaction is where each UTXO, either as an input or an output, increases the overall transaction size.
  • The accumulation of very small UTXOs (dust) that are unspendable because you can’t combine enough of them together to cover the transaction cost.
  • A UTXO cannot be partially spent; it must be fully spent as part of a transaction.

Therefore, the goal of a coin selection algorithm is to avoid dust and create usable UTXOs. Coin selection algorithm considerations apply to both Ada and native tokens. The most common approaches for coin selection are Largest-first and Random Improve.10 Largest-first selects UTXO inputs in the order from the largest value to the smallest value. The Random Improve algorithm uses two phases. The first phase cycles through a descending ordered list of UTXO inputs, and randomly selects UTXOs until the required amount of output value is obtained. In the second phase, the algorithm again chooses randomly from the descending order list of UTXO inputs until it is approximately twice the amount of output value. Coin selection is typically implemented by the wallet and/or the transaction builder framework. That said, it is important to understand the coin selection algorithms when selecting UTXO inputs. Also, you need to be aware that users may run into issues related to dust or UTXO fragmentation caused by too many UTXOs required as inputs.

Minting Policy Use Case

Next, we are going to look at a real-world use case for minting on the Cardano blockchain. In this example, we will use a minting policy to create a fixed number of tickets for an event.

Minting event ticket tokens have the following blockchain advantages, outlined in Table 7.1:

Blockchain Feature

Who

Benefit

Number of tickets minted is fixed and cannot be changed

Performer

Knows exactly how many tickets were issued for a particular event

Attendee

The value of a ticket cannot be diluted by additional tickets being created after the initial allocation

Minting and Cardano ledger rules

Attendee

If an attendee has a valid ticket, then this is a genuine ticket and cannot be fake

Seller

For secondary market sales, the ticket can be verified by examining the blockchain to confirm that it is a genuine ticket

Performer

The movement of tickets is recorded on the blockchain so it is easy to audit how many tickets have actually been sold or canceled (burned)

Table 7.1: Minting Use Case Benefits

Helios On-Chain Minting Policy

The on-chain Helios minting policy on line 1, Figure 7.3, states the purpose of the script as minting and the name of the script called ticket. There are two Redeemer conditions, Mint and Burn, on lines 7 and 8. The contract parameters on lines 11–16 will be updated dynamically at run-time. So, for now, they are initialized with dummy values and will be updated in the off-chain code.

Figure 7.3: Helios On-Chain Minting Redeemer and Parameters11

Next, in Figure 7.4, we can see the main function contains the minting policy logic. First, note that only a Redeemer and ScriptContext are passed as arguments. On line 20, we create the MintingPolicyHash dynamically, which takes into account the contract parameters that have been passed into the script at run-time. We then create an AssetClass that will be used to verify what is minted. On line 25, we pull out the minted value that was part of this transaction. Now, we have the information we need to handle the Redeemer conditions.

Figure 7.4: Helios On-Chain Minting Logic12

On line 30, in Figure 7.4, we confirm that the token minted in the transaction matches the token name and amount in the contract parameters. Lines 31 and 32 check that the UTXO specified in the contract parameter is included in the inputs. Since a UTXO can only be spent once, the minting policy will only allow it to be minted once, which guarantees uniqueness. Finally, on line 38, we allow the burning as long as the token belongs to the minting policy and has a negative quantity.

Helios Off-Chain Mint

We will highlight the differences between the off-chain code in this example and the previous validator example was shown in Chapter 6, Validators. On line 119 in Figure 7.5, we create the minting policy program and then apply the contract parameters on lines 120–123. Only after the parameters have been applied, can we compile the script and attach it to the transaction on line 129. We also construct the Mint Redeemer on line 132, which uses the Redeemer type specified in the on-chain code.

Figure 7.5: Helios Off-Chain Contract Parameters13

In Figure 7.6, an array of tokens on line 136 uses the ticket name and qty passed in from the user input to create the assets that we will mint, as shown on lines 139–141. Next, we specify the tokens to mint in the transaction on line 145. We use the MintingPolicyHash from the compiled script and include both the tokens and the Redeemer created previously. We also create an output, where we send the minted ticket tokens to an address from the user input, as shown on lines 153–156.

Figure 7.6: Helios Off-Chain Mint Tokens14

In this minting use case, we also make use of transaction metadata following the CIP-25 standard. On line 160 in Figure 7.7, we make a call to generateMetadata, which returns a JSON-like record for the data passed in the arguments. The name, qty, utxoId, and utxoIdx will be stored in the metadata and can be used to reconstruct the minting policy later if burning is required. The mintingPolicyHash, name, description, and image are used off-chain for displaying the token in wallets, dApps and blockchain explorers.

Figure 7.7: Helios Off-Chain Metadata15

Helios Off-Chain Burn

There may be a scenario requiring the burning of unsold ticket tokens. Since the Cardano ledger tracks and avoids any double spending of native assets, the burning of ticket tokens can only be done if the ticket tokens actually exist in the transaction inputs. Therefore, the only way to burn a token is to actually have possession of that token in your wallet, so it can be spent as a transaction input.

We will first look at line 230 in Figure 7.8, which queries the blockchain to get the mint metadata for a particular token. As we recall, we need the token name, qty, utxoId, and utxoIdx as contract parameters to rebuild the correct minting policy. Now that we have the contract parameters, we can compile the script and attach it to the transaction. We also create a Burn Redeemer on line 246 that allows the burning of the ticket tokens accordingly.

Figure 7.8: Helios Off-Chain Metadata Query16

Lastly, we build the MintingPolicyHash from the policyId stored in the metadata and then add the token to burn by using a negative qty number on line 252, as shown in Figure 7.9.

Figure 7.9: Helios Off-Chain Burn Tokens17

Aiken On-Chain Minting Policy

The Aiken on-chain minting policy uses token_name, qty, and utxo_ref as contract parameters that are applied during run-time on line 15, as shown in Figure 7.10. Only the Redeemer and ScriptContext are passed to the ticket function, which contains the minting policy logic. On line 18, the expect statement is used to create a new binding for policy_id from the script purpose. On line 19, we deconstruct the Transaction type and pull out both the inputs and the minted value from the transaction. We then use the expect statement on line 22 to bind asset_name and amount with a tuple from the mint value and match the policy_id. Now we have all the information we need to execute the Redeemer conditions. On lines 31 and 32, we check that the input contains the utxo_ref that was passed in the contract parameter. On line 34, we confirm that the asset name and amount match what was provided as contract parameters. The Burn Redeemer on line 37 allows the burning of any negative amount as long as the asset_name within this script minting policy is included in the transaction.

Figure 7.10: Aiken On-Chain Minting Policy18

Lucid Off-Chain Mint

The Lucid off-chain code is very similar to the Helios off-chain code, and we will only show the differences between the two approaches. In particular, the way contract parameters are applied is different, as shown in Figure 7.11. On line 89, a custom function takes in the contract parameters and then applies them to the Plutus minting policy script on line 96. The Plutus script is read from the blueprint file, which was generated from the aiken build command.

Figure 7.11: Lucid Applied Contract Parameters19

In Figure 7.12, the validator (minting policy) is returned on line 132 using the name, qty, and outRef calculated from the UTXO in the wallet. On line 135, the Redeemer is manually constructed to match the Redeemer in the on-chain Aiken code. The policyId and assetName are constructed using the validator Script and name, respectively, on lines 138 and 139.

Figure 7.12: Lucid Minting Policy ID and Asset Name20

The last step is to generate the metadata on line 142, in Figure 7.13, and build the transaction on lines 150–160. The validator script, metadata, and minted assets are attached to the transaction on lines 153, 154, and 155 accordingly. Finally, the output is created to send the ticket tokens to the address provided by the user input on line 159.

Figure 7.13: Lucid Minting Metadata21

The Lucid-off chain Burn is similar to Mint, except that mintAssets() uses a negative quantity and has no metadata or transaction output.

Setup

To set up the Demeter.run environment to run the Lucid + Aiken demo, please execute the following commands in a terminal window (if you have not already done so from Chapter 6, Validators).

$ echo export NEXT_PUBLIC_BLOCKFROST_API=https://cardano-preprod.blockfrost.io/api/v0 >> ~/.bashrc

$ echo export NEXT_PUBLIC_BLOCKFROST_KEY=your-blockfrost-API-key >> ~/.bashrc

$ echo export NEXT_PUBLIC_ENV="dev" >> ~/.bashrc

$ echo export NEXT_PUBLIC_HOST="demeter-run-host" >> ~/.bashrc

$ echo export NEXT_PUBLIC_PROTOCOL="https" >> ~/.bashrc

$ echo export NEXT_PUBLIC_PORT="" >> ~/.bashrc

$ source ~/.bashrc

$ npm install

$ npm run dev

Conclusion

We reviewed what a Cardano native asset is and how it is different from an Ethereum ERC-20 token. We explored the concept of token bundles and how they are used to group a collection of tokens together inside a UTXO. Next, we understood the role of the minting policy and how to mint Cardano native asset tokens. The differences between Non-Fungible Tokens (NFTs) and Fungible Tokens (FTs) were discussed, where NFTs represent a unique asset and FTs represent assets that are identical to each other. The CIP-25 and CIP-68 metadata standards were also reviewed with examples of what CIP-25 metadata looks like. We briefly discussed the role of the Token Registry, as well as the importance of using an optimal coin selection algorithm. We finished up the chapter by looking at a real-world use case involving minting ticket tokens for an event. The next chapter will focus on Cardano tooling used for blockchain development, queries, and monitoring.

Points to Remember

  • Cardano native assets use the Cardano ledger to prevent double spending of tokens.
  • A minting policy hash will be unique if a UTXO is used as a contract parameter and is spent in the mint transaction.
  • A token bundle includes one or more tokens and must always include a minimum amount of Ada.
  • Tokens are included in a UTXO and can be locked at a Base, Enterprise, or Plutus Script address.
  • A minting policy only controls the minting or burning of tokens.
  • A minting policy does not manage the ledger state of the token or the circulating supply.
  • An off-chain code is required to determine the circulating supply by examining all the mint and burn operations for a specific minting policy.
  • Non-fungible tokens (NFTs) are unique and distinct assets that are not interchangeable with each other.
  • Fungible Tokens (FTs) are identical and can be exchanged with each other.
  • CIP-25 is a metadata standard that is used when submitting a transaction.
  • CIP-68 uses a reference token and a datum value that can be either immutable or updateable, depending on the use case.
  • The Token Registry registers a minting policy and token name to provide metadata about the token, which is used by wallets, blockchain explorers, and exchanges.
  • Using a proper coin selection algorithm is important to avoid dust, which is a small amount of UTXOs that cannot be spent because the cost of doing so exceeds their combined value.

Multiple Choice Questions

  1. Which of the following statements is false?
    1. Cardano native tokens are identical to ERC-20 Tokens
    2. Cardano native assets use the base ledger to avoid double spending
    3. A Cardano minting policy typically uses less code than an ERC-20 contract
    4. A Cardano minting policy is typically more secure than an ERC-20 contract
  2. How does a one-time Minting Policy create the initial circulating supply?
    1. Custom code inside the minting policy
    2. Queries the Cardano ledger
    3. Queries the minting policy metadata
    4. None of the above
  3. How is CIP-25 metadata stored on-chain?
    1. As a hash value on-chain and included as auxiliary data as part of a transaction
    2. In JSON format and stored fully on-chain
    3. Nothing is stored on-chain
    4. Content is stored in a UTXO datum
  4. How do you specify the precision units of a Fungible Token (FT)?
    1. Only possible using CIP-25
    2. In the Token Registry or CIP-68
    3. Inside the minting policy
    4. None of the above
  5. What is UTXO fragmentation?
    1. There are too many UTXOs on the blockchain
    2. There are too many UTXO needed as inputs to satisfy the outputs
    3. There are too many UTXO outputs
    4. There are too many assets locked into one UTXO

Answers

  1. a
  2. a
  3. a
  4. b
  5. b

Questions

  1. What are some other possible use cases for minting on Cardano?
  2. In what situations would you use CIP-25 or CIP-68?
  3. What are the ways to make a Minting Policy ID or Token Name unique?
  4. For a given native token, is it possible to know the current Address where the UTXO is locked at and how can this be done?
  5. Can you describe other coin selection algorithms besides Largest-first and Random Improve?

Key Terms

  • Asset Name: An immutable token name on-chain of a specific minting policy.
  • CIP-25: Metadata standard using transaction metadata.
  • CIP-68: Metadata standard using a reference token and datum.
  • Collection: The grouping of tokens together in a token bundle.
  • Dust: A very small value UTXO that cannot be spent.
  • ERC-20: Ethereum FT token standard
  • ERC-721: Ethereum NFT token standard
  • Monetary Policy: Specifies the amount of tokens in circulation and the inflation or deflation rate.
  • Policy Identifier (ID): A unique identifier that is the hash of a Minting Policy script.
  • Quantity: The accounting unit.
  • Reference Inputs: Read-only UTXOs that are included as transaction inputs but cannot be spent.

CHAPTER 8

Tooling

Introduction

Developing smart contracts often involves working with code that exists outside the blockchain (off-chain) and integrating it seamlessly with the Cardano network. In this chapter, we will review some of the tools and service providers you can use to help accomplish these tasks. First, we will delve into Cardano Blockchain API providers such as Blockfrost, Maestro, Koios, and Dandelion. Next, we will explore hosted environments with Demeter.run to set up services like Cardano Node, Ogmios, and Kupo. Finally, we will learn how to set up a local Devnet for rapid development and prototyping with the Yaci DevKit.

Structure

In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:

  • Blockchain API/Monitoring Providers
    • Blockfrost
    • Maestro
    • Koios
    • Dandelion
  • Tooling Components
    • Cardano-node
    • Ogmios
    • Kupo
    • Local Devnet
    • Additional Resources

Blockchain API/Monitoring Providers

The examples throughout this book demonstrate the use of Blockfrost API to query the blockchain and submit transactions. In this section, however, we will look at additional widely used blockchain service providers and tooling that help avoid a centralized point of failure within the Cardano ecosystem.

Blockfrost

Blockfrost (https://blockfrost.io/) was one of the first providers in Cardano and has earned the trust of a large user base with over 100M+ daily requests and 20K+ developers.1 Blockfrost has excellent API documentation and a wide range of services, from simple blockchain queries to webhook callbacks for your application. They have free entry-level plans that allow you to test out APIs for your application. You can make HTTP REST calls to their API or use their convenient SDKs for Node.js, Python, Rust, C#, Go, and more. Blockfrost supports Mainnet, Preprod, and Preview networks, and you can run your own instance from their GitHub Repo.2

Maestro

On July 19, 2023, Maestro (https://www.gomaestro.org/) released V1 of their dApp platform with new features that significantly improved Cardano blockchain integration:3

  • Turbo Transactions: Accelerate transaction finality with Maestro’s transaction propagation system.
  • New Blockchain Indexer: High performance, low latency, optimized Cardano blockchain indexer with convenient and efficient APIs.
  • Transaction Manager: A transaction monitoring system with automatic retries and zero-delay pending, on-chain, and rollback notifications.4

Code Example

In our vesting and ticket examples, we change the Lucid provider in pages/index.tsx to use Maestro, which is included in the Lucid library, as shown in Figure 8.1.

Figure 8.1: Lucid Maestro Provider5

For the ticket minting example, we also needed to change the blockchain query in getTicketMetadata.ts in the pages/api folder. We use the Node.js API provided by Maestro, as shown in Figure 8.2.

Figure 8.2: Maestro TypeScript API6

In Figure 8.2, on line 18, we create the Maestro client and then call maestroClient.assets on line 27 to get the metadata for the asset.

Setup

To set up the Demeter.run environment to run the Maestro demo, execute the following commands in a terminal window:

$ export NEXT_PUBLIC_MAESTRO_API_KEY=your-maestro-key-here

$ export NEXT_PUBLIC_ENV="dev"

$ export NEXT_PUBLIC_HOST="your-demeter-run-host"

$ export NEXT_PUBLIC_PROTOCOL="https"

$ export NEXT_PUBLIC_PORT=""

Koios

Koios7 is a distributed, open-source, RESTful APIs for querying and submitting to the Cardano blockchain. It is built and maintained by the highly respected Cardano Stakepool Guild Operators.8 Koios promotes decentralization by using distributed processing architecture, a core value of Cardano. A typescript Koios provider client can also be used directly from the Helios library.

Dandelion

Dandelion9 is a collection of Cardano Blockchain APIs as a free community service. The services include:

  • GraphQL10
  • Ogmios11
  • Cardano DB Sync/Postgres API 12
  • Rosetta API13

Tooling Components

One of the major benefits of Cardano is that the code is open source, and you have the ability to download, build, and run all the required components yourself. Or, you can use a third-party provider to manage and host these components. We have already seen how to set up an environment for the examples in this book with Demeter.run, but they offer many other Cardano components and services. We will look at some of the more popular components used for Cardano blockchain integration.

Tooling components are used to query, react, or transact with a blockchain. A typical, off-chain tooling setup is illustrated in Figure 8.3, where Cardano DB Sync processes blocks from a Cardano Node and stores data in a Postgres Database. An application can then query the database or react to blockchain events accordingly.

Figure 8.3: DB Sync Off-Chain Indexing

The challenge with DB Sync is that it is difficult to set up and requires significant hardware resources for Mainnet. Fortunately, Demeter.run provides hosted instances that you can easily deploy with no manual configuration. This is great for networks like Mainnet or Preprod but not well-suited for custom networks or local testing environments.

Cardano Node

The core component of the Cardano blockchain is the Cardano Node and the source code is located on GitHub.14 You can download and compile the source code, run the static binaries, or use docker containers. Demeter.run also has an extension to provision a Cardano Node as follows:

  1. Go to https://demeter.run/
  2. Choose the project you want to deploy to. We will enable this extension for a workspace already created in Chapter 5, Transaction Builder.

  3. Once the workspace is provisioned in Figure 8.4, you must enable the Cardano Node by selecting the Connected Extensions by scrolling down the page.

    Figure 8.4: Demeter.run Cardano Workspace

  4. Select Browse Extensions.
  5. Find the Nodes box, select Deploy, and then Provision Resource.
  6. Scroll down, select Preprod, and select the Create Resource button at the bottom of the page.

  7. When you go back to the Project Overview you should see the Node extension connected, as shown in Figure 8.5.

    Figure 8.5: Demeter.run Cardano Node Connected

  8. Select the workspace you previously provisioned.
  9. Select the Visual Studio Code icon on the workspace to launch the Visual Studio web client.
  10. From the hamburger menu, select Terminal -> New.

To verify that your cardano-node is connected to the network, type the following command and you should see syncProgress at 100%.

~/workspace/repo$ cardano-cli query tip --testnet-magic 1

{

"block": 1595041,

"epoch": 106,

“era”: “Babbage”,

"hash": "ed3be308f9538fe59241f5d90b333de06ddc581ee783ba0002d0c911b3b

df3f6",

"slot": 44370786,

"slotInEpoch": 220386,

"slotsToEpochEnd": 211614,

"syncProgress": "100.00"

}

Most of the tooling that connects to the Cardano Node uses a socket and you can find out the location of the socket by printing out the shell environment variable:

~/workspace/repo$ echo $CARDANO_NODE_SOCKET_PATH

/ipc/node.socket

The cardano-cli is the Swiss army knife of Cardano and there are lots of resources on its usage and would suggest starting here.15 Type cardano-cli to see the following list of top-level common options. Use the --help flag to get additional usage info for each command.

~/workspace/repo$ cardano-cli
