Ogmios

Ogmios16 is one of the most widely used and popular tools for Cardano developers, and it has excellent documentation. It is a translation layer that exposes the Cardano node capability via a REST API or Web RPC interface. Ogmios is open source and can be downloaded, built, and installed locally from GitHub,17 or by a docker container. We will deploy the hosted version using Demeter.run, so we can quickly demonstrate how to interact with it using our examples.

To add an Ogmios extension:

  1. Go to the Project page, scroll down, and select BROWSE EXTENSIONS, as shown in Figure 8.6.

    Figure 8.6: Demeter.run Project View

  2. Select Ogmios Deploy.
  3. Select Create Resource and the Preprod network.
  4. Select v5 as the version and the CREATE RESOURCE button.  

  5. You see the instance running, as shown in Figure 8.7.

    Figure 8.7: Demeter.run Ogmios Live

Now, let’s try out the Ogmios API using a web tool like Hoppscotch.18

  1. Go to the URL: https://hoppscotch.io/realtime/websocket.
  2. Paste in the public https hostname for Ogmios and prefix wss:// to the address.
  3. Select Connect.

  4. Then paste the following query and select Send:

    {

    "type": "jsonwsp/request",

    "version": "1.0",

    "servicename": "ogmios",

    "methodname": "Query",

    "args": {

    "query": "ledgerTip"

    }

    }

  5. You should see the successful result, as shown in Figure 8.8.

    Figure 8.8: Hoppscotch Ogmios Success

Kupo

Kupo19 is an open-source Cardano blockchain indexer that is fast, lightweight, and configurable. The API documentation20 is excellent and is the go-to choice for many Cardano development teams who prefer to use in-house tools. Blockchains are designed to be a sequence of blocks connected together, forming a chain. This, however, makes it very difficult to perform efficient data queries. This is why we have indexers that take in blockchain data and store it in a database. This data can be indexed and then queried, filtered, and analyzed. Kupo can be downloaded, built, and installed locally, but it can also be set up as a hosted service using Demeter.run.

We will again go to the Project page and add another resource, this time a Kupo resource. Select deploy and create the resource for the preprod network. Next, copy the https URL for future reference. We should see four resources: Workspace, Node, Ogmios, and Kupo, as shown in Figure 8.9.

Figure 8.9: Demeter.run Resources

To do a quick test to confirm Kupo is working correctly, you can execute the following command and replace the hostname with the hostname copied from the Demeter.run Kupo resource.

$ curl -fsSL https://dmtr_kupo12e…mph005.preprod-v2.kupo-m1.demeter.run/health

# TYPE kupo_configuration_indexes gauge

kupo_configuration_indexes  1.0

# TYPE kupo_connection_status gauge

kupo_connection_status  1.0

# TYPE kupo_most_recent_checkpoint counter

kupo_most_recent_checkpoint  59235759

# TYPE kupo_most_recent_node_tip counter

kupo_most_recent_node_tip  59235759

In this case, the Kupo instance is healthy and you can see the most recent checkpoint and node tip. Checkpoints are blocks on-chain that have been processed by Kupo and are mainly used to synchronize with the blockchain after a restart.21

Code Example

Now, we are ready to update the vesting and ticket code examples to use Ogmios and Kupo together. Lucid has a provider we can use for this exact purpose, called kupmios, as shown in Figure 8.10.

Figure 8.10: Lucid Kupmios22

The vesting example will work without any other changes by switching to the Kupmios provider. The ticket example, however, requires changes to the pages/API getTicketMetadata.ts, as shown in Figure 8.11.

Figure 8.11: Kupo getMetadata23

To retrieve metadata from Kupo, first get the transaction associated with the mint transaction. The token asset is provided on line 70, and the results are sorted by the order of the transactions that occurred on the blockchain. Therefore, the mint is the first transaction, so use the first transaction ID and then look up the metadata on that specific transaction on line 73. Lines 76–81 parse the metadata object and return the results to the front end.

Setup

To set up the Demeter.run environment to run the Kupo + Ogmios demo, please execute the following commands in a terminal window.

$ export NEXT_PUBLIC_KUPO_API=https://dmtr_kupo12e…mph005.preprod-v2.kupo-m1.demeter.run

$ export NEXT_PUBLIC_OGMIOS_API=wss://dmtr_ogmios1v…g0t6mk.preprod-v5.ogmios-m1.demeter.run

$ export NEXT_PUBLIC_ENV="dev"

$ export NEXT_PUBLIC_HOST="your-demeter-run-host"

$ export NEXT_PUBLIC_PROTOCOL="https"

$ export NEXT_PUBLIC_PORT=""

Local Devnet

Thanks to the awesome work from BoxBean24; it is now easy to install a local Devnet, similar to Hardhat for Ethereum. The Yaci Devkit uses lightweight components for indexing and querying blockchain data. In particular, BoxBean created Yaci, a modular, Java-based Ouroboros min-protocol that listens to incoming blocks, monitors the local mempool, and submits transactions.25 The team also created Yaci Store, a Java-based solution that provides an API to query indexed data and handle rollbacks.26 To enable integration with a browser extension wallet like Nami and Helios for off-chain transaction building, the local Devnet will also use a local Cardano DB Sync to feed blockchain data to a local Blockfrost API provider.

The following five steps are required to set up a local devnet:

  1. Set up YaciDevkit27
  2. Set up Cardano DB Sync
  3. Set up Blockfrost backend RYO (Run Your Own)
  4. Build and install Nami browser wallet with a local Testnet network
  5. Top up wallet with funds

Environment

The following setup instructions have been tested on Ubuntu Linux 20.04.1. You may require additional setup for other operating systems.

Install Docker

$ sudo apt -y install apt-transport-https ca-certificates curl

software-properties-common

$ curl -fsSL https://download.docker.com/linux/ubuntu/gpg | sudo gpg --dearmor -o /usr/share/keyrings/docker-archive-keyring.gpg

$ echo "deb [arch=amd64 signed-by=/usr/share/keyrings/docker-archive-keyring.gpg] https://download.docker.com/linux/ubuntu $(lsb_

release -cs) stable" | sudo tee /etc/apt/sources.list.d/docker.list > /dev/null

$ sudo apt update

$ sudo apt -y install docker-ce docker-ce-cli containerd.io

$ sudo usermod -aG docker $USER

$ newgrp docker

$ docker run hello-world

Step 1: Setting up Yaci Devkit

Once docker is installed, you can cd into the yaci-devit directory, so that we can install the following two docker images.28 We will install the following images as part of the setup process:

$ git clone https://github.com/ava-orange-education/Mastering-Cardano-Smart-Contracts.git --recurse-submodules

$ cd Mastering-Cardano-Smart-Contracts/chapter_08/local-devnet/yaci

$ sudo ./yaci-cli.sh

Step 3: Starting The Blockfrost-RYO

In another new terminal window:

$ cd chapter_08/local-devnet/blockfrost-backend-ryo

$ nvm install 20.9.0.

$ yarn install

$ yarn run dev-testnet

Step 4: Installing the Nami Local Devnet Version

Open up another terminal window and go into the Nami directory.

$ cd chapter_08/local-devnet/nami

$ nvm use 20.9.0

$ npm install

$ npm run build

This will create a build directory that can be loaded into the Chrome browser. Open a Chrome browser tab extension window chrome://extensions/ and select “Load Unpacked” at the top left side of the window. Then navigate to the nami/build directory that was just created and select it. The Nami wallet should now be installed, and you can launch the browser extension. It will first ask you to create a new wallet and a passphrase. You will need to switch to the Testnet network and can do that by selecting the avatar at the top right of the window. Next, select Settings, Network, and choose Testnet from the Network dropdown menu, as shown in Figure 8.12. Also, enable Custom Node and enter http://localhost:8090/api/submit/tx as the URL. You can confirm you are on the correct network by seeing the Testnet label on the bottom left of the Nami pop-up window.

Figure 8.12: Nami Wallet Devnet

Tip: If you installed the Nami wallet using the unpack method and closed down the browser, you may need to follow the unpack instructions again. If you have also re-created the new local Devnet network, then you will need to create a new Nami account or explicitly remove the Nami wallet browser plugin to clear cached Nami account data.

Step 5: Top Up Wallet with Funds

With the Nami wallet open, select the receive button, and copy the receiving address. Then, go into the yaci-cli terminal, type the topup command, and paste the receiving address and the amount of Ada to send, as shown in Figure 8.13.

Figure 8.13: Yaci DevKit Top-up

You can confirm in your Nami wallet that you have received this amount, as presented in Figure 8.14.

Figure 8.14: Nami Wallet Top-up

Conclusion

This chapter provided an overview of some of the more popular Cardano off-chain providers and tooling available to developers. First, we explored Cardano Blockchain providers such as Blockfrost, Maestro, Koios, and Dandelion. Next, we understood some of the Cardano infrastructure components and tooling, such as Cardano Node, Ogmios, and Kupo, and how to provision them with Demeter.run. We also learned how to deploy a local devnet using Yaci DevKit to test both the on-chain and off-chain code. Finally, we provided an overview of the most popular tooling components for completeness. We are now ready for the next chapter to use our knowledge of off-chain tooling to build a complete end-to-end blockchain application.

Points to Remember

  • Multiple blockchain API service providers and tooling options help promote decentralization and avoid a single point of failure in the ecosystem.
  • Blockfrost was one of the first Cardano API service providers and is the most used API service provider in the Cardano ecosystem.
  • Maestro recently launched a new version of its APIs with low latency, high-performance chain index queries, transaction state management, and optimized transaction submission.
  • Koios is a decentralized, distributed platform that provides Cardano Blockchain API services.
  • Off-chain tooling components are used to query, react, or submit blockchain data.
  • Cardano Node can be run from a docker container, static binaries, or by compiling the source code.
  • Demeter.run has resources such as Cardano Node that can be run and attached to a workspace
  • cardano-cli is the Swiss army knife of Cardano with multiple uses and functionality
  • Ogmios is popular because it exposes Cardano node functionality using REST APIs or a Web RPC interface.
  • Kupo is also a popular open-source Cardano Blockchain indexer that captures and saves blockchain data quickly to an in-memory database.
  • Lucid has a built-in Kupo and Ogmios provider that can connect to a Kupo and Ogmios instance for blockchain data lookups and transaction submissions.
  • Yaci DevKit can set up a local devnet similar to Hardhat for Ethereum.

Multiple Choice Questions

  1. Why is blockchain tooling needed?
    1. Used by on-chain in validator logic
    2. Used by the Cardano Ledger
    3. Needed to start/stop the Cardano Blockchain
    4. Access blockchain data efficiently
  2. What functionality cannot be done using cardano-cli?
    1. Start/Stop a cardano node
    2. Submit a transaction
    3. Query the Cardano Blockchain tip
    4. See the contents of a UTXO
  3. How do you access Ogmios?
    1. Node socket
    2. SQL query
    3. HTTP REST API
    4. HTTP REST API or RPC Web Socket
  4. What are some of the features of the Yaci Devkit?
    1. A blockchain explorer, Kupo and Ogmios APIs
    2. Token registry
    3. Mainnet network
    4. GraphQL
  5. Which tool can submit a transaction?
    1. Kupo
    2. Oura
    3. Carp
    4. ouroboros-network-js

Answers

  1. d
  2. a
  3. d
  4. a
  5. d

Questions

  1. What are the centralization risks to any blockchain?
  2. Why is blockchain technology generally not efficient for direct data access?
  3. How does tooling speed up access to data queries?
  4. What did Maestro do to help improve the finality times for a submitted transaction?
  5. What are some of the advantages of using a local Testnet or devnet?

Key Terms

  • Node Tip: The latest (highest) confirmed block in the blockchain.
  • RESTful: It uses HTTP requests to access and set data between a client and server.
  • SDK: Software Development Kit makes it more convenient for developers to access service providers’ APIs.
  • Service Provider: A company that provides off-chain blockchain API services.
  • Transaction Finality: The time it takes to finalize a transaction on the blockchain.
  • Webhook: A callback function that will make a network call to an application when a certain condition is met.

CHAPTER 9

Ticket Application Design

Introduction

This chapter will discuss Smart Contract design patterns that leverage and utilize the Cardano UTXO architecture. Anti-patterns are also examined which should be avoided and lead to poor application performance and UTXO contention. We will discuss Cardano Improvement Proposals (CIPs) and where they will be applied to the application design. Next, we will examine a state machine diagram to see how the entire event ticket lifecycle works. Then we will explore state transitions looking at the inputs and outputs required for a successful transaction. Finally, we will summarize the chapter by reviewing sequence diagrams showing each use case’s interaction between system components.

Structure

In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:

  • Design Patterns
    • Thread Token
    • Beacon Token
    • Order Book
  • Anti-Patterns
    • Liquidity Pools
    • Batchers
  • Cardano Improvement Proposals
    • CIP-0003 Wallet Key Generation
    • CIP-0030 Cardano dApp-Wallet Web Bridge
    • CIP-0032 Inline Datums
    • CIP-0040 Explicit Collateral Output
    • CIP-0068 Datum Metadata Standard
  • The Application Architecture
    • On-Chain State Machines
    • Off-Chain Sequence Diagrams

Design Patterns

By now, you are aware of the significant differences between a UTXO and an account model blockchain. In particular, many account-based blockchain design patterns are not optimal for UTXO blockchains and are called anti-patterns. Let’s first examine common UTXO design patterns and then contrast them with anti-patterns.

Thread Token

One of the first and most common design patterns involves using a thread token. A unique token is minted and included in a UTXO locked at a script address. The script can only be spent if the transaction includes this thread token, as shown in Figure 9.1. It is critical that this token is unique, so it is created using a minting policy that requires a specific UTXO as an input. By leveraging the underlying uniqueness of UTXOs, it can only be spent once, and we can guarantee that the thread token will be unique.

Figure 9.1: Thread Token Example

A thread token can be used to control the sequence of validator script execution. Every transaction must include the thread token, guaranteeing that only one transaction can be executed at any time. This is useful if you are trying to update some type of state and need the validator to ensure the correct before and after state transitions (refer to Figure 9.1). This is also useful to confirm preconditions such as whether a token has been minted in a separate transaction. By using a thread token, Smart Contracts can communicate with each other using the existence (or absence) of a particular token in a transaction.

Beacon Token

A Beacon Token1 is used to easily identify the address of a specific UTXO that contains a Beacon Token. There are many examples where UTXOs are created, and the off-chain application needs to keep track of these UTXOs. As we saw in Chapter 8, Tooling, chain indexers can capture the minting transaction information and record the UTXO and address containing a token. The indexers can also monitor token movement and be able to provide the current address where a token resides. This is useful if you need to provide a list of UTXOs that match a specific Beacon Token. A Beacon Token can also be used as a Thread Token, combining both the Thread Token and Beacon Token design patterns.

Order Book

To take advantage of the EUTXO model for concurrency and scalability, you need to design your application, which will scale as the number of users grows. Fortunately, the EUTXO architecture can facilitate this because it uses local state and is fully deterministic. This means that multiple concurrent users can interact with your application, each with their own UTXOs that are independent of each other. Therefore, as more users use the application, there is no centralized access that will limit throughput as in global state systems. Additionally, local state is well suited for creating state channels and zero-knowledge proofs to segment a set of UTXOs for scaling solutions.2 We will discuss scaling solutions such as Hydra in Chapter 11, Testing, Security, and Scaling.

An Order Book design is an example of leveraging EUTXO architecture’s benefits. In particular, the design involves a three-step process:

Step 1: Place an order on-chain with a UTXO locked at a contract script address

Step 2: Identify or monitor the orders off-chain for a match

Step 3: Submit a transaction to fill (or cancel) the order

In a simplified example, as shown in Figure 9.2, a swap script address uses a parameter to record the asked asset, the offered asset, Beacon Token and the public key hash (PKH) of the user wallet. This creates a unique swap address for each asset pair. The PKH is used, so only the wallet that placed the order can cancel it. A unique Beacon Token is attached to each order UTXO so that off-chain code can easily find all swaps when looking for order matches.

Figure 9.2: Simplified Order Book Example

Alice and Bob have positions that can be swapped without any third-party intermediary. A swap position can be partially filled, or swap positions can be aggregated together to fill multiple swaps. This is known as composability, and teams like Cardano Swaps3 advocate order book design standards to enable swap composability across the Cardano ecosystem.

In Figure 9.2, Alice offers 100 Ada for 50 WMT tokens, and Bob offers 50 WMT tokens for 100 Ada. This valid swap is detected off-chain, and a Fill Order transaction is submitted on-chain to execute the swap. Once the Fill Order is completed, the Ada and WMT tokens in the swaps are transferred to each party. Bob gets 100 Ada, and Alice receives 50 WMT tokens. Charlie decides to cancel his swap position without any third-party intermediary and is allowed to do so because he can sign the transaction with the required public key hash (PKH).

The Order Book design pattern works well because of the following Cardano EUTXO architecture principles4:

  • Local state
  • Independent and unique wallet UTXOs and smart contracts
  • Immutable UTXOs
  • Determinism
  • On-chain verification and Off-chain computation
  • Parallel transaction processing

An Order Book design can be applied to many different use cases that involve exchanging value between two or more parties.

Anti-Patterns

Now that we have looked at design patterns that take advantage of EUTXO architecture, let’s look at some design patterns we should avoid. Most of the anti-patterns are design approaches that come from an account model such as Ethereum.

Liquidity Pools

The concept of a centralized liquidity pool was introduced, where users can pool their liquidity in trading pairs. This would create an automated market maker (AMM) for market orders, as shown in Figure 9.3.

Figure 9.3: Liquidity Pools

The disadvantage of this approach is that liquidity is aggregated into a centralized pool and typically involves custodial ownership. It means that someone else has the keys to spend your funds and you no longer have direct control over them. This also makes for an excellent attack vector, which is why we saw over $3.1 billion USD stolen in 2022 from DeFi protocols.5 In this model, we must also introduce Liquidity Provider (LP) pools where LP tokens are staked to incentivize liquidity providers. This is not required in an order book design pattern and adds complexity. Finally, the negative side effect of impermanent loss only occurs with centralized AMM pools. This worsens the more significant change (+/-) in the price of an asset pair being provided as liquidity.6

Batchers

To make matters worse, when this design pattern is used in an EUTXO architecture, the centralized liquidity pools create UTXO contention and scalability is impacted as more users are added. To mitigate this, another anti-pattern involves Batchers processing an order on the user’s behalf, as shown in Figure 9.4.

Figure 9.4: Batchers

Batchers, unfortunately, introduces centralization where typically only approved entities can run a Batcher and add liquidity to a pool or execute a swap. This can also lead to front-running transactions to the detriment of users trying to get the best price for a swap. On Ethereum, this is known as miner extractable value (MEV).

Cardano Improvement Proposals

Cardano Improvement Proposals (CIPs)7 are standards or references for design and implementation best practices. We will review the following CIPs and how they apply to our application design.

CIP-0003 Wallet Key Generation

In our ticket application design, the application will need to sign a transaction in addition to the signatures from the end user. For an application to sign a transaction, we must create a private and public key following the standards defined in CIP-0003.8 CIP-0003 references BIP-39 which uses a mnemonic seed phrase to generate a wallet and is used for entropy. The CIP also references HD Wallets already covered in Chapter 3, Transactions.

CIP-0030 Cardano dApp-Wallet Web Bridge

CIP-00309 is a standard for browser-based, wallet integration. It specifies the JavaScript APIs that are injected into the browser window and how a web application can interact with them. We will use CIP-0030 in our application design, but it should be noted that there is a newer standard under review that allows for P2P wallet communication and is documented in CIP-0045.10

CIP-0032 Inline Datums

CIP-003211 improved Cardano Smart Contract development and efficiency. Before CIP-0032, only a Datum hash could be stored on-chain, not the actual Datum values. This meant that an application would have to recreate the Datum value, which made it challenging for other Smart Contracts to use it. With CIP-0032, the Datum value can reside on-chain so applications can simply reference the on-chain value. The Datum value is locked inside a UTXO, which Smart Contracts can access as part of a transaction. Combining CIP-0032 and CIP-0031 (reference inputs) allows for read-only data values on-chain and is frequently used by Oracle data providers today.

CIP-0040 Explicit Collateral Output

A new type of output, called Collateral Outputs, was introduced with CIP-0040.12 Collateral, as we recall from Chapter 4, Plutus, is required as a deposit that is used if a Plutus script phase two evaluation fails. Although this helps to protect the network against spam or malicious attacks, it also introduces an additional Ada requirement to submit a transaction. Using collateral outputs, a transaction can be constructed so that we can use any UTXO as collateral, but only consume the deposit amount if needed and return the change as a collateral return output. Before CIP-0040, a pure UTXO with only Ada could only be used as the collateral input.

CIP-0068 Datum Metadata Standard

CIP-006813 is a standard that defines a way to store token metadata using a datum. This is different from CIP-0025, which stores token metadata using transaction metadata. The advantage of using a Datum is that it can be updated and read directly by a smart contract. This standard applies to Non-Fungible Tokens (NFTs) or Fungible Tokens (FTs). CIP-0068 involves creating a reference token linked to the original token(s) minted, as shown in Figure 9.5. This design allows the NFTs or FTs to move around on the blockchain, but the reference token remains in one location.

Figure 9.5: CIP-0068

The token naming convention is to prefix hex-encoded labels such as 222 for NFTs and 100 for the reference token. The common labels to prefix are listed in Table 9.1.

Label

Class

Description

Hex Encoded Label

100

NFT

Reference Token located at reference token address

000643b0

222

NFT

Non-Fungible Token at wallet or script address

000de140

333

FT

Fungible Token at wallet or script address

0014df10

444

RFT

Rich-Fungible Token at wallet or script address. This can be used to represent fractional NFT ownership

001BC280

Table 9.1: CIP-0068 Labels14

The token name must be identical, allowing the two tokens to be linked to or reference each other. For more detailed information and with basic examples, please review CIP-0068 on GitHub.15

The steps to create your own label are16:

  1. Convert the decimal label to hex17
  2. Get the checksum of the hex18
  3. Drop the 0x and use the last 2 digits
  4. Concat the 4-digit hex and the 2-digit checksum, then add a zero to the start and end

The Application Architecture

Using our knowledge of EUTXO design patterns and standards, we are now ready to create the application architecture for a ticket application where users can mint, burn, buy, sell and redeem event tickets.

On-Chain State Machine Diagrams

The complete state machine diagram for the ticket token lifecycle is shown in Figure 9.6. We will look at how the application transitions to each state in detail.

Figure 9.6: Ticket State Machine

Minting Tickets

Tickets for an event must be first minted and sent to the holding validator script, as shown in Figure 9.6. The Ticket Minting Policy in Figure 9.7, shows the script parameters that will be used including the UTXO input, name of event, show time, number of tickets minted, stake and payment PKH, holding validator address, and the minimum amount of lovelace. The ticket holding script requires the stake key signature to move the ticket tokens locked at this script address. Transaction metadata is created to record the minting policy script parameters which can be used for future minting policy-related transactions (for example, burn or convert). The payment PKH will be used for the payment address when the ticket is sold (converted).

Figure 9.7: Mint Tickets Transaction

Burning Tickets

The minting policy will allow the burn of any token that belongs to the minting policy, as shown in Figure 9.8. Unsold tickets may be burned to reduce the supply of event tickets. The mint is a one-time event, but the burn can happen any time after the mint. Typically, however, an event organizer will not want to burn purchased/converted ticket tokens, which could be used for rewards, special access to content, or future events.

Figure 9.8: Burn Ticket Transaction

Opening Swap

Opening a swap requires executing both a spending and a minting script within the same transaction. In this case, we must execute Transfer Tickets, as detailed in Figure 9.9, and Mint Beacon outlined in Figure 9.10. The price of the swap is specified in the Datum and is updateable. For example, if the askedAsset is set to 10 Ada, the swap can only be executed if 10 Ada is paid for each ticket token.

Figure 9.9: Transfer Tickets Transaction

As we recall from the previous section, the Beacon Token is used to easily find off-chain the swap script UTXOs. The Beacon Token minting policy outlined in Figure 9.10 will be the same for all Beacons related to the marketplace owner, but each Beacon token name will be linked to a unique UTXO that is spent as part of the mint transaction. This is how we ensure the uniqueness of each Beacon Token. The stake PKH used to mint the event tickets must also sign the transaction when transferring ticket tokens to open a swap position.

Figure 9.10: Mint Beacon Transaction

Updating Swap

The user can update a swap position at any time if the following two conditions are true:

  • The stake PKH of the wallet matches the stake PKH in the swap contract parameter.
  • The Beacon Token specified as in the swap contract parameter is always returned to the script address.

If both conditions are true, then the smart contract will allow an update to the price, as shown in Figure 9.11. The types of assets being swapped, however, cannot be changed since this is hardcoded as a contract parameter.

Figure 9.11: Update Swap Transaction

Executing Swap

Anyone can execute a swap provided that the price of askedAsset is met. The amount of offeredAsset (ticket tokens) available to purchase will be the amount of the ticket tokens locked in the swap UTXO, as shown in Figure 9.12. The swap validator will also ensure that the Beacon Token is always sent back to the validator script address, the seller receives the correct amount of askedAsset and the buyer receives the correct amount of offeredAsset (ticket tokens).

Figure 9.12: Execute Swap Transaction

During the swap Execute transaction, we must also convert the ticket token into a unique ticket token that can be marked used when entering the venue. We do this by both burning the original ticket token and minting a new one within the same transaction, as illustrated in Figure 9.13. The Reference Token Datum value is initially set to an empty string but will be populated with the payment PKH when the user prints their ticket.

Figure 9.13: Convert Ticket Transaction

Closing Swap

A swap can be closed anytime by the seller, as shown in Figure 9.14.

Figure 9.14: Close Swap Transaction

The ticket tokens must be returned to the token holding script address, defined as a swap script parameter. The Burn Beacon transaction detailed in Figure 9.15 must also be included in the Close Swap transaction.

Figure 9.15: Burn Beacon Token Transaction

Print Ticket

To access an event, the user must prove that they, in fact, own the ticket token. This is done by using the Print Ticket transaction that will update the reference token datum with the spending PKH signed as part of the transaction, as shown in Figure 9.16. The application will create a QR code that can be shown later which includes a digital signature of the transaction.

Figure 9.16: Print Ticket

Off-Chain Sequence Diagrams

The next section will review the high-level, happy path sequence diagrams that describe how different components within the application interact. Transactions are denoted by [], and any values passed between components, if shown, are denoted by a ().

Minting Tickets

Minting event tickets using the application is shown in Figure 9.17. The first step is to get a list of UTXOs from the wallet and then build the Mint Ticket transaction. The user will sign the transaction with both their spending key to pay for the transaction costs and the staking key to prove they actually own the staking key. Once the transaction has been successfully submitted, the application database is updated to link the stake key with the new minting policy ID. An Event record is also created to show the new event to the user in the application.

Figure 9.17: Mint Tickets

Connected Wallet

When a user connects with their wallet, they can see the list of events they have created, as shown in Figure 9.18. Additional security measures could be implemented such as requiring the user to sign with their stake key to see the list of events.

Figure 9.18: Connect Wallet

Opening Swap

After the tickets have been minted, the user can then decide to open a swap position and sell tickets, as shown in Figure 9.19. The first step is to determine what event Minting Policy Hashes (MPHs) belong to the user. Next, we rebuild the Ticket Holding validator to get the script address and UTXO. We then build the Transfer Ticket and Mint Beacon transaction using the wallet UTXOs, Ticket Holding UTXO, Ticket Holding, and Beacon Minting Policy scripts. The application must also sign the transaction with the owner PKH to be able to mint the beacon token. This is not required, but allows the application owner, if they need to, control who gets to sell tickets on the application. This could be used for a paywall, KYC, or fraud prevention.

Figure 9.19: Open Swap

Viewing Swaps

All open swaps created by the application are available by querying the blockchain with the Beacon minting policy, as illustrated in Figure 9.20. The Beacon minting policy is derived from the application owner’s public key hash, so anyone can query it and rebuild the minting policy script. Blockfrost will return all assets that belong to the beacon minting policy ID, which corresponds to all of the open swap positions. For our application, however, we have stored event and swap information in a database to reduce the latency and costs of each request. So, in our case, we will query the database directly to get the list of swaps, but remember that the blockchain is always the source of truth.

Figure 9.20: View Swaps

When using data replication in a local database, we need to make sure that we receive updates from the blockchain so we have current and accurate information, as shown in Figure 9.21.

Figure 9.21: Blockchain Swap Updates

Updating Swap

The user can, at any time, update a swap position to change the price. They would select a swap from their own swaps, and update it with the new price (refer to Figure 9.22). The Beacon token for a swap is used to find the UTXO (and metadata) for a particular swap. Using the wallet UTXOs, swap UTXOS, swap metadata, and the swap script, the transaction can be built and submitted.

Figure 9.22: Update Swap

Executing Swap

A buyer can now go to the ticket application and view all available tickets for sale. If the price being paid is what is being asked for, and there is sufficient quantity for the order, then the swap will go through, as shown in Figure 9.23. Using the wallet UTXOs and the swap UTXO, the value paid for the ticket is sent directly to the seller’s address and the buyer receives the ticket token(s). During the ticket conversion, a reference token is created for each unique ticket token minted. This reference token can only be updated by the application and contains a flag to indicate if a token has been used or not at an event.

Figure 9.23: Execute Swap

Closing Swap

A seller will want to close a swap when either the amount of tickets in the swap is zero, or if they no longer want to make the tickets available for sale (refer to Figure 9.24). The Beacon asset is used to get the swap UTXO needed to build the swap transaction and send the ticket tokens (if any) back to the ticket holding address. The Beacon token is also burned as part of the close transaction, so the swap will no longer appear in the list of available swaps.

Figure 9.24: Close Swap

Print Ticket

The ticket application is also used to redeem event tickets to access an event. A user will show a QR code that verifies ownership and this can be done any time before (or at) the event. The QR code is a digital signature that is generated when they have signed a transaction with a wallet that has the ticket token. During the Print Ticket transaction, the reference token datum is updated with the wallet spending PKH. This is then used at the event to validate the spending PKH with the QR code shown. After verifying the QR code at the event, the ticket will be marked used in the application DB, as shown in Figure 9.25.

Figure 9.25: Show Ticket

Verifying Ticket

QR code generation can be done in advance. This avoids having the user bring their wallet to the event venue and submit blockchain transactions at the time of entry. Since the reference token datum may be updated with a payment PKH if printed, it is important that the ticket is verified before any secondary transfer. The application has an API that allows for querying the blockchain to confirm if a ticket has been shown or if it has been used to access an event, as shown in Figure 9.26.

Figure 9.26: Verify Ticket

Conclusion

In this chapter, we understood the Thread Token, Beacon Token, and Order Book design patterns and how they leverage the benefits of EUTXO architecture. We looked at anti-patterns such as pools and batchers that typically lead to sub-optimal designs on UTXO blockchains such as Cardano. We explored several Cardano Improvement Proposals (CIPs) and how they relate to our application design. In particular, CIP-0068 was reviewed, as to how it provides a different way to store token metadata using datums. We then started to work on the design for the ticket application using the design patterns and standards reviewed in this chapter. Next, we created state transition diagrams with inputs and outputs for each use case. Then, we created sequence diagrams showing the interactions between system components. We are ready to implement the ticket application in the next chapter, Chapter 10, Ticket Application Implementation.

Points to Remember

  • Account model design patterns are generally not well suited for an EUTXO architecture.
  • The Thread Token design pattern uses a unique token to help isolate a specific UTXO locked at a script address.
  • A Beacon Token design pattern uses a unique token to easily identify a particular UTXO off-chain.
  • A Beacon Token can be used for both the Thread Token and Beacon Token design patterns.
  • The Order Book design pattern is well suited for the EUTXO architecture because UTXOs are immutable, unique, and independent of each other.
  • Identifying atomic swap pairs can be done using off-chain computation.
  • A Swap is executed on-chain and does not involve any third party intermediary.
  • Atomic swaps retain full self-custody and can be canceled at any time.
  • An Order Book design pattern can be applied to many different use cases
  • Liquidity Pools and Batches are examples of anti-patterns with sub-optimal performance and scalability
  • The CIP-0068 Metadata standard uses Datums to store metadata for NFTs or FTs which can be updateable.
  • The Mint Ticket transaction uses CIP-0025 transaction metadata to publish the minting script parameters.
  • The Open Swap transaction needs to include both Transfer Ticket (ticket.hl) and Mint Beacon (beacon.hl) in the same transaction.
  • The Execute Swap transaction needs to include both Execute Swap (swap.hl) and Convert Token (ticket.hl) in the same transaction.
  • Convert Ticket uses CIP-0068 with a Datum value to save a payment PKH for event ticket verification.
  • Update Swap and Close Swap can only be done by the wallet that created the swap and can be done at any time.
  • When printing a ticket for an event, the user must sign a transaction with the wallet’s public key to prove ownership of the ticket token.

Multiple Choice Questions

  1. How can a Plutus script ensure a correct state transition?
    1. Use an on-chain oracle
    2. Use off-chain metadata
    3. Use a Thread Token
    4. Use a Reference Script
  2. Why is EUTXO architecture well suited for Order Books?
    1. Uses an account model
    2. Uses local state
    3. Uses global state
    4. UTXO contention
  3. What CIP allows a Smart Contract access to metadata?
    1. CIP-0068
    2. CIP-0031
    3. CIP-0025
    4. CIP-0033
  4. How are Order Book swaps usually identified?
    1. Thread Token
    2. Metadata
    3. Beacon Token
    4. UTXO
  5. Why must a user sign a transaction to verify ticket token ownership?
    1. Prove the ticket token is unique
    2. Show that the ticket has not already been used
    3. Prove that the ticket is located at the spending address of the wallet
    4. To prevent the ticket from being burned

Answers

  1. c
  2. b
  3. a
  4. c
  5. c

Questions

  1. What are some of the disadvantages of the Order Book design pattern?
  2. When do you want to use Reference Scripts?
  3. What was the motivation behind CIP-0068?
  4. How do we avoid double spending when processing tickets at an event?
  5. What will happen to the application if a blockchain rollback occurs?

Key Terms

  • Anti-Patterns: Design patterns that should be avoided.
  • FT: Fungible Token
  • KYC: Know Your Customer
  • MPH: Minting Policy Hash
  • NFT: Non-Fungible Token
  • PKH: Public Key Hash
  • P2P: Peer-to-Peer communication
  • State Channel: Segmenting a set of UTXOs to be processed off-chain chain that mirrors the same protocol as the base layer.
  • State Machine: Describes system state transition lifecycles.
  • UTXO: Unspent Transaction Outputs
  • UTXO Contention: A concurrency issue with more than one transaction is trying to spend a UTXO input.
  • Zero Knowledge: Uses cryptography to prove a claim without revealing the details of the claim.
