CHAPTER 10

Ticket Application Implementation

Introduction

In this chapter, we will walk through the implementation of the ticket application, including blockchain integration. We will start with an overview of the system components and application stack, which include CharkraUI, Next.js, Redis DB, and Blockfrost. Next, we will look at the front-end and off-chain code that is used to build the transactions that we defined in Chapter 9, Ticket Application Design. Then, we will examine the corresponding Smart Contract on-chain code, business logic, and validation rules. We will look at the back-end components, such as the Redis DB schema set up to cache and query relevant blockchain data. We will also look at the application APIs and Blockfrost webhooks that are called when a blockchain event occurs. Finally, we will run through an end-to-end demo of the application.

Structure

In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:

  • Application Stack
  • Application Resources
  • Front-end Components
  • Smart Contracts
  • Back-end Components
  • Application Demo
  • Blockchain Benefits

Application Stack

In our ticketing application, we need the ability to search and filter on an arbitrary number of swaps that contain tickets for sale. This can be done efficiently if we store the swap details in an application database with indexes for quick access. The source of truth, however, will always be the blockchain, and the database is simply a cached copy of the blockchain data. By introducing data replication, we must ensure that the database is updated accordingly when a transaction is submitted to the blockchain. Figure 10.1 shows how swap searches are queried against the local application database, which is continually updated as new relevant blockchain data arrives via a Blockfrost webhook.

Figure 10.1: System Components

Next.js will be used as the application server framework for building the html and JavaScript components. The application will also use a Redis DB with a full stack JSON and search modules for fast, in-memory database storage and retrieval. For improved error handling and troubleshooting, all transactions will be submitted using the Blockfrost API. This approach allows us to easily change the blockchain API service provider if required to do so. We will also use Blockfrost webhooks to monitor the blockchain for transaction confirmations that involve a swap created by the end user.

Application Resources

The application uses the Next.js directory structure, as shown in Figure 10.2.

Figure 10.2: Next.js Directory Structure

Common

The common folder contains client-side JavaScript code that may be used by one or more UI components. The following files are found in this directory:

  • metadata.ts: used for creating transaction metadata
  • network.ts: used for interacting with network APIs
  • orders.ts: used for order calculations
  • types.ts: defines custom data types
  • utils.ts: includes utility functions

Components

All React functional components are located in this folder, which are used to build out the application web pages or popup windows.

Config

This folder defines the application settings, which include the list of supported wallets, network, theme settings, and compilation flags, if applicable.

Init

The init folder contains a command line script for generating the application owner’s public and private keys. The owner public key is used when minting the beacon token so that we can identify all swaps created by this application instance.

Lib

The lib folder contains JavaScript libraries needed by the Redis connection library.

Node Modules

This folder stores all of the npm node modules and is created automatically when the npm install command is executed.

Pages

The pages folder contains all of the web pages and popup windows that are used in the application.

Pages / API

The api folder includes all of the application API interfaces called by client-side JavaScript and any webhooks called by Blockfrost. All client-side JavaScript network calls are made through these application APIs to avoid browser CORS errors and API key leakage.

Public

The public folder contains all static, publicly available resources such as icons and images.

Public/Contracts

The contracts folder contains all of the Smart Contract on-chain code. In particular, it contains all the business logic for the Smart Contracts that we need for this application:

  • beacon.hl: Smart Contract for the beacon minting policy
  • holding.hl: Smart Contract for the ticket holding validator
  • swap.hl: Smart Contract for all swap operations (open, update, close, and execute)
  • ticket.hl: Smart Contract for the ticket minting policy

Schema

The schema folder contains the database schemas used to define the data structures that will be stored in the Redis database. These include the following:

  • beacon.ts: This schema is used to store and keep track of beacons created by the application so they can be retrieved easily.
  • event.ts: The event schema is used to store event information locally that is linked to the stake key hash of the user.
  • stake.ts: The stake schema maps all of the event assets created to a user’s wallet stake key.
  • swap.ts: The swap schema stores swap information so that the swap can be quickly retrieved, filtered, and shown to the user.

Front-end Application

The full source code is located on the GitHub repository, so this section will look at only code fragments to highlight the key aspects of the application. The application is a single-page application with React functional components, as shown in Figure 10.3. Chakra UI was chosen for its ease of use and out-of-the-box components and is used for all of the styles and front-end UI components. For more information on Chakra, please see their documentation.1

Figure 10.3: React Functional Components

The main entry point to the application is located in pages/index.tsx with a tab navigation containing the Get Tickets, My Events, and My Tickets views. The index.tsx page also contains the following global state variable information:

  • walletAPI: The walletAPI JavaScript object for the browser extension wallet.
  • walletInfo lists the wallet base address, stake address, lovelace balance, and event assets associated with this wallet.
  • swapViewRefresh will trigger a useEffect to refresh the list of swaps to show to the user. This is called when a change is made to a swap such as opening, updating, or closing a swap.

Header and Footer

The Header and Footer components are included in the components/layout.tsx file and contain static information only.

Wallet Connector

The wallet connector is a key component and contains the logic to connect to the walletAPI object and saves the wallet information to a useState variable in pages/index.tsx. The walletAPI and walletInfo variables are made available to all other components by passing them as props to every child and sub-child component. We build upon the same code and logic for connecting to a user wallet as discussed in Chapter 5, Transaction Builder. To improve the user experience, a list of installed and supported browser wallets is presented to the user to select from, as shown in Figure 10.4.

Figure 10.4: Get Available Wallets2

There also exists a Wallet Connect3 component created by the Cardano Foundation. However, due to overlap and duplication with the off-chain transaction builder frameworks, we will not be using it.

Swap View

The swap view component obtains a list of swaps by calling getFilterSwaps() on line 43 in Figure 10.5. Filters will only be applied if they are set by the user using the Filter Button on the Get Tickets tab. SWR4 is used to cache the list of Swaps unless the getFilterSwaps() returns a different result.

Figure 10.5: Get FilterSwaps5

The array of swaps returned populates each swap card and paginates the view if necessary. Filter parameters are defined as state variables in the SwapView component and applied to the database query. The list of filter parameters is shown in Figure 10.6.

Figure 10.6: Filter Parameters6

The API call to pages/api/getFilterSwaps.ts first creates a record object of the key/value filter parameters, as shown in Figure 10.7.

Figure 10.7: getFilterSwaps API7

The record object is then passed to the dynamicQuery() function in Figure 10.8, which dynamically constructs the Redis database query and returns a filtered list of swaps.

Figure 10.8: Redis Database Dynamic Query8

Swap Card

Each Swap Card is passed the swap, walletAPI, walletInfo, and setSwapViewRefresh as props, as shown in Figure 10.9. These variables and functions display the Swap Card and are also passed to the ExecuteSwap, UpdateSwap, or Close Swap popup windows to build and submit a transaction.

Figure 10.9: SwapCardProps Interface9

To execute a swap, a user selects the “Buy” button that changes the buySwapOpen variable to true. When this occurs, the ExecuteSwapPopup page component is shown to the user (see Figure 10.10).

Figure 10.10: Execute Swap Popup Component10

Event View

The layout of the event page is similar to the swap view but requires the user to connect their wallet. Each event is linked to the wallet’s stake key, so we must connect the wallet first before creating a new event or seeing events already created.

Event Card

The Event View contains Event Cards for each event linked to the connected wallet. The user can either sell tickets or archive the event. It also displays event statistics, such as the number of tickets allocated, released, and converted. All of this information is available on the blockchain, but we also update the database directly to provide faster, real-time updates within the application.

My Tickets

On the My Tickets screen, the user is able to see the tickets that they currently have in their browser wallet with relevant metadata information such as event image, name, location, and showtime. This is also where the user can print each ticket and view the verification QR code, as shown in Figure 10.11. The NFT event tickets are Cardano native tokens, so they can also be viewed directly within the wallet or transferred to any other wallet or dApp, just like Ada.

Figure 10.11: My Tickets View

Print Tickets

When the Print Ticket Button is selected, a Print Ticket popup window will appear and allow the user to generate the QR code. Once the transaction is signed and the reference ticket CIP-68 metadata is updated, the QR code will be displayed. The signing of the transaction proves that they control the private key corresponding to the public key that the ticket asset is locked at. When the QR code is presented to the venue, the unique ticket will be marked used in the application DB to prevent double-redeeming of the ticket.

The Verify Button in Figure 10.11 can be used to simulate a ticket redemption and granting access to an event. The QR code can be generated and saved in advance, which avoids having the user bring their wallet to the event venue and submitting a blockchain transaction at the time of entry. If the event ticket token is subsequently transferred to another person, the reference token datum history should be checked to confirm that it has not already been printed (or redeemed). The QR code is a string that includes the ticket asset name (minting policy hash + token name), the transaction hash, and the witness signature for the transaction that was signed and submitted, as shown in Figure 10.12.

Figure 10.12: QR Code Generation11

Transaction Builder

The off-chain transaction builder code for swap operations such as open, update, execute, and close is done inside a popup window. When a popup window is enabled, a useEffect is triggered to call the appropriate function to build the off-chain transactions, as shown in Figure 10.13.

Figure 10.13: Execute Swap useEffect12

The off-chain code follows a similar pattern to the off-chain builder code in previous chapters. For an Execute Swap transaction, we will apply the contract parameters to build the Plutus script. If the swap validation is successful, we use the ticket minting policy to convert each ticket. Following the CIP-68 Metadata NFT standard, both the NFT token and the reference token are minted. On lines 224–227 in Figure 10.14, both the NFT tokens and the reference tokens are added to arrays that contain the total number of tokens to mint.

Figure 10.14: Convert Tickets13

During the swap transaction, we update the datum and create the outputs that will satisfy the business logic in the swap validator. This will ensure that the asking price and the amount of offered assets are correct. The calcOrderDetails() function determines the values that must be sent to the buyer and seller and returned to the swap, as shown in Figure 10.15.

Figure 10.15: Calc Order Details14

In the ticket application, we also introduce the concept of time to ensure that a swap transaction is executed within a specific period of time. It is best practice to explicitly set the time interval, which must be set when checking for time constraints within a Plutus script. Figure 10.16 shows a typical off-chain validity interval.

Figure 10.16: Time Validity Interval15

Once a validity interval has been set, a Plutus script can determine if a transaction occurs before, after, or during a specified time interval. In the next section, we will look at the on-chain code since we need to ensure the swap does not execute after a specific deadline (for example, showtime).

If the transaction is successfully built, signed, and submitted then we update the application database to increment the ticket converted amount and decrement the offered quantity in the swap. We will also include the transaction ID and set the confirmation flag to false on lines 481–482 in Figure 10.17. There will be a Blockfrost webhook to make a callback to the application after 3 block confirmations and to set the confirm flag to true. Finally, a call to handleClose() on line 493 is used to force a refresh of the swaps and events in the Swap View and Event View, respectively.

Figure 10.17: Swap and Event DB Updates16

Smart Contracts

The ticket application on-chain code involves the following Plutus scripts:

  • Beacon Minting Policy
  • Ticket Minting Policy
  • Holding Validator
  • Swap Validator

Beacon Minting Policy

The beacon on-chain code is passed a transaction ID and index via the Redeemer to create a unique token name so we can easily locate all swaps. This is a different approach than what we saw in Chapter 7, Minting, where we used the transaction ID and index as contract parameters. The application owner’s PKH is the only contract parameter in the beacon minting policy, as shown in Figure 10.18.

Figure 10.18: Beacon Redeemer17

In Figure 10.19, we determine the value of what was minted on line 22, and the value of the minting policy hash for the script itself on line 23. On line 28, we combine both the TxId and TxIdx, and then hash them using the blake2b hash algorithm. This follows what we do in the off-chain code so that the token names being minted are the same as the on-chain code. We also check that only 1 token was minted on line 36 and that no other tokens are minted as part of this minting policy.

Figure 10.19: Beacon Minting Policy18

The Redeemer Mint code on lines 38–43 in Figure 10.19 confirms that the min transaction is signed by the application owner PKH and that the transaction ID and index correspond to a UTXO input. Requiring the owner’s signature helps ensure that the PKH provided in the contract parameters is actually owned by the entity submitting the transaction. The Redeemer Burn allows the burning of any token that belongs to this minting policy, as shown in Figure 10.20.

Figure 10.20: Beacon Burn

Ticket Minting Policy

The Ticket Minting Policy has a Mint, Convert, and Burn Redeemer. The Convert Redeemer passes a number of tickets to convert, and a list of unique ticket tokens is minted, as shown in Figure 10.21. It should be noted that careful consideration is required when passing a list of an arbitrary length. The budget costs for executing this script will depend on how many tickets are included in the userTicketTokenName array. In our case, this is required because we are explicitly minting individual unique NFT tokens. Therefore, we need to know each ticket’s name to be able to mint and validate them in the minting and validator script accordingly.

Figure 10.21: Ticket Minting Policy19

In Figure 10.22, on lines 27 and 28, a TxOutputId is created using the TX_ID and TX_IDX contract parameters. This will be used to ensure that the minting policy is unique for each event. The totalAssetVal function on line 31 is a recursive function that combines all of the NFT tokens and reference tokens into one Value.

Figure 10.22: Token List Value20

The Mint Redeemer in Figure 10.23, lines 68 and 69, confirms that the TX_ID and TX_IDX contract parameters match a UTXO that is included as an input. On lines 73 and 75, the minting policy confirms that the transaction is signed by the event owner’s stake key and the minted output is sent to the holding validator script. There is also an additional check on line 77 that will cause the transaction to fail if the mint occurs after the event showtime.

Figure 10.23: Ticket Mint Redeemer21

The Convert Redeemer calls the totalAssetVal function on line 84 in Figure 10.24 and combines the NFT ticket tokens and reference tokens into one Value. By adding the minted NFT and reference tokens with the burned released tokens, we get the total amount minted and burned in this transaction. We also do a safety check to ensure that the quantity passed in the redeemer is always greater than 0 on line 91.

Figure 10.24: Ticket Convert Redeemer22

The Burn Redeemer, in Figure 10.24, allows any amount of allocated, released, or converted tokens to be burned, but this transaction needs to be signed by the event owner’s stake key.

Holding Validator

The Holding Validator script acts as a staging area for ticket tokens that have been minted but not yet released into swaps. They can only be spent by the event owner’s stake key, which is hard-coded as a contract parameter, as shown in Figure 10.25. The implementation of the holding validator script was somewhat tricky because we wanted to ensure that there was no leakage of tokens when transferring back and forth to the swaps. The challenge is, that when the holding validator is created, the swap address is unknown. So, we run into the scenario with a circular reference where the holding script needs to know the swap address, the swap address needs to know the holding address and both should be contract parameters for each other.

To help mitigate this issue, we leverage the beacon token minting policy, which can be calculated in advance. As seen in Figure 10.18, the beaconMPH is set as a contract parameter on line 16. In Figure 10.25, we introduce a new helper function on line 19 called checkTokenOut that will return true if there are any tokens contained within a set of outputs, which will be used to check for token leakage.

Figure 10.25: Holding Validator checkTokensOut23

As seen in Figure 10.26, the Transfer Redeemer is used to transfer the ticket tokens from the holding script to a swap. The transaction must be signed by the event owners’ stake key and must also contain a beacon token. As we recall, every swap must have a beacon token, which will help to ensure that these tickets go to a swap and nowhere else. Only outputs going to the swap and optional holding validator script for change should contain tokens. This rule is enforced on lines 43–49.

Figure 10.26: Holding Validator Transfer Redeemer24

The Return Redeemer is similar to the Transfer redeemer and is used to validate that the ticket tokens from a closed swap are sent back directly to the holding script address. When a swap is closed, the beacon token is always burned, so we check for this condition in the transaction, as shown in Figure 10.27, on line 56. There is also only one output going to the holding script address, so we will make sure that this condition is true on line 62. We double-check that no other tokens are in any other outputs on line 64.

Figure 10.27: Holding Validator Return Redeemer25

The Burn Redeemer simply checks that the transaction is signed by the event owner’s stake key and that no tokens exist in the outputs. When we archive an event, we burn all remaining allocated tokens.

Swap Validator

We define the datum of the swap validator to only contain two Values, the askedAssetVal and the offeredAssetVal, as shown in Figure 10.28. The askedAssetVal contains the asset class (in this case Ada) that we are asking for. The price for an offered asset is the amount set in askedAssetVal. So, askedAssetVal contains both the type of asset being asked for and the price for each offered asset. The swaps are designed so that an event owner (seller) can update the price of a swap at any time. If the user wants to change the asset they are selling or asking for, then they need to close the swap and create a new one.

The offeredAssetVal is used to indicate the asset that is for sale and the amount that is locked in the swap. The offeredAssetVal gets updated according to the amount of offered assets that leave the swap. Figure 10.28 shows the datum struct as well as the Redeemer for the swap script. The Swap Redeemer is passed a buyer public key hash as well as the quantity and token names that will be converted (minted) in this transaction on lines 11–14.

Figure 10.28: Swap Validator Datum and Redeemer26

Figure 10.29 shows the contract parameters and global variables used in the swap validator.

Figure 10.29: Swap Validator Contract Parameters27

The checkAdaVal function, on line 38 in Figure 10.30, is used to add the minimum amount of Ada (lovelace) if a value contains a native token. For simplicity, we use a fixed minimum of 2,000,000 lovelace for all smart contract token interactions. As we recall, we can only send a native asset so long as there is a minimum amount of lovelace included. The exact amount of lovelace can be determined by calculating the bytes used in a particular UTXO. This amount can be larger or smaller depending on how many tokens are included in a UTXO. Therefore, we tightly control the outputs when sending native tokens to ensure that the correct minimum lovelace is always included.

We also create a function called totalAssetVal on line 46, in Figure 10.30, which is similar to the totalAssetVal in the ticket minting policy. For the Swap validator, however, we only need to include and validate the ticket tokens and not the reference tokens.

Figure 10.30: Swap Validator totalAssetVal28

Inside the main swap validator function, we start off by obtaining the ScriptContext and the hash of the validator in Figure 10.31. Next, we create an AssetClass for both the asked assets and the offered assets, which will be used later in the program. On line 84, we get an array of all of the outputs locked at the swap address for this transaction. Fortunately, the order of the outputs is always set explicitly during the off-chain transaction construction. The same cannot be said about inputs that may be re-ordered canonically, so we try to avoid inspecting inputs as much as possible.

Figure 10.31: Swap Validator main function29

The Close Redeemer in Figure 10.32 checks that the beacon token is burned, no outputs are sent back to the script address, and any offered assets are returned to the holding script address. It also confirms that only a transaction signed with the event owner’s stake key can close a swap position.

Figure 10.32: Swap Validator Close Redeemer30

The Swap Redeemer creates the beacon and ticket token values that will be part of the transaction outputs in Figure 10.33. Using the buyer’s PKH and the event owner’s PKH, we obtain the outputs that are sent to the buyer and event owner (seller) addresses, respectively. On lines 121—127, we pull out the required values from the input and output datums, which will be used to validate the swap’s spending conditions.

Figure 10.33: Swap Validator Swap Redeemer Datum Values31

Now, we are ready to validate the core swap logic in Figure 10.34. On line 130, we first check that the asked asset does not change between the input and output datum. On lines 133—136, we check that the asked asset in the datum matches what was specified as the contract parameter. On line 140, we also check that the offered asset in the datum matches what was specified as a contract parameter as well. Lines 143—149 are the most critical business logic checks that ensure the correct values are sent to the swap, buyer, and seller, respectively. Finally, on line 152, we check that the amount of tokens to be converted matches what is recorded in the datums.

Figure 10.34: Swap Validator Swap Redeemer Logic32

The Update Redeemer in Figure 10.35 verifies that the transaction is signed by the event owner’s stake PKH on line 170. Any changes to the asked assets or offered assets will require a new swap and are checked on lines 176, 179, and 183. Similar to the Swap Redeemer, the Datum and output values are calculated on lines 165—168. Finally, the swap value is verified to return to the swap address on line 173.

Figure 10.35: Swap Validator Swap Update Redeemer33

Back-end Application

The following sections describe the back-end components that interact with the front-end code to provide blockchain data and state information.

Redis DB

Figure 10.36 shows the Redis DB schemas to support blockchain data replication and application state management. Access to the Redis DB is only done through API calls and is not directly accessed by the front-end code. Each schema has a corresponding class defined in common/types.ts. These classes help serialize and transmit the data structure from the front-end to the back-end API code.

Figure 10.36: Redis DB Schemas

APIs

The following is a list of the APIs used to allow communication between the front-end and the back-end application components, such as the Redis DB and Blockfrost.

$ tree pages/api

pages/api

├── closeSwap.ts

├── getAssetUtxo.ts

├── getEvent.ts

├── getEvents.ts

├── getFilterSwaps.ts

├── getTicketMetadataCIP25.ts

├── getTicketMetadataCIP68.ts

├── getTicketPkh.ts

├── getWalletInfo.ts

├── printTicket.ts

├── setBeacon.ts

├── setEvent.ts

├── setStakeKey.ts

├── setSwap.ts

├── signSubmitTx.ts

├── submitTx.ts

├── updateEvent.ts

├── updateSwap.ts

├── updateTxStatus.ts

└── verifyTicket.ts

All front-end API calls are made from the common/network.ts file, which uses axios34 to make HTTP GET or POST requests. The Blockfrost webhook updateTxStatus.ts is called when there is a blockchain transaction matching the beacon minting policy. The webhook is configured to fire after three blockchain confirmations. When the Blockfrost webhook is called, it will update the event and swap that has the corresponding transaction ID and mark it confirmed.

Redis DB Setup

Before running the demo, a Redis DB instance with JSON stack must be created locally or using a cloud instance.

  1. Go to https://redis.io/
  2. Sign in or create an account.
  3. Create a $0 subscription.
  4. Create a new Redis Stack database (includes JSON stack).
  5. Select the Databases navigation link on the left-hand side of the page.
  6. Select Connect under the Endpoint column.
  7. Then select Redis Client to obtain your credential information, as shown in Figure 10.37.

Figure 10.37: Redis DB Setup

Blockfrost Setup

  1. Log into the Blockfrost dashboard and create a webhook.

  2. Enter the URL of your application as the Endpoint URL:

    https://your.host.domain.name/api/updateTxStatus

  3. Set the required number of blockchain confirmations.

  4. Enter the Beacon Minting Policy ID as a trigger condition:

    policy id = 421dacdd…e5ad68e76a6

  5. Create another webhook and use the same Endpoint URL, but use the event owner’s stake key as the sender:

    sender = stake_test1uqk6gt…6a5pj6p5qh6lc8v

  6. Copy the Auth Token and add it to the application environment variables:

    export WEBHOOK_AUTH_TOKEN=8e81d5…24e0f5

Application Owner Public/Private Key

You can use the generatePrivateKey.mjs utility in the init folder to create the application owner’s public and private keys.

$ cd chapter_10/helios/ticket-app

$ nvm use 18.17.0

$ npm install

$ export ENTROPY="put your 24 word seed phrase here"

$ node ./init/generatePriveKey.mjs

$ export OWNER_PRIVATE_KEY=383be…b632341a4e

$ export NEXT_PUBLIC_OWNER_PKH=61942b…653a7d3cdb0

Demo

We will now run the application to demo the functionality of the ticket application on the Cardano preprod network. Please ensure the following bash environment variables are set with correct values before starting the application:

$ cat ~/.bashrc

export NEXT_PUBLIC_HOST=localhost

export NEXT_PUBLIC_PORT=:3000

export NEXT_PUBLIC_PROTOCOL=http

export NEXT_PUBLIC_ENV=dev

export NEXT_PUBLIC_NETWORK=preprod

export REDIS_PASSWORD=CnitD9…9UsOAQ

export REDIS_HOST=redis-13…labs.com

export REDIS_PORT=13633

export BLOCKFROST_API_KEY="prepro…hE1aMi"

export BLOCKFROST_API="https://cardano-preprod.blockfrost.io/api/v0"

export WEBHOOK_AUTH_TOKEN=8e81d5…24e0f5

export OWNER_PRIVATE_KEY=889c4a…7cf5d5

export NEXT_PUBLIC_OWNER_PKH=18b022…9e3aeb

Start the development server:

$ source ~/.bashrc

$ npm run dev

Use a browser and go to the external port to access the application.

Initial Startup

Figure 10.38 shows the initial view of the Get Tickets page when no swaps have been created. To first create an event, the user connects their wallet and selects the My Events tab.

Figure 10.38: Default Initial Ticket View

Connect Wallet

When the user selects Connect Wallet, they are presented with a list of browser wallets that are supported and installed, as shown in Figure 10.39.

Figure 10.39: Connect Wallet

My Events

After the user has connected to their wallet, they can go to the My Events tab and will see the list of events they have created. Since we have not yet created any, we will see an empty list of events, as shown in Figure 10.40.

Figure 10.40: My Events

New Event

When a user selects the New Event button, they will see a popup window that allows them to enter the event details, as shown in Figure 10.41. When selecting the Mint Tickets button, the allocated tickets will be minted and sent to the ticket holding address. In addition, please note that there is a link to view the smart contract at the bottom of the popup window if a user wants to verify the smart contract independently.

Figure 10.41: New Event

After the Mint Tickets button is pressed, the wallet prompts the user to sign a transaction to mint the event tickets. Figure 10.42 shows the successful minting of a batch of 1000 tickets that are sent to the ticket holding script address.

Figure 10.42: Allocated Tickets

Open Swap

Next, the tickets are made available for sale by opening a swap position, as shown in Figure 10.43. A description is also added and can be modified for each swap that is opened. The user may release some or all of the tickets from the ticket holding script and put them into the swap position. In this case, we will select 700 tickets to sell now.

Figure 10.43: Sell Tickets

Get Tickets

After the user signs the open swap transaction, the tickets will appear in the Get Ticket screen, as shown in Figure 10.44. If the user is still connected to their wallet, the swap will show the ability to either update or close it. This is only available to the owner of the swap position with the correct staking key.

Figure 10.44: Released Tickets

Update Swap

Next, we will update the swap and change the price from 12 Ada to 15 Ada on the Get Tickets page. The update transaction is successful, as shown in Figure 10.45.

Figure 10.45: Update Swap

Buy Tickets

After connecting with the buyer’s wallet, we select the Buy Tickets button, as shown in Figure 10.46. Remember to enable the buyer wallet account with dApp access if this is required in your wallet.

Figure 10.46: Buy Tickets

We will enter a quantity of four tickets and then select the Buy Tickets button. After signing the buy transaction, a purchase success message will appear. The current inventory available in the swap has changed to 696 since we purchased four tickets, as shown in Figure 10.47.

Figure 10.47: Buy Tickets

In the wallet transaction history shown in Figure 10.48, we can also see that 2 Plutus scripts were executed. This allowed us to burn 4 released tickets and mint 4 ticket tokens plus 4 reference tokens. This can also be viewed on a Cardano block explorer.35

Figure 10.48: Buy Tickets Wallet Transaction

My Tickets

Once the transaction has been confirmed on the blockchain, the user can go to the My Tickets page to see the tickets in their wallet, as shown in Figure 10.49. Since the user owns the ticket tokens, they can also send them to another wallet or list them on a Cardano DEX for resale. Closer to the time of the event, the user will print their tickets to generate a unique QR code that they present at the event. The QR code is used to verify the ticket and will be marked as used when verified.

Figure 10.49: My Tickets

Print Ticket

When the user selects a Print Ticket button, they will need to sign a transaction that updates the reference token with the public key hash of the wallet. This way we know for sure that the user actually owns the ticket at the time of printing. If this transaction is successful, the user will be presented with a QR Code that they can save for later and present at the event, as shown in Figure 10.50.

Figure 10.50: Print Tickets

Verify Ticket

To simulate verifying a ticket at an event, the Print Ticket popup window has a Verify Ticket button. When selected, it will check the QR Code signature and message to confirm that they match the public key hash in the database or reference token datum, as shown in Figure 10.51. Only a user with the corresponding private key could have generated this QR Code.

Figure 10.51: Verify Ticket

Close Swap

The last part of the demo is to close a swap position. Therefore, we will switch wallets back to the event owner wallet and go to the My Events page, as shown in Figure 10.52. We can now see that 4 tickets have been converted, 696 tickets are in released status, and 300 tickets are in the holding script.

Figure 10.52: Converted Tickets

On the Get Tickets page, using the event owner’s wallet, the swaps are displayed with a Close Swap button that returns the tickets back to the holding script, as shown in Figure 10.53.

Figure 10.53: Close Swap

After the Close Swap transaction has been completed, the event owner will see that the number of tickets in the holding script is 996, as shown in Figure 10.54.

Figure 10.54: Holding Tickets

Search

The user is also able to search for open swaps using either a text-based search or filter on showtime or the number of tickets. In our demo, we now have two swaps open and will text search “Jane Town”, which is only found in one of the swaps, as shown in Figure 10.55.

Figure 10.55: Search Swaps

Once the user has selected the Apply Filter button, only the ticket swaps that match the text are displayed, as shown in Figure 10.56. We are able to filter the swaps because we have replicated the blockchain swap data and metadata into our application database.

Figure 10.56: Search Swaps

Browser Wallet

The ticket tokens can also be viewed directly in the browser wallet, as shown in Figure 10.57. As previously mentioned, the ticket tokens are Cardano NFT native tokens and can be sent to another wallet or dApp directly without requiring a third party to facilitate the transaction.

Figure 10.57: Browser Wallet Tokens

Blockchain Benefits

Currently, we have centralized, close-door event ticket providers with monopolistic control in the market that limits and stifles competition. Using blockchain, an event organizer can create their own ticket sales and redemption processes, leveraging the security and decentralization properties of blockchain. In particular, the advantages of a blockchain solution are as follows:

  • Event data immutability
  • Ticket asset uniqueness and authenticity
  • Fair and transparent ticket allocation and distribution
  • Genuine ticket resale verification
  • Permissionless (or Permissioned) ticket token resellers
  • Trustless auditing of ticket sales and attendance
  • Ticket tokens can also be used for rewards and loyalty points
  • Avoids third-party payment processors and settlement

Conclusion

In this chapter, we reviewed the implementation details of a more complex, multi-validator blockchain-based application. We explored the concepts of data replication to improve the user experience when dealing with blockchain data and events. We leveraged the technical stack of ChakraUI, Next.js, Redis DB, and Blockfrost to create a fully functional ticketing application. This included managing the complete ticketing lifecycle (mint, sell, buy, redeem). Using a QR code, we showed how the application is able to generate a digital signature to prove the ownership of a ticket for event redemptions. We also looked at some of the typical challenges with Smart Contract development such as circular references, inspecting inputs and outputs, and blockchain confirmations. We looked at how to implement a Redis DB schema to support the searching of blockchain swaps and creating APIs to support a dynamic blockchain-based React application. Finally, we finished with an end-to-end demo showcasing the features and benefits of a blockchain application. In the next chapter, we will look at testing techniques and how to write secure and scalable blockchain applications.

Points to Remember

  • Pure client-side blockchain applications are more true to the principles of blockchain and provide the best transparency and auditability.
  • The Redis DB contains the data models used by the application and is a copy of the relevant blockchain data.
  • Redis DB is an in-memory database that allows for extremely fast read operations.
  • Replicating blockchain data and metadata into an application database allows for an improved user experience and rich UI features such as multi-dimensional filters and searches.
  • Global state information in Next.js is passed into child React components using props such as the walletAPI JavaScript object.
  • UI changes can trigger real-time application and database changes while the application listens for blockchain block confirmations.
  • The CIP-68 metadata standard prefixes a hex label to the token name to distinguish between a user token and a reference token.
  • The swap datum contains an askedAssetValue and an offeredAssetValue.
  • The askedAssetValue specifies the asset being asked for and the price for each of the offered assets.
  • The offeredAssetValue specifies the asset being offered and the amount of the asset currently locked in the swap.
  • Time validity intervals are needed when trying to evaluate deadline conditions within a Smart Contract.
  • A thread token can be created using the transaction ID and index as contract parameters or as the token name.
  • Circular references occur when a smart contract depends on knowing in advance the address of another smart contract and vice versa.
  • A Blockfrost webhook calls the application when a blockchain event involving a beacon policy occurs.
  • Ticket tokens once minted and converted are unique Cardano NFT native tokens.

Multiple Choice Questions

  1. What are the advantages of a pure client-side blockchain application?
    1. Faster response times
    2. Easier to audit and trust
    3. Easier to build
    4. Does not require a browser
  2. What are the advantages of replicating blockchain data in blockchain applications?
    1. Simplified architecture
    2. Improve decentralization
    3. Better user experience
    4. Better vendor support
  3. What is the difference between CIP-25 and CIP-68?
    1. They are the same and both record transaction metadata
    2. CIP-25 uses a datum to record transaction metadata
    3. CIP-68 uses transaction metadata to record NFT metadata
    4. CIP-68 uses a reference token with a datum to record NFT metadata
  4. Why are time validity intervals needed?
    1. The Cardano blockchain can only confirm if a range of time is valid for a particular transaction
    2. The Cardano blockchain uses Oracles for time
    3. The Cardano blockchain is non-deterministic
    4. A Cardano Block is created approximately every 20 seconds
  5. When can a circular reference occur?
    1. A Plutus Script uses a reference script
    2. Cardano is deterministic, so this is not a valid scenario
    3. A Plutus script references another Plutus script and vice versa
    4. Using a pointer address

Answers

  1. b
  2. c
  3. d
  4. a
  5. c

Questions

  1. What are some of the approaches to improve the user experience while maintaining audibility and transparency?
  2. How do CIP-0067 and CIP-0068 relate to each other?
  3. How do you calculate the correct minimum lovelace for a particular UTXO?
  4. How are inputs sorted, and why is it difficult to know in advance?
  5. Are there other approaches to resolving the circular reference problem?

Key Terms

  • Blake2B: Produces cryptographic hashes optimized for 64-bit architectures.
  • Blockchain Confirmation: The acceptance of a block to the Cardano blockchain network.
  • DEX: Decentralized Exchange.
  • Functional Components: A React UI component.
  • Redis JSON full stack: Optional components to the Redis DB to store JSON objects.
  • Time Interval: The start and end time in milliseconds.
  • Trustless Model: Ability to self-audit and does not require trusting an application.
  • useEffect: Triggers and actions based on a page mount or variable change.
  • Witness Signature: Contains both the message and the signature and proves the message was signed by the given public key.

CHAPTER 11

Testing, Security, and Scaling

Introduction

This chapter will show how to test Smart Contracts and include them in a popular testing framework such as Vitest. We will look at both the traditional testing and property-based testing approaches. Next, we will focus on security and review vulnerabilities that have been identified in real-world audits of Cardano applications. We will look in detail with code examples of how to make your Smart Contract code more robust and more resilient to such attacks. Finally, we will close the chapter with a discussion around scaling and some tools and techniques to improve dApp performance.

Structure

In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:

  • Testing Frameworks
    • Test Strategy
    • Test Cases
    • Property-Based Testing
  • Secure Smart Contracts
    • Double Satisfaction
    • Invalid Datums
    • Token Leakage
    • Audits
  • Scaling
    • Avoiding UTXO Contention
    • Reference Scripts
    • Advanced Smart Contract Designs
    • Hydra
    • Mithril
    • Input Endorsers

Testing Frameworks

Testing Smart Contracts is always tricky because code is executed on distributed nodes across the Cardano network. Fortunately, most Smart Contract developer tooling includes a local emulator that allows us to catch many errors that would otherwise occur on the node. Typically this involves generating traces when the code is executed. Traces are, however, disabled by default and removed for production builds to optimize performance. That said, there may be situations where an error only occurs in production and in those cases, you may need to turn on traces to obtain additional debugging information. Traces are enabled for Helios when the optimization flag is set to false, and for Aiken by executing aiken build with the flags --trace-level or --filter-traces.

In this section, we will look at how to use Vitest,1 a popular testing framework, to test both the on-chain and off-chain code. Vitest is fast and has an interactive web page for inspecting passed and failed test cases. It is suitable for watching any changes in off-chain code and re-running the tests automatically. Vitest can also be part of an overall application testing framework that can test the entire application and not just the Smart Contracts. A disadvantage of Vitest is that it does not work well with Webpack because Vitest uses Vite, which does not use Webpack. There are other testing frameworks such as Jest2 that do support Webpack, but Jest has its own challenges with setup and configuration.

We will use the vesting example in Chapter 6, Validators, to demonstrate how to use Vitest and test Smart Contracts. Before we can start, we first need to refactor the code to pull out the off-chain code from the front-end user interface (UI) code. This has two benefits:

  • Clean separation of the UI from off-chain transaction building.
  • Creates functions that can be used by the front-end and testing framework without any modifications.

The new folder structure to support this separation of UI and off-chain code is shown in Figure 11.1.

Figure 11.1: Refactored Vesting Project

All UI code is located in the pages/index.tsx and components directory. The UI code will contain all UI elements and application state information, such as what wallet is connected, wallet balance, and more. All off-chain code is located in contracts/off-chain, and the on-chain code is located in contracts/validators directories. The contracts/config folder defines the network we connect to (for example, Mainnet, Preprod, and so on) and the protocol parameters.

Testing Strategy

The testing strategy uses both traditional testing and property-based testing.3 Traditional testing involves creating specific test cases to confirm that the Smart Contract correctly implements the requirements. This can include both positive and negative test cases. Property-based testing will attempt to exploit inputs into the Smart Contract and test conditions that are not expected.4 We will use fast-check5 for typescript that integrates well with Vitest. One consideration in doing this type of testing is that the off-chain transaction builder framework performs validity checks before UPLC evaluation. So, some of your failed tests will be the error handling of the off-chain transaction builder and not the Smart Contract. A potential solution is to have off-chain tooling to avoid any checks while still building and submitting a transaction. Making this type of change is outside of the scope of this book, but it may be considered as a future feature request to enable this option. With Aiken, you can modify the script context for traditional and property-based testing. However, the downside to this approach is that it does not integrate well into a testing framework such as Vitest and is time-consuming to set up.

The test cases for the vesting application are shown in Figure 11.2.

Figure 11.2: Vesting Test Cases

The test cases that confirm the business logic and negative test cases are located in cancel.test.ts, lock.test.ts, and unlock.test.ts. The property-based tests are located in lock.property.test.ts and unlock.property.test.ts. The vitest.config.* files specify what tests are included in each test run. To execute a test run locally, issue the following commands:

$ cd chapter_11/helios/vesting

$ npm install

$ npm run test:ui

This will start up Vitest in watcher mode and display the test execution results, as shown in Figure 11.3.

Figure 11.3: Vitest Watcher

We can then drill down into any passed or failed scenarios to see each individual test execution, as shown in Figure 11.4.

Figure 11.4: Vitest Test Details

In Figure 11.4, we selected the unlock test scenario, which executed three test cases:

  • Lock 2 Ada test case
  • Unlock 2 Ada with an incorrect redeemer
  • Unlock 2 Ada with a correct redeemer

The console view on the right shows the executed transactions and the fee, CPU, and memory costs. Locking 2 Ada to a vesting script address does not invoke the Smart Contract, so no script execution units are used. Only the unlock (or cancel) transactions invoke the Smart Contract and will show cpu and mem values. We have also included trace information inside the Smart Contract, which is shown on the console view in Figure 11.4. This allows us to easily find what in the validator script is failing.

Test Cases

The Helios and Lucid + Aiken test case source code and setup are almost identical. The Lucid provider, however, is slightly easier to switch between a real network and an emulator network. Therefore, we will show the Lucid + Aiken code in this section. In Figure 11.5, the first step is to create an emulator with two test accounts, as shown on lines 41 and 42.

Figure 11.5: unlock.test.ts6

On line 49, we specify the test scenario, which includes one or more test cases. The lucid provider created on line 54 will use the emulator instance we created on line 51. The first test case is to lock Ada on line 63 in Figure 11.6. We test for pre-conditions to ensure that the amount locked at the Smart Contract and wallet balance beforehand is correct.

Figure 11.6: Lock 2 Ada Test Case Part 17

The next step is to call the lock off-chain function on line 81 in Figure 11.7. This is the same function that is called from the front-end UI, except that the lucid provider is connected to an emulator network. Once the lock function has been executed, we check if the return status is true on line 86 and then update the execution cost counters, which will be used in the test case reports. We must also manually create an emulator block on line 89. Finally, we check that the 2 Ada has been successfully locked at the Smart Contract address on line 99.

Figure 11.7: Lock 2 Ada Test Case Part 28

The unlock (or cancel) test case is very similar to the lock test case, except that we need to pass a message to the redeemer on line 168 in Figure 11.8. We also include pre- and post-conditions for the user’s wallet to confirm that the user (or owner for cancel) received the locked 2 Ada, minus the transaction fees.

Figure 11.8: Unlock 2 Ada Test Case9

Property-Based Testing

To perform property testing, we typically need to build the transaction using off-chain code to directly feed inputs into the Smart Contract redeemer, datum, or contract parameters. The unlock test case, as shown in Figure 11.9, involves sending invalid redeemer messages to the off-chain unlock function. To use the fast-check library, we must call fc.assert on line 163, which will wrap the fc.asyncProperty. It is inside the asyncProperty that fast-check will generate pseudo-random strings that we can pass into the unlock function as the message. We expect this to fail every time, so the property test passes when every execution attempt fails.

Figure 11.9: Unlock Property Test Case10

Both Helios and Aiken also have native property-based testing frameworks. Helios has a helper class called FuzzyTest11 that allows you to create randomized data, which can be fed into your Plutus script. Aiken has excellent support for unit- and property-based testing, including a report coverage report. The following is an example of an Aiken unit test and a fuzzy test:12

use aiken/fuzz

fn answer_of_life(n: Int) -> Bool {

n == 42

}

test question_of_life() {

answer_of_life(42) == True

}

test prop_bool_distribution(predicate via fuzz.bool()) {

fuzz.label(

if predicate {

@"True"

} else {

@"False"

},

)

True

}

When the aiken check command is run, the following report will be generated:

┍━ aiken_test ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

│ PASS [mem: 1802, cpu: 680589] question_of_life

│ PASS [after 100 tests] prop_bool_distribution

│ · with coverage

│ | False  53.0%

│ | True   47.0%

┕ with --seed=4272986809 → 2 tests | 2 passed | 0 failed

Summary 101 checks, 0 errors, 0 warnings

Aiken property tests allow you to specify a predicate to evaluate, which is included in the report. In this example, we can verify that True and False values are generated and have a good distribution.

Secure Smart Contracts

The most important aspect of Smart Contract development is security. Smart contracts can be responsible for the value transfer of millions of dollars or for validating the results of an election. Therefore, special consideration is required in designing and writing Smart Contracts in the most secure way possible. This can only be done if you are aware of what not to do and examples and best practices on what to do. This section will review some of the common pitfalls that have been identified through audits on Cardano.

Double Satisfaction

One of the most frequent vulnerabilities in Cardano Smart Contracts is the double satisfaction vulnerability, as shown in Figure 11.10. In this simple NFT swap implementation, the validator script looks at the datum values in each swap to validate the business logic. The assumption is that only one NFT swap is executed at one time. However, there is nothing preventing a transaction from having two swaps included in the same transaction. When this occurs, the swap validator validates each UTXO separately and both hold true so the amount of Ada that goes to Alice is double counted. She should receive 250 Ada for both NFTs but only receives 150 Ada. This is because the higher amount of 150 Ada satisfies both the NFT swap conditions, hence the double satisfaction vulnerability.

Figure 11.10: Double Satisfaction Validator

There are several approaches we can take to address this exploit:

  1. Create a unique address for each swap script corresponding to the NFT being sold.
  2. Match inputs to outputs so there is a one-to-one correspondence between them.
  3. Verify and restrict the outputs that if Alice gets 150 Ada, Bob only gets one NFT B.
  4. Use a validity token such that if it is present, the transaction can proceed because validation has occurred elsewhere.
  5. We should also restrict other transaction types such as Minting and Withdraws unless there is specific business logic.

Figure 11.11 shows a code example written in Helios for this vulnerability. On line 15, the check to see if the amount sent to the seller is at or above the price is performed.

Figure 11.11: Double Satisfaction Code Exploit13

To fix this code and avoid the double satisfaction vulnerability, we add a few additional checks, as shown in Figure 11.12. First, we require the PKH of the buyer in the redeemer on line 8. Second, we define contract parameters to hard code the NFT minting policy hash and token name for the validator script on lines 10 and 11. We also create a utility function on line 15 to recursively go through the given outputs and return the total value. Inside the main function, we check that the NFT value is sent to the buyer’s address using the PKH provided by the redeemer. Then, we calculate the total value of all of the outputs, subtract the NFT going to the buyer, and verify the remaining value. If the remaining value contains no other tokens, we know that the transaction only had one NFT in the outputs and that it went to the buyer.

Figure 11.12: Double Satisfaction Fix14

Invalid Datums

We saw in the design patterns in Chapter 9, Ticket Application Design, that a state machine is frequently used in UTXO architecture. It involves maintaining the state in a datum and ensuring the correct datum state is updated as part of a transaction. It is always important to remember that anyone can create a UTXO and lock it at a script address with a malicious datum. Therefore, it is extremely important that a Plutus script always checks whether the inbound and outbound datums are valid. The example we will look at is a multi-sig contract for controlling when the funds can be spent by a beneficiary. It is a two-step process to exploit the vulnerability. The first step is for each required signature to sign a transaction and update the datum with their PKH, as shown in Figure 11.13.

Figure 11.13: Invalid Datum Sign Tx

Although there is a check that the PKH of the signer is included in the outbound Datun, there is no check that the other Datum fields are not modified. This leads to the exploit where Carol changes the required number of signers from 2 to 1. Now, Bob the beneficiary can release the funds and not have to wait for the second signature, as shown in Figure 11.14.

Figure 11.14: Invalid Datum Withdraw Tx

Let’s examine the code that contains the vulnerability, as shown in Figure 11.15. We define the Datum on line 3 and the Redeemer on line 9 accordingly. The two signatures allowed to sign and update the datum are hard coded as contract parameters.

Figure 11.15: Invalid Datum Exploit Datum15

The business logic is contained in the main function in Figure 11.16. In particular, line 31 checks that the datum created for the output contains the signers PKH in the signatures array.

Figure 11.16: Invalid Datum Exploit16

As we can see, the exploit allows the signer (Carol in this case) to change the required number of signatures so that Alice’s signature is no longer required. Fixing this vulnerability requires checking all datum values and not just the one that is getting updated. In Figure 11.17, we create what the output Datum is supposed to look like on line 24. We only look for an output UTXO that matches the new datum on line 30. We perform the same check that the signing PKH is included in the updated signatures array on line 32 but tighten the restriction to make sure there are no duplicate signatures on line 38. Finally, we ensure that the signatures list has increased by 1 on line 39.

Figure 11.17: Invalid Datum Fix17

Token Leakage

Another common vulnerability we will delve into is token leakage. This can seriously impact state machines or liquidity pools that rely on a unique token being minted. Smart Contracts may incorrectly validate a transaction if more than one unique token is minted. A high-profile Cardano example was with MinSwap,18 where this type of vulnerability was identified. Fortunately, no funds were lost, thanks to WingRiders19 and their quick action. An excellent write-up was provided by Tweag, who performed an audit on the code.20

There are variations of this type of vulnerability, and we will look at one particular type in Figure 11.18. It can be difficult to spot these types of vulnerabilities in multi-contract, complex applications.

Figure 11.18: Token Leakage

The code in Figure 11.19 contains an exploit allowing an attacker to mint their thread token. The Mint Redeemer correctly tests that the Value of what is being minted is the thread token and is a quantity of one. So, the vulnerability actually lies in the Burn redeemer used for burning a thread token. The Burn allows any token to be burned as long as it has the minting policy on line 29.

Figure 11.19: Thread Token Exploit21

The issue is that we are not checking the token quantity that is actually being burned; only the minted value contains a particular minting policy. To fix this issue, explicitly check the quantity that is being minted and confirm that it is negative. The code for the fix is shown in Figure 11.20, lines 29—31. It looks at the value that is being minted and then iterates through a map to check that every entry has a value of negative one.

Figure 11.20: Thread Token Fix22

Audits

It is very important to have an extra pair of eyes to review your code to help spot and identify potential vulnerabilities. This is typically the role of an auditor, and there are several auditing companies that can help with Cardano Smart Contracts.23 It is also highly recommend to explore the excellent repository by Vacuum Labs, which provides more examples of vulnerabilities from real-world audits using Aiken.24 Finally, you should also review completed audits to understand the types of common vulnerabilities known to exist fully. Although the Smart Contract code language may be different, such as Haskell or Plutarch, the concepts are the same. The following is a list of published audits for further reading:

  • Vacuum Labs25
  • Tweag26
  • Mlabs27

Scaling

There are several ways to scale smart contracts on the Cardano Blockchain, which we will review in this section. When improving scalability, one must typically consider the trade-offs between decentralization and security. An application is always constrained by the underlying blockchain architecture and that is why choosing what blockchain to use is critical. As we know, Cardano uses the UTXO architecture, which in turn uses local state and not global state. We will leverage this property to increase the scalability of our application.

Avoiding UTXO Contention

For the ticket application in Chapter 10, Ticket Application Implementation, we can easily handle many events running simultaneously without any UTXO contention or bottlenecks. However, if there was an extremely popular event, there could be UTXO contention if all of the event tickets were located in one swap UTXO. The way to mitigate this is by creating multiple swaps with batches of tickets that can be accessed in parallel. Much like having multiple ticket windows at a stadium, the off-chain code would need to be modified to route or surface swaps not currently waiting for block confirmations. By distributing event tickets across multiple swaps without any changes to the Smart Contracts, the application scaling limits would directly depend on the underlying blockchain block size and frequency.

Reference Scripts

All of the examples we have looked at so far require spending the UTXO inputs as part of a transaction. With the Vasil hard fork in September 2022, Reference Inputs28 were introduced, allowing read-only UTXOs to be included in a transaction and not have to be spent. This is particularly useful if you only need to read a datum value and not update it. It dramatically improves concurrency because multiple scripts can simultaneously include and use a reference input. The final benefit paved the way for Reference Scripts,29 which allows a Plutus script to be included in a UTXO on-chain. By using reference scripts, a transaction does not need to include the Plutus CBOR payload, which will reduce the overall transaction size. As long as the Plutus script is included as a UTXO Reference Input, the transaction will be able to evaluate and execute the Plutus script that is stored on-chain.

Using the vesting example, we will show how to submit a reference script with Helios in Figure 11.21. The reference script is put on-chain as a one-time activity inside the lock function. On line 89, we first check to see if there already exists a reference script, and if not, then build the output that contains the compiled Plutus script (compiledProgram) on line 91. We must also evaluate the cost of storing the Plutus script on-chain on line 99. The fee is included in the overall transaction costs and is calculated using the coinsPerUTxOWord from the network parameters.

Figure 11.21: Lock With Reference Script30

Once the reference script has been submitted on-chain, we create a reference input in Figure 11.22. On line 83, inside the UnLock function, we query the blockchain for the reference script UTXO. Then, on line 88, we add it to the transaction using addRefInput, which includes the reference script UTXO and a copy of the original UPLC script (uplcProgram). The included script will not be submitted to the network and is only used for local evaluation to ensure the transaction is valid before submitting it to the network.

Figure 11.22: UnLock With Reference Script31

Advanced Smart Contract Designs

When Smart Contracts were first launched on Cardano, the inefficient Plutus scripts made creating and deploying real-world decentralized applications very difficult. As a result, there were a number of clever ways to reduce the Plutus script size and computation overhead. Some of these advanced techniques are still applicable today, but they come at a cost of added complexity and reduced readability. In situations that require additional performance gains beyond applying the best practices in this chapter, please look at the excellent examples provided by Anastasia Labs32 for further study.

Hydra

There are two general approaches to scaling a layer 1 blockchain: rollups and layer 2 side chains. Rollups involve processing transactions off-chain, which can be done faster and more efficiently and then applied as one transaction at a later point in time. Layer 2 side chains involve creating a blockchain network similar to layer 1, with faster block creation and settlement times but less decentralization and security. Hydra is a layer 2 scaling solution that creates isomorphic state channels.33 This means that it has similar ledger rules and a Plutus evaluator that can run most Smart Contracts without any modifications. Hydra is a suite of protocols with the Hydra Head protocol involving the creation (and destruction) of a layer 2 network and the transferring of value back and forth to layer 1.34

Hydra’s design traces back to the original concepts of the settlement layer and the computation layer for Cardano. There will be situations that require a dedicated layer for computation at the expense of decentralization and security. For example, a Hydra Head could be created for the ticket application discussed in Chapters 9 and 10 when there is a planned high-demand ticket sale, as shown in Figure 11.23. The Hydra head would be managed by the application with a potential use case as follows:

  1. Users who want to participate in a high-profile ticket sale would lock Ada into a Hydra Smart Contract and obtain vouchers (tokens inside the Hydra Head) representing the amount of Ada they have locked.
  2. The ticket tokens would be minted in advance on the Cardano layer 1 network and locked into a Hydra Head Smart Contract, so that they are available for sale inside the Hydra Head.
  3. Users would interact with the ticket swaps running in the Hydra Head using a custodial wallet managed by the Ticket Application.
  4. Once the ticket sale is over, the tickets purchased in the Hydra Head are transferred back to the Cardano layer 1 blockchain to the new owners.
  5. Any unspent vouchers are converted to Ada on layer 1 and returned to the original owner.

Figure 11.23: Hydra Head

The disadvantage of this type of architecture is that the user needs to trust the application owner while the Hydra head is being used. Hydra is currently available for Mainnet but is still early in adoption and maturity. For more information on Hydra, please check out their documentation and discord channel.35

Mithril

Mithril is an important development for blockchain scalability, wallets, and Stake Pool Operators (SPOs). It is a solution that allows a client to obtain the current state of the blockchain without having to download and replay all of the transactions from the Genesis block.36 The core innovation behind Mithril is using stake-based threshold multisignatures37 (STM) to create a certificate by an honest majority of stakeholders that a Cardano snapshot is valid. Using this certificate, a light client or SPO can verify a snapshot with security properties that are similar to a fully synchronized node.38

Input Endorsers

Input Endorsers, as documented in Ouroboros Leios, are an ambitious and profound design extension of the Cardano Ouroboros protocol.39 In particular, it focuses on maximizing the utilization of the network by parallelizing block production and transaction selection.40 This would significantly increase block production throughput while minimizing additional resource demands on the network. Figure 11.24 illustrates the current underutilization of the Cardano network and areas for optimization.

Figure 11.24: Network Utilization41

Ouroboros Leios introduces three new block types:

  • Input Blocks
  • Endorsement Blocks
  • Ranking Blocks

At a high level, Input Blocks contain the list of transactions taken from the mempool of a block-producing node. This can be done asynchronously during the idle periods, as shown in Figure 11.24. Endorsement Blocks then generate certificates of valid Input Blocks. Ranking Blocks then use Endorsement certificates and form a chain of Endorsement Blocks. The ledger state is then based on the order of the Ranking Blocks. It should be noted that Input Endorsers do not change the consensus mechanism of determining the longest chain.42 For a more detailed and thorough description of Input Endorsers, please refer to the Ouroboros Leios research paper.43

Conclusion

We should now have a good understanding of how to test Smart Contracts and include them as part of an overall testing framework. Traditional and property-based testing were examined using tools like Vitest and fast-check. Next, we reviewed code examples from real-world audits and how to avoid common Smart Contract vulnerabilities. Finally, we discussed scaling and some tools and techniques such as avoiding UTXO contention, Reference Scripts, Hydra, and Mithril. We also reviewed layer 1 scalability improvements using Input Endorsers that will significantly increase block production while maintaining security and minimizing additional network resource demands. In the next chapter, we will review troubleshooting tools and techniques that can be used when writing Cardano Smart Contracts.

Points to Remember

  • Testing Smart Contracts is difficult because code is executed on distributed nodes across the network.
  • Application design best practices separate UI code from off-chain and back-end code.
  • A test strategy can include both traditional testing and property-based testing.
  • Security is the most important aspect of Smart Contract development.
  • A Double Satisfaction vulnerability involves spending two or more inputs while only validating (satisfying) one input condition.
  • An Invalid Datum vulnerability occurs when not all datum fields are checked and verified.
  • Token Leakage involves minting more than one unique Thread or Validity token.
  • Audits are a key part of production readiness and should be used to review Smart Contract source code.
  • Application scaling techniques include avoiding UTXO contention and making use of Reference Scripts.
  • Hydra is a layer 2 scaling solution but impacts security and decentralization properties.
  • Input Endorsers is a Cardano layer 1 scaling solution to significantly increase block production throughput.
