CHAPTER 12

Troubleshooting

Introduction

This chapter aims to walk through debugging tools and techniques for Helios and Lucid + Aiken. We will look at generating CBOR for a transaction and comparing data types against formal specifications. We will address reducing the computational overhead of a Plutus script that exceeds the execution budget. We will also examine compiler errors and how transaction builders provide robust error-handling support. Furthermore, Cardano Node errors will be discussed, along with the best strategies for understanding them if they occur. Finally, the chapter will conclude with a brief review of browser-based wallet errors.

Structure

In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:

  • Trace and Print
    • Helios
    • Aiken
  • CBOR Analysis
    • Helios
    • Lucid + Aiken
  • Execution Budget Errors
  • Compiler Errors
  • Transaction Builder Errors
  • Cardano Node Errors
  • Wallet Errors

Trace and Print

When your smart contract is not working correctly, the best way (at the time of writing) to troubleshoot is to use print and traces inside your smart contract. This allows you to write the console log and see what is getting executed in your Smart Contract. We will focus on the Helios and Lucid + Aiken Smart Contract development toolkits used in this book.

Helios

For Helios, as an example, you can print out the value being minted with a particular transaction using the print and show functions. The Helios optimize flag must be set to false to enable tracing.

func main(redeemer: Redeemer, ctx: ScriptContext) -> Bool {

tx: Tx = ctx.tx;

print("tx.minted: " + tx.minted.show());

This will generate the following output when running the Plutus script.

INFO (ticket:13:10) tx.minted: lovelace: 0

INFO (ticket:13:10) 0af9021a0…d93cba53ecf15.54687265616420546f6b656e: 2

The line number of the Helios script is also shown in the INFO brackets. The trace() function can also be used to emit an output to test a boolean condition.

value_minted.contains_policy(mph).trace("TTP3: ")

This will generate an output of the evaluation when the Plutus script is executed.

INFO (ticket:33:52) TTP3: true

If the Smart Contract fails and returns a false boolean value, a stack trace is generated in Figure 12.1, which allows you to see where and why it failed.

Figure 12.1: Helios Trace()

Aiken

When you build an Aiken script you can specify what type of tracing you want to include, as illustrated in Figure 12.2.

Figure 12.2: Aiken Build Trace Options

To emit print statements, use the trace function.

trace "redeemer message: "

trace string.from_bytearray(msg)

To emit a trace when a condition is evaluated to be false, you use the ? operator.

some_expression_a? && some_expression_b?

In this example, it can help isolate what expression is failing because if either condition evaluates to false, the entire script will fail.

An example of emitting trace information is shown in Figure 12.3.

Figure 12.3: Aiken Traces

The validator script is compiled using the -t flag.

$ aiken build -t verbose

The following output is generated when the script is executed when the validator fails, as shown in Figure 12.4.

Figure 12.4: Aiken Traces

CBOR Analysis

As we learned in previous chapters, a Plutus script is compiled into UPLC and converted into the CBOR format. We typically inspect CBOR using the hexadecimal representation of the underlying bytes. This binary format is stored and executed on-chain, so looking at this when troubleshooting is helpful.

Helios

Helios has excellent support for CBOR serialization and deserialization and you can easily view any part of the transaction in CBOR format.

await tx.finalize(networkParams, alice.address, utxos);

console.log("tx final: ", tx.toCborHex());

You can also easily deserialize a transaction or any transaction component back into a Helios object using fromCbor().

const txCbor = tx.toCborHex();

const newTx = Tx.fromCbor(txCbor);

console.log("tx from cbor: ", newTx.dump());

Lucid + Aiken

Aiken provides the cbor.diagnostic() function to help generate CBOR that can be used for troubleshooting. To print out the CBOR representation of the msg in Figure 12.3, we can use:

trace cbor.diagnostic(msg)

This will generate the CBOR representation of a bytearray:

h'596F7520526F636B21'

We can also print out the CBOR representation of the entire transaction easily using Lucid.

const signedTx = await tx.sign().complete();

console.log("signedTx: ", signedTx.toString());

The CBOR output in hexadecimal can then be used in https://cbor.nemo157.com/ to inspect and analyze the CBOR elements. Once you have the CBOR elements, you can compare them against the formal CDDL specifications1 to see if there are any discrepancies. As we can recall from Chapter 5, Transactions, the CDDL is the Concise Data Definition Language used to describe the structure of a transaction.

Execution Budget Errors

If you have not encountered execution budget errors, you will eventually.

CekError An error has occurred: User error:\\\\nThe machine terminated part way through evaluation due to overspending the budget.\\\\nThe budget when the machine terminated was:\\\\n({ cpu: -447572\\\\n| mem: 5302\\\\n}

Execution budget errors are typically detected during transaction construction, so the off-chain transaction builder framework generates a more user-friendly error. The preceding error can also occur when the off-chain framework has not supplied the correct budget values in the redeemer for each Plutus script being executed. This will result in an execution budget mismatch, which the Cardano node rejects.

There is another scenario where the order of the inputs is different between what is executed locally and what is executed on the Node. The order of the inputs will be re-ordered to follow the canonical CBOR order format.2 However, the ordering of the outputs will remain the same as when they were created during off-chain transaction construction. This is why it is preferred to inspect outputs rather than inputs. For more information on this subject, please refer to CIP-00213, which documents the canonical ordering and how it relates to hardware wallets and tokens.

In the previous chapter, we showed how to include CPU and memory units in your testing so you can keep track of a transaction’s execution budget. Remember that a Plutus script is resource-constrained to minimize the hardware requirements for the node operators. This, in turn, promotes decentralization since anyone can run a node without requiring specialized hardware or infrastructure.

When you trigger a CPU or memory execution limit error using Helios, the error message will be displayed, as shown in Figure 12.5. It indicates what was exceeded and the current values of the protocol parameters during the evaluation. This is helpful so you can isolate exactly what contributes to the cpu and mem units and tune accordingly.

Figure 12.5: Helios Execution Units Error

Protocol parameters can be changed by network updates, so it is best to query the network to get the current value. This can be done using a tool like https://cexplorer.io/params or cardano-cli, as shown in Figure 12.6.

Figure 12.6: Network Protocol Parameters

In general, the following guidelines will help you optimize your Plutus script:

  • Compile Helios scripts with optimize=true and aiken build without any traces or logging levels.
  • Avoid unnecessary data conversions and work directly with the serialized data structures.
  • Avoid iterating through the list of UTXO inputs if possible and focus on specific UTXO outputs.
  • If you encounter script size or CPU and memory execution limit errors, split out validator logic across multiple scripts using separate transactions.
  • Use reference scripts to reduce the transaction size.

Compiler Errors

Both Helios and Aiken handle errors well during script compilation. A typical error generated using Helios creates a stack trace that shows you exactly what and where the error occurred.

Here is an example of invalid syntax for assigning a variable using Helios.

tx = ctx.tx;

Figure 12.7 shows the error message, which states the type of error, the script name, and the line number.

Figure 12.7: Helios Builder Error

Here is an example of an invalid variable assignment for Aiken.

let { transaction, purpose } = context

This will generate an error and indicate the line number and reason for the error (refer to Figure 12.8).

Figure 12.8: Aiken Builder Error

Transaction Builder Errors

Both Helios and Lucid have good transaction-building error messages, and there are a lot of error conditions and different reasons why an error may occur. The most common error is insufficient funds because a transaction is not balanced. This can occur because there is insufficient Ada to cover transaction fees or if there are not enough tokens being minted to match the outputs. Luckily these errors can be debugged using a local debugger and you should be able to identify the problem. If you still have issues, search and ask questions on the Cardano stack exchange4 or the Helios5 and Lucid/Translucent6 discord channels accordingly.

Cardano Node Errors

Unfortunately, Cardano Node (Ledger) errors are the most challenging to diagnose. This is because you will see unfamiliar error messages that are poorly formatted, making them difficult to read7 and understand. Figure 12.9 provides an example of a Cardano Node error you may see when a transaction fails.

Figure 12.9: Cardano Node Error

To better understand what the error messages mean, you may need to refer to the Cardano Ledger8 source code written in Haskell. You will see the code and comments that can explain why an error occurs. The example in Figure 12.9 is fairly straightforward and is an execution budget error. There are other errors not as obvious and may require looking at the code to understand what the error means. Fortunately, most of these errors are now caught by the off-chain transaction builder, so encountering Cardano Node errors is not as common as it used to be.

Wallet Errors

The last type of error we will review is wallet error. These occur when using a browser extension wallet and are listed in Table 12.1.

Conclusion

This chapter provided an overview of debugging tools and techniques for Helios and Lucid + Aiken. We explored how to generate CBOR for a transaction and data types to compare it against formal specifications. We also looked at what to do if the Plutus script exceeds the CPU and memory execution limits and suggestions to improve script performance. We then explored how Helios and Lucid + Aiken frameworks provide error messages when we build and submit a transaction. Cardano Node errors were also examined and why we may need to look at the Cardano Ledger source code to troubleshoot if they occur. Finally, we closed the chapter by reviewing browser-based wallet errors that follow the CIP-0030 specification. In the next chapter, we will explore and review the Cardano ecosystem and the resources available to help you build an application on the Cardano blockchain.

Points to Remember

  • Debugging Smart Contracts involves emitting traces that can print variables and state information.
  • Both Helios and Aiken support traces and have the capability to trace boolean expressions.
  • Trace and print statements in Helios are removed if the optimize flag is set to true.
  • Traces in Aiken are removed by default and only present if enabled during the aiken build.
  • Converting a transaction to CBOR is helpful because you can then compare the transaction structure to the CDDL specifications for correctness.
  • CPU and memory execution budget errors are usually detected by the off-chain transaction builder framework.
  • By querying the chain using cardano-cli or blockchain explorers, you can find the current protocol parameters.
  • Unbalanced transactions are the most common error when using off-chain transaction builders.
  • Errors occurring only on the Cardano Node and not caught by the off-chain transaction builder are rare but can happen.
  • Wallet errors are well-documented and return a status code that will map back to a well-defined error message and description.

  2. When would you want to turn on traces in production?

We will also look at Midnight, a data-protection sidechain being built using the Cardano partner chain framework. Finally, we will discuss additional Cardano Smart Contract development languages and learning resources.

State of Cardano

Messari released its latest Q4, 2023 report on Cardano, and it is worth reviewing some of the key insights of that report.2

  • The Cardano Total Value Locked (TVL) of Ada in DeFi protocols increased 223.6% year over year. When converted to USD, this ranked Cardano TVL 11th compared to all other blockchains. It is important to mention that staking is not included in Cardano TVL, whereas it is on other blockchains.
  • Circulating market capitalization is at 21.1B USD, a 145.3% YoY increase
  • The Treasury balance is 1.43B Ada (or 861.4M USD)
  • The Nakamoto coefficient was at 31 which is higher (better) than other PoS blockchains. It is important to be aware that it is not always possible to determine if multiple pools are in fact operated by the same entity.3 This was one of the reasons why the Edinburgh Decentralization Index (EDI) was created.4

One of the best ways to explore the Cardano ecosystem is to use a website like Cardano Cube.5 The website breaks down the ecosystem into the following categories and has project logos and links in each category, including:

  • AI
  • Community and DAO
  • DeFi
  • Developer Tools
  • Dex - Liquidity
  • Education
  • Gaming
  • Identity and Data
  • Infrastructure
  • Launchpad
  • Lending and Borrowing
  • Marketplaces
  • Meme Coins
  • Metaverse
  • Payment and Stablecoins
  • Social
  • Tools and Explorers
  • Wallets

Year after year, the Cardano ecosystem continues to expand, grow, and mature.

Cardano GitHub Repositories

The Cardano blockchain was built by IOG (formerly IOHK), and they controlled and maintained the Cardano GitHub repositories. In Q4 of 2023, these repositories were handed over to Intersect, which oversees the Cardano Membership Based Organization (MBO). The Cardano blockchain source code is fully open-source and built with Haskell. Haskell is a very powerful functional programming language and an excellent choice for building high-assurance, complex software that can be linked back to a formal verification or specification.6 The following is a list of the core Cardano blockchain GitHub repositories7:

  • Cardano API8
  • Cardano Base9
  • Cardano Ledger10
  • Cardano Node11
  • Formal Ledger Specifications12
  • Ouroboros Consensus13
  • Ouroboros Network14
  • Plutus Core15

Cardano Improvement Process (CIP)

The Cardano Improvement Process is the mechanism to request and implement changes on the Cardano blockchain. It is important to mention that the CIP process focuses on how to get changes done on the Cardano blockchain and not the governance or prioritization of what changes should be done and when. The CIPs are located on GitHub16 and have the following states:

  • Proposed: Meets all of the CIP criteria but not yet active
  • Active: Is ready for, or in production
  • Inactive: No longer relevant or has been withdrawn
  • Draft: Is no longer used, but some older CIPs may still be in this state. It is similar to a Proposed CIP pull request that has not been merged due to missing or incomplete information.

The onus to obtain feedback and alignment from impacted teams is on the CIP author. Cardano is a large, complex system so it may not always be clear who the impacted teams are and they may change over time. That said, the steps to get a CIP from concept to implementation are as follows:

  1. The CIP author opens a pull request with the required CIP information and format.
  2. The CIP author seeks feedback and input from impacted teams.
  3. CIP editors review incoming CIPs and respond with feedback.
  4. CIP editors may invite authors and other relevant teams/individuals for reviews to facilitate discussions, feedback, and alignment.
  5. Once the CIP pull request contains all required information, including sufficient detail, feedback, impact analysis, and a path to active, it will be merged with the main CIP branch.
  6. When the CIP status changes to Active, it will be implemented following the CIP’s documented implementation plan.

CIP Editors are responsible for safeguarding the CIP process and will form groups to help enforce the standards outlined in CIP-0001.17 CIP Reviewers may be subject matter experts who are engaged to provide feedback and review a particular CIP. CIP Editors are not expected to be experts in all areas of Cardano, and it is the responsibility of the CIP author to ensure the satisfactory involvement of the required reviewer(s) of a CIP. CIP Editors can be nominated by existing CIP editors or community members if they have shown active contributions to the CIP process regularly.18

There is a lot more detailed information on CIPs and the CIP process that the reader must review19 thoroughly before proceeding with a CIP pull request. A Cardano Problem Statement (CPS)20 has also been introduced to help capture and document a particular problem, which can be used to help create a CIP. A CPS is not required but is advisable when requesting a change via the CIP process.

Member-Based Organization (Intersect)

Intersect was launched in July 2023 and is the Member-Based Organization (MBO) of Cardano. Using community governance, Intersect will prioritize and allocate Cardano treasury funding for future maintenance and development of the Cardano Blockchain. To achieve this goal, Intersect focuses on five key areas21:

  • Support the community and growth of the Cardano ecosystem.
  • Administer and advocate on-chain governance as documented by CIP-1694.22
  • Oversee the delivery of the Cardano Technical Roadmap.
  • Facilitate the continuity of Cardano system stability.
  • Coordinate open-source development of Cardano core technologies.

Intersect is a non-profit corporation based in Wyoming, United States. Wyoming has favorable legislation23 regarding the legal structure of a decentralized autonomous organization (DAO), and that is why Intersect was incorporated in that state.24 There is a board of directors for Intersect, and they meet monthly with meeting notes published on the Intersect website.25 Any Ada holder can join Intersect26 with community announcements, committee working groups, and meeting information shared on Discord once you become a member.

CIP-1694

Cardano is now in the Voltaire era of the Cardano roadmap which will use on-chain governance to allow the Cardano network to become a self-sustaining system.27 CIP-1694 documents how an on-chain governance system should work and what would be required for an initial bootstrapping phase known as Minimal Viable Governance (MVG). The most important aspect of CIP-1694 is the confirmation that one Ada equals one vote when on-chain voting occurs.

There are three governing bodies documented in CIP-1694 and each play a key role in the governance process.28

  • Delegated Representative (DRep)
  • Stake Pool Operator (SPO)
  • Constitutional Committee (CC)

These governing bodies will vote to approve or reject an on-chain proposal depending on the type of proposal being voted on. On-chain governance tooling is currently (as of writing) being tested on the Sanchonet Testnet.29 Tooling is needed to allow DReps and SPOs to participate in on-chain governance easily and conveniently.

Delegated Representatives (DReps)

For an Ada holder to vote on an on-chain proposal, they would delegate their voting power like a user delegates their staked Ada to a Stake Pool Operator. Any Ada holder can submit an on-chain proposal, but typically DReps with their delegated Ada voting power will cast votes. DReps will then vote on proposals and the outcome will indicate whether the vote has been approved or not by the DRep governing body.

Stake Pool Operators (SPOs)

SPOs play a critical role in maintaining the network’s security by operating the nodes that process and distribute blocks in the Cardano blockchain. As a result, they will have a vote when there are on-chain proposals that involve a hard fork which in turn requires upgrading a network node.30 They also participate in votes regarding changes to the constitutional committee. Similar to DReps, the collective SPO voting power is used to determine if a vote has been approved or not by the SPO governing body.

Constitutional Committee (CC)

The CC is a group of members elected to the committee for a fixed period. The role of the CC is to vote only on the constitutionality of a particular proposal.31 For example, if the constitution states that a treasury must always exist with 0.125% of all Ada in circulation, then the CC would be opposed to a vote withdrawing Ada from the treasury that violates this threshold. The CC can be dissolved at any time with a vote of no confidence, on which only DReps and SPOs can vote. The CC quorum parameters will be used to determine if a vote has been approved or not by the CC governing body.

There are seven governance actions documented in CIP-1694 and shown in Table 13.1:

Governance Action

Purpose

Who Can Vote

Motion of no-confidence

Used to dissolve the CC

DRep, SPO

New CC or CC threshold changes

Elect members to the CC or change the CC quorum requirements or term limits

DRep, SPO

Update to the Constitution

Make changes to the constitution

DRep, CC

Hard-fork initiation

Approval for a hard fork

DRep, SPO, CC

Protocol parameter changes

Approval for protocol parameter changes

DRep, CC

Treasury withdrawal

Approval to make a withdrawal from the treasury

DRep, CC

Info

Creates on-chain info only record

DRep, CC

Table 13.1: Governance Actions32

Stake Pool Operators

A key aspect of the success of Cardano’s Proof-Of-Stake protocol is the role of the staking and the Stake Pool Operator (SPO). A lot of analysis and design went into the mechanics of how an Ada holder can easily and securely delegate their Ada to a stake pool operator while still retaining self-custody. As a result, there is now a large community of Stake Pool Operators that you can stake your Ada with. Most Cardano wallets will provide you with a list of Stakepool operators ranked by Return Of Stake (ROS) that you can choose. The following is a list of online tools that allow searching and filtering of Cardano SPOs:

  • PoolTool33
  • ADASTAT34
  • CExplorer35
  • AdaPool36
  • CardanoScan37
  • Pool.pm38

Setting up a Stake Pool is outside of the scope of this book, but there are a lot of great resources online and communities that can help you get started:

  • Operate A Stake Pool39
  • Guild Operators40
  • CoinCashew41
  • IOG Academy42

Project Catalyst

One of the most interesting aspects of the Cardano ecosystem is Project Catalyst. Project Catalyst started as an experiment to give grants to projects based on voting by Ada holders. There have been 11 Funding rounds, with Fund 1 announced in September 202043. The amount of funding has increased between Fund 1 and Fund 9 and is now currently set at 50 Million Ada per funding round. Using the IdeaScale platform44 Project Catalyst claims to be one of the largest decentralized innovation funds in the world.45

A funding round typically lasts approximately 4 months, allowing the community to participate in various ways and receive compensation in Ada. The community can participate as reviewers, project submitters, voters, or milestone verifiers. The format has changed over time and will continue to evolve based on community feedback and input. Anyone can submit a proposal and form a team; this is one of the main drivers of attracting and retaining talent in the ecosystem. Please refer the following for more information on project catalyst:

  • Cardano IdeaScale46
  • Project Catalyst Website47
  • IOG (IOHK) YouTube Channel48
  • Project Catalyst Announcements Telegram Channel49

A typical Project Catalyst timeline is shown in Figure 13.1:

Figure 13.1: Fund 11 Timeline

Atala Prism (HyperLedger Identus)

Digital Identity and Self-Sovereign Identity (SSI) have the potential to become the next killer app in the blockchain space. Using a secure and decentralized blockchain, an identity solution can allow users to control and own their identity. This is a significant shift from today where a handful of tech companies own your digital identity and where you have no control over how it is used. Furthermore, social media platforms are littered with bots, scammers, and impersonators because SSI systems are not used. Social media companies are not financially incentivized to fix this issue because their revenue is based on users on the platform, whether real people or not.

The challenge and the opportunity is that people will start demanding assurances that the person or entity they are interacting with is genuine and accurate. Second, users will want to control who has access to their identity. The Atala Prism SSI framework which follows the W3C-DID standard50, allows you to build secure, private, and verifiable interactions.51 Using SSI, you can solve both problems, verify who you are dealing with, and control who has access to your identity. Atala Prism has recently been open-sourced and renamed to HyperLedger Identus.52

Midnight

Midnight is a data-protection blockchain using zero-knowledge proofs to transact with a public permissionless blockchain while keeping sensitive data private.53 This has several advantages and can be used in use cases such as identity verification, tokenized asset privacy, AI modules, credit scoring, and voting.54 Midnight is being built using Parity Technologies’ Substrate stack which lays the foundation for additional partner chains in the future.55 The concept of partner chains will help realize the goal of separation of the Cardano Settlement Layer (CSL) and the Cardano Computation Layer (CCL).56 Cardano also has other side chains57 such as Milkomeda C158, Wanchain59, and World Mobile60.

Additional Cardano Smart Contract Languages

Although this book has focused on Helios and Lucid + Aiken for Cardano Smart Contract development, other options are available to developers, including:

  • PlutusTx is a Haskell Module and was the first way to write Cardano Smart Contracts. Several resources, including the Plutus Developer Portal61 and the Plutus Pioneer Program62, are available for writing smart contracts using PlutusTx in Haskell.
  • PyCardano + OpShin63 allows you to write Cardano Smart Contracts using Python for both the off-chain and on-chain code respectively.
  • Plu-ts64 also allows you to write both the on-chain and off-chain code for Cardano Smart Contracts but in typescript.

Learning Resources

There are many excellent resources available to help you learn about Cardano.

  • Cardano Academy65, created by the Cardano Foundation is an excellent all-around resource on blockchain and Cardano.
  • IOG Academy66, created by IOG (IOHK), contains a collection of videos on Cardano, Haskell, and the Plutus Pioneers Program.
  • Emurgo Academy67, created by Emurgo is a paid program with some of the best developer resources in the ecosystem.
  • Gimbalabs68, a pioneer in the open education space in Cardano, focuses on project-based learning and online coding sessions for learning and collaboration.
  • Learn Cardano69 always has great topics and speakers on its show, making it a go-to resource for keeping up to date on the Cardano ecosystem.
  • Cardano With Paul70 has been creating great Cardano content for years. It continues to showcase exciting new projects and keeps up-to-date with the latest developments in Cardano.
  • And many more…

Conclusion

We started the chapter by reviewing the state of Cardano and looking at the published Messari Q4, 2023 report. Next, we reviewed the public GitHub repositories transferred to Intersect, which contained the Cardano blockchain source code. The CIP process and how changes are made to the Cardano blockchain using a CIP pull request, with the required information, were examined in the correct format. Intersect, the Cardano Members Based Organization (MBO), and the five areas it focuses on were also discussed. Next, we discussed the Voltaire era and how CIP-1694 captures the mechanics of on-chain voting for Cardano governance. We reviewed the list of governance actions such as protocol parameter changes, hard forks, and treasury withdrawals. We also looked at SPO return on stake (ROS) rankings and the role of Project Catalyst in the Cardano ecosystem. We also introduced Atala Prism, a Self-Sovereign Identity (SSI) solution built on Cardano, and the benefits and opportunities it can provide. Finally, we discussed Midnight, the first type of partner chain on Cardano, as well as additional Smart Contract languages and learning resources. The next and final chapter will discuss the ethos and social impact of Cardano and how blockchain is following the five stages of technology adoption.

Points to Remember

  • Cardano Total Value Locked (TVL) does not include the 22.68B Ada staked in the Cardano network.
  • Nakamoto coefficients can be misleading because the entities controlling a stake pool are not always known.
  • The public Cardano GitHub repositories are now managed by Intersect.
  • The CIP process is about making changes on the Cardano network, not governing or prioritizing such requests.
  • The CIP author is responsible for securing feedback and alignment on a proposed CIP.
  • Intersect, a non-profit DAO is the Cardano Members Based Organization.
  • CIP-1694 documents how Cardano on-chain voting should work and the initial bootstrapping phase known as Minimal Viable Governance (MVG).
  • DRep, SPO, and CC are the three governing bodies of on-chain voting.
  • Different governing bodies will be required to vote depending on the governance action.
  • DReps use delegated voting power from Ada holders to cast a vote, which is aggregated into a yes or no DRep vote for a governance action.
  • The CC can be dissolved when a governance action of no confidence is passed.
  • The CC members, quorum requirements, and term limits are voted on by DReps and SPOs.
  • Project Catalyst, one of the largest decentralized innovation funds in the world, allows Ada holders to vote on projects that will receive grant funding from the treasury.
  • There have been 11 Project Catalyst funds, with the last two funds having 50M in Ada available for grants.
  • Atala Prism is a Self-Sovereign Identity (SSI) solution built on the Cardano blockchain.
  • SSI allows users to control their identity and who has access to it.
  • Midnight is a data protection side chain built using zero-knowledge-proof technology.
  • Midnight is an example of a Cardano partner chain that separates the Cardano Settlement Layer from the Cardano Computation Layer.

Multiple Choice Questions

  1. Who manages the Cardano open-source code repositories?
    1. Intersect
    2. IOHK
    3. Cardano Foundation
    4. All of the above
  2. What example would not be well suited for a CIP?
    1. Suggest a change to the ledger to accommodate a feature request
    2. Suggest a change to the protocol parameters to improve performance
    3. Request a treasury funding withdrawal
    4. Document on-chain governance processes
  3. What is the goal of CIP-1694?
    1. Create the Cardano constitution
    2. Finalize Cardano governance and end the Voltaire era
    3. Elect members to the constitutional committee
    4. Document the on-chain governance processes and establish MVG
  4. What is false about DReps?
    1. Ada holders will be able to delegate their Ada voting power to a DRep
    2. Ada holders will not be able to change their Ada delegation to a DRep
    3. Ada holders can change or withdraw their Ada delegation by submitting a transaction
    4. Ada holders will be able to see what governance actions their DRep has voted on
  5. What statement is true about Project Catalyst?
    1. Project catalyst controls the funds in the Cardano Treasury
    2. Most of the Cardano treasury is spent by Project Catalyst
    3. 20% of transaction fees go to Project Catalyst
    4. The Project Catalyst operations team is funded by Project Catalyst

Answers

  1. a
  2. c
  3. d
  4. b
  5. d

Questions

CHAPTER 14

Closing Remarks

Introduction

This chapter will review the ethos of Cardano and how it relates to the peer review process and formal methods. We will examine the software industry standards and how these standards fall short for high-assurance software. We will see how semi-formal methods were used to build Cardano and how cryptographic associations like IACR facilitate the peer review process. Next, we will look at the potential for social good using Cardano with real-world examples. We will also discuss regulatory and compliance risks and what to be aware of. Then, we will review the five stages of technology adoption and how they apply to blockchain. Finally, we will discuss the Cardano journey and the road to get here.

Structure

In this chapter, we will discuss the following topics:

  • Cardano Ethos
  • Software Industry Standards
    • Blockchain Risks
    • Semi-Formal Methods
    • IACR
  • Potential For Social Good
  • Regulatory and Compliance Risks
  • Technology Adoption
  • The Cardano Journey

Cardano Ethos

Openness and transparency are the ethos of Cardano1 and are one of the reasons why there is such an emphasis on peer review and formal methods. The peer review process for organizations such as The International Association for Cryptographic Research (IACR)2 uses a double-blind process that makes both the author and the reviewer anonymous to each other.3 This helps eliminate biases and promotes fairness and transparency during the review process. In addition to the peer review process, IOG (IOHK) applies formal methods that rigorously use mathematical techniques to ensure that software works as intended.4 For example, the Cardano ledger code, written in Haskell, can be linked back to the formal specification which has been peer-reviewed. This level of traceability and transparency is unparalleled in the blockchain industry and is typically only used in high-assurance software found in the space, airline, and financial industries.5

Fundamentally, blockchain technology is about giving agency and self-control to the edges. The benefits of empowering the edges (people or employees) lead to higher productivity, better engagement, and social outcomes.6 Since the underlying cryptocurrency token is free to trade, this will, however, result in speculation. This speculation is frequently abused where blockchains (and projects) masquerade as permissionless and decentralized with the primary purpose of extracting value. This has been an issue since the earliest days of Bitcoin with ICOs.7 Therefore, it is advisable to align with blockchains and projects that focus on the service of others, eventually leading to correct price discovery, value, and market fit.

Software Industry Standards

Quality, resilience, and bug-free are not typically associated with most software, particularly that has been recently released. This “Industry Standard” is a low bar and is usually addressed by regular software updates to improve the quality of the software over time. This approach does not work well for blockchains! Blockchains, by their nature, are immutable, and once deployed, a true decentralized and permissionless blockchain cannot be stopped, reset, or patched by a central authority. Furthermore, blockchains can involve millions of dollars of value that can be stolen by exploiting a bug found in the software.

Blockchain Risks

Let us first look at what can go wrong when designing and deploying a blockchain. The following is a list of issues that could occur:8

  • Flawed protocol design
  • Incorrect implementation of the design
  • Typical software mistakes
  • Amateur cryptography implementations
  • Missing performance deadlines
  • Collapse under load
  • Failure to scale
  • DoS failures
  • Vulnerability to economic attacks
  • Social or voting collapse
  • Macroeconomic collapse
  • and more…

Semi-Formal Methods

As we can see, there are many pitfalls when designing and building a permissionless and decentralized blockchain. IOG (IOHK) mitigated these issues by linking together a semi-formal specification, a Haskell specification, and the Haskell implementation code, as illustrated in Figure 14.1.

Figure 14.1: Chain Of Evidence9

A semi-formal specification for the ledger defines transition types and transition rules which make it formal, but not machine-checked.10 By creating a Haskell specification, test cases can be run that compare the outputs of the specification executable to those created by the implementation executable. If the two outputs match, we can have reasonably high assurances that the implementation code implements the semi-formal specification. IOG continues to improve the formal verification process and has recently released a paper titled, “Formal specification of the Cardano blockchain ledger, mechanized in Agda.”11 The goal is to use Agda to create formal specifications going forward, with a work-in-progress version located on GitHub.12

IACR

The International Association for Cryptographic Research (IACR) is a non-profit scientific organization that advances cryptology and related fields.13 It organizes three leading conferences that are used by academics in the field to present and review scientific papers:

  • Crypto
  • Eurocrypt
  • Asiacrypt

IACR also runs four additional specialized conferences, one symposium, and a series of cryptography training schools.14 The Journal of Cryptography is the flagship journal of the IACR and provides a forum for the publication of original work in modern information security.15

Potential for Social Good

Cardano’s mission to bank the unbanked goes back to Charles Hoskinson’s TED talk in 2014 before Cardano even existed.16 Cardano began with a vision of a world without intermediaries, where individuals have control over their own data and how they can transact.17 This has resonated with many in the Cardano community and is why there are great synergies with companies like World Mobile, which are connecting the unconnected.18 Aspiring to be the financial operating system of the world,19 Cardano has significant potential for social good.

World Mobile

There are almost 3 billion people unconnected in the world, with 40% in developing countries not having access to a mobile network.20 World Mobile is creating a sharing economy for telecommunications which is only possible because of blockchain.21 World Mobile nodes can be purchased and receive World Mobile Tokens (WMT) from network transaction fees and token staking that incentivizes the network buildout. The mobile network is a Cardano sidechain that uses Decentralized Identifiers (DIDs) for provisioning and billing.22 World Mobile has deployed networks in countries such as Tanzania and Zanzibar.23

Empowa

Homeownership in Africa is out of reach for most people because of the high cost of borrowing (45%), which in turn leads to a housing supply shortage.24 Empowa is a decentralized property development and collectibles platform that provides microfinancing to underserved communities in Africa.25 Empowa has used the Cardano blockchain to finance affordable homes in Mozambique.26

Crypto Green Research

Cardano ranks as one of the highest-scoring blockchains from an ESG perspective.27 It has very low energy consumption and electronic waste. It is best in class in transparent governance, with a good distribution of token assets and a low barrier to entry.28 As a result, Cardano scores on the following blockchain metrics.

  • Environment: A-
  • Social: A-
  • Governance: A+

Social Good Projects

Following is a list of additional examples of using Cardano for social good:

  • IOG (IOHK) issues digital identities to Ethiopian students as a pilot program with the Education Ministry in Ethiopia29.
  • Community groups such as WADA focus on blockchain education hubs in Ghana, and Cameroon and future plans for other countries in West and Central Africa.30
  • A community of stake pool operators called Climate Neutral Cardano is committed to using 100% renewable energy.31
  • UNHCR awarded the Cardano Foundation and Switzerland recognition for allowing 80% of staking rewards to go toward refugees in pressing need.32
  • Sustainable Ada maintains a repository of social impact Cardano projects.33
  • AdaFilms has a collection of short documentaries on real, impactful use cases using the Cardano blockchain.34

Regulatory and Compliance Risks

The regulatory climate in the US has been particularly difficult and challenging for all blockchain companies located there. This is why most blockchain companies or foundations have headquarters outside the US in crypto-favorable jurisdictions such as Switzerland. Regulatory clarity has improved over the last few years and will continue to do so as legitimate use cases are realized and the benefits of blockchain are advocated.

Compliance risks, particularly KYC and AML regulations, are not always compatible with a pseudonymous, decentralized, permissionless blockchain. The Cardano blockchain is a toolbox with many tools that can address various functional and non-functional requirements, including KYC and AML if required. So, always be aware of your users’ regulatory environment and tailor your solution accordingly. Also, be aware that there may be reporting requirements or outright bans on holding cryptocurrency assets in certain jurisdictions.

Technology Adoption

Blockchain appears to be following a similar trajectory to other disruptive innovations such as cell phones, the Internet, and social media, as illustrated in Figure 14.2. The innovators are first in this space, pioneering and championing the first iterations of the technology. Then comes the early adopters who see the potential and evangelize the benefits. Next, the expectation is that the early majority will arrive and that this will usher in a new era with mass adoption.

Figure 14.2: Technology Adoption Curve

Unfortunately, this does not happen and instead, the chasm occurs. The early interest and excitement fade and the technology is discarded by many as a passing fad. But the builders keep building during this phase, strong with conviction that this new technology has significant potential. At some point in the future, “killer” applications are created that realize the unique value proposition of this new technology. It fundamentally changes how society views solutions to previously unsolvable problems. At this point, the early majority arrives and mass adoption occurs at scale. By the late majority phase, the technology is no longer novel and is accepted as the new normal. Finally, there will be the laggards who will remain skeptical of the technology but will have no choice but to use it to stay connected with the rest of the world.

The Cardano Journey

The road to get here was not easy for the teams involved in building and deploying the Cardano blockchain. Delays, setbacks, resets, and pivots caused a lot of friction and frustration. This is not uncommon for new, complex, highly integrated systems. What made this and other cryptocurrency projects more challenging than most, is that it is built in public with many people having a direct financial stake in the outcome. Throughout it all, however, IOG (IOHK)35 and Charles Hoskinson36 provided regular updates on YouTube and blog posts. Constantly having to dispel the naysayers, respond to the critics, and reassure the community that progress was being made. It was truly amazing to be part of this journey, watching and participating in it in real-time, and looking forward to what comes next.

Conclusion

In this chapter, we reviewed the ethos of Cardano and why there is such an emphasis on the peer review process and formal methods. We looked closely at software industry standards and how the current quality mitigation approaches are unsuitable for blockchains. We looked at how semi-formal methods are used to link high-assurance software back to the formal specification. We also examined how cryptographic associations like IACR promote and advance cryptographic research through conferences and publications. Next, we looked at how Cardano has significant potential for social good with real-world examples. We discussed the regulatory and compliance risks when creating a blockchain-based application. We also reviewed the five stages of technology adoption and how it applies to blockchain. Finally, we discussed the road to get here as part of the Cardano journey.

Points to Remember

  • The Cardano ethos is openness and transparency.
  • The peer review process is important to Cardano because it aligns with the Cardano ethos and best practices for high-assurance software.
  • Haskell was chosen as the language to write the Cardano blockchain because of its similarity to the mathematical proofs of correctness.
  • Blockchain empowers and gives agency to the edges of the network.
  • Blockchains (and projects) focusing on the service others will typically succeed in the long run and the market will determine price, value, and product fit.
  • The current software industry standards for quality are unsuitable for high-assurance software.
  • A true decentralized and permissionless blockchain has no central authority to shut down, restart, or patch a network.
  • A blockchain software bug can result in millions of dollars lost or stolen.
  • A semi-formal method is an approach used by IOG (IOHK), in which a specification executable generates outputs that can be compared to implementation executable outputs.
  • The International Association of Cryptographic Research (IACR) is a non-profit organization that advances cryptography.
  • Cardano’s mission to bank the unbanked goes back to the earliest days of Cardano.
  • The potential for social good using Cardano is significant, and many real-world examples exist.
  • Regulatory risks need to be monitored and assessed for each jurisdiction that is applicable to an application.
  • Understanding KYC and AML regulations for a system or application’s users is important for a project’s success.
  • The five stages of technology adoption include the innovators, early adopters, early majority, late majority, and the laggards.
  • The chasm typically occurs between the early adopters and the early majority, a period when expected growth and user adoption do not occur at the expected pace.
  • The Cardano journey has been long and has faced many challenges, which are not uncommon when creating a new, complex, and highly integrated platform.

Multiple Choice Questions

  1. What is the ethos of Cardano?
    1. Creating value
    2. Banking the unbanked
    3. Openness and transparency
    4. All of the above
  2. What are the benefits of the peer review process?
    1. Facilitates the sharing and collaboration of ideas
    2. Provides unbiased feedback and review of published papers
    3. Advances the field through scientific study and research
    4. All of the above
  3. What statement is false?
    1. Software industry standards are suitable for high-assurance software
    2. Blockchain gives agency to the edges of the network
    3. IACR is a non-profit organization
    4. Cryptocurrency speculation first started with ICOs
  4. What are the five stages of technology adoption?
    1. Innovators, Early Adopters, Early Majority, Late Majority, Laggards
    2. Innovators, Early Majority, Later Majority, Late Adopters, Laggards
    3. Early Adopters, Early Innovators, Early Majority, Late Majority, Laggards
    4. Innovators, Early Adopters, Early Majority, Middle Majority, Laggards
  5. Why is it difficult to build a blockchain?
    1. A cryptocurrency token will include speculation and financial stakeholders
    2. Building is done in public where anyone can see the last open-source code
    3. It is a highly complex, secure, and integrated system
    4. All of the above

Answers

  1. c
  2. d
  3. a
  4. a
  5. d

Questions

  1. Why is the peer review process not typically done in other blockchains?
  2. What other languages could have Cardano been written in and what are the pros and cons of each?
  3. Why are 3 billion people in the world unbanked?
  4. What are the difficulties of using formal methods in software development?
  5. Can agile software development methodologies and formal methods coexist?
  6. Why does the chasm appear in the five stages of technology adoption?
